Tame class field theory over local fields
Rahul Gupta, Amalendu Krishna, Jitendra Rathore

Abstract




2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14C25; Secondary 14F42, 19E15

Abstract.

For a quasi-projective scheme
X
admitting a smooth compactification over a local
field of residue characteristic
p>0
, we construct a reciprocity map

\rho^{{\rm t}}_{X}\colon C^{{\rm t}}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
from a tame class group to the
abelian tame étale fundamental group. We describe the prime-to-
p
parts of
its kernel and cokernel.
This generalizes the higher dimensional unramified class field theory
over local fields by Jannsen-Saito and Forré. We also prove a finiteness
theorem for the geometric part of the abelian tame étale fundamental group,
generalizing the results of Grothendieck and Yoshida for the unramified
fundamental group.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

The main problem in geometric class field theory is to describe abelian extensions
of a field
K
which is finitely generated over a given base field
k
in terms of
geometric invariants of finite type regular
k
-schemes with function field
K
.
This problem is equivalent to describing the abelian étale fundamental group
of a connected regular quasi-projective
k
-scheme
X
whose function field is

K
, in terms of motivic objects (e.g., motivic cohomology) associated to
X
.
The objective of ramified class field theory is to address this problem when
X

is not necessarily complete.

When
k
is a finite field and
X
is one-dimensional,
the ramified class field theory is classically known (see
[
90
]
).
In higher dimensions, the class field theory of regular (equivalently,
smooth) projective

k
-schemes was settled by Kato-Saito
[
54
]
while the tame class field
theory was achieved by Schmidt-Spieß
[
89
]

(see also
[
61
, Thm. 8.3]
).
The class field theory of higher dimensional
regular quasi-projective schemes over finite fields
with arbitrary ramification has been recently settled by Kerz-Saito

[
60
]
and Binda-Krishna-Saito
[
9
]
. In all these cases,
the geometric invariant is an idele class group which is a group of 0-cycles on the
scheme modulo certain rational equivalence. Moreover, it turns out that the reciprocity
map describes the abelian étale fundamental group of the scheme as the
profinite completion of this idele class group.

Let us now assume that
k
is a local field, i.e., a complete discrete valuation field
with
finite residue field. In this case, the class field theory of regular quasi-projective
schemes is significantly more challenging than the previous case, even when the
schemes are smooth
1
1

1

Note that regularity is not same as smoothness over
a local field of positive characteristic.
and projective over
k
.
It was already observed by Bloch
[
10
]
and Saito
[
85
]
that for a
smooth projective curve over
k
, the reciprocity map (which was constructed in

[
10
]
for projective schemes) in general has non-trivial
kernel and cokernel.
It can be even worse in higher dimensions, as Sato
[
87
]
showed that
the reciprocity map from the idele class group to the abelian étale
fundamental group of a smooth projective surface over
k
may have a non-divisible
kernel. This problem however does not occur for curves, thanks to

[
85
, Cor. 5.2]
and
[
54
, Prop. 3]
.
The prime to the residue characteristic version of this divisibility result for
curves was earlier proven by Bloch
[
10
]

under the assumption of good reduction.

In 2003, Jannsen-Saito
[
47
]
established a class field theory for smooth
projective surfaces over a local field
k
. They proved that if
X
is such a surface,
then the reciprocity map
\rho_{X}\colon SK_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
from the idele class
group to the abelian étale fundamental group has the property that its kernel is
the direct sum of two groups, one of which is finite and the other is divisible by every
integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.
They proved an extension of their result in higher dimensions in

[
48
]
under the assumption of good (more generally, ordinary quadratic)
reduction.

In 2015, Forré
[
27
]
proved a vast generalization of
the work of Jannsen-Saito. He showed that for any smooth projective
variety
X
over
k
, the reciprocity map
\rho_{X}\colon SK_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)

has the property that its kernel is the direct sum of two groups, one of which is
finite and the other is divisible by every
integer prime to the residue characteristic of
k
. It should however be noted
that Forré’s theorem is slightly weaker than that of
Jannsen-Saito in that it does not guarantee the divisibility of
the non-finite part of
\ker(\rho_{X})
by the residue characteristic of
k
.
This question is still open in higher dimensions.
Forré also proved a finiteness result for the image of the reciprocity map.

We want to study the possibility of extending
the above results to the tame class field theory when
X
is not complete.
Let
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
denote the abelian tame étale fundamental group of
X

(see §
2.1
).
When
X
is a dense open subscheme of a smooth projective geometrically connected
curve over a local field
k
, the tame class field theory of
X
was
achieved in two papers of Hiranouchi (see
[
43
]
and

[
44
]
). For such a curve, he constructed a
reciprocity map
\rho_{X}\colon C(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)

and showed that its kernel is divisible by every
integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
. The question of divisibility by
{\rm char}(k)

was left open by him.

Yamazaki
[
111
]
introduced a tame
class group for any finite type scheme over a local field.
He showed the existence of a reciprocity map for smooth schemes over
a local field of characteristic zero. His reciprocity
map coincides with that of Hiranouchi for curves.
Uzun
[
103
]
showed that Yamazaki’s characteristic zero
reciprocity map is an isomorphism with finite
coefficients if the underlying variety has a
smooth compactification which admits a good reduction.

The objective of this paper is to study the tame class field theory of smooth
quasi-projective schemes of arbitrary dimensions
over local fields (of any characteristic)
whose smooth compactifications may have bad reductions.
For such a variety
X
, we define an idele class group
C(X)
and
the tame idele class group
C^{{\rm t}}(X)
. Both
coincide with
SK_{1}(X)
if
X
is complete
(see Definitions
4.7
and
5.2
).
We show that
C^{\rm t}(X)
also coincides with
the tame class group of Yamazaki.
Using this class group, we show that the main results of Jannsen-Saito
[
47
]
and
Forré
[
27
]
in unramified class field theory over
k
extend
verbatim to the setting of tame class field theory if
X
admits a smooth
compactification. Our main main results are described below.

1.2.
Main results

Let
k
be a local field and let
{\mathfrak{f}}
be the residue field of
k

such that
{\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})=p>0
. Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
of pure dimension

d\geq 0
.
Assume that there is a smooth projective scheme
\overline{X}
over
k

such that
X
is an open subscheme of
\overline{X}
.
One may note that this assumption is satisfied if
{{\rm char}}(k)=0
.

The first result of this paper is the following extension of the structure
theorems of Grothendieck
[
34
]
and Yoshida
[
112
, Thm. 1.1]
to the tame
étale fundamental group of smooth varieties over
k
(see Theorem
11.6
for
a general statement). Grothendieck proved the
prime-to-
p
part and Yoshida proved the
p
-part of this result
for smooth projective geometrically connected schemes over
k
.
Let
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}
denote the kernel of the canonical map

\pi_{X}\colon\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to G_{k}
,
where
G_{k}
is the abelian absolute Galois group of
k
.

Theorem 1.1
.

If
X
is geometrically connected over
k
, then

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}=F\oplus\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{r},

where
F
is a finite group and
r
is the
{\mathfrak{f}}
-rank of the special fiber of
the Néron model of
{\rm Alb}(\overline{X})
.

The next set of results establishes a class field theory for the tame abelian étale
fundamental groups of
X
as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

There exist continuous reciprocity homomorphisms
2
2

2

This does not require
the existence of smooth compactifications of
X
.

\rho_{X}\colon C(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X);

\rho^{\rm t}_{X}\colon C^{{\rm t}}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)

such that the following hold.

(1)

The maps
\rho_{X}
and
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}
are compatible with the canonical maps

C(X)\twoheadrightarrow C^{\rm t}(X)
and
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
.

(2)

{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})=F^{\prime}\oplus D
, where
|F^{\prime}|<\infty
and
D

is divisible
by every integer prime to
p
.

(3)

If
\text{\rm dim}(X)\leq 2
, then
D
is divisible by every integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.

(4)

(2) and (3) hold also for
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
.

(5)

If
\text{\rm dim}(X)\leq 1
, then
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
is divisible.

Note that part (5) resolves the problem left open by Hiranouchi
[
44
]
for
curves.

We let
E^{\rm t}(X)={\rm Ker}(C^{\rm t}(X)\to C(\overline{X}))
and
J^{\rm t}(X)={\rm Ker}(\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X}))
.

Theorem 1.3
.

The group
J^{\rm t}(X)
is finite and the reciprocity map induces a surjection

\rho^{\rm t}_{X}\colon E^{\rm t}(X)_{\rm tor}\twoheadrightarrow J^{\rm t}(X).

As an application of Theorem
1.3
, we show that
J^{\rm t}(X)

can be written as a quotient of the direct sum of the 0-th ramification subgroups of
the function fields of integral curves lying in
X
.
We also obtain a similar description of
E^{\rm t}(X)
which plays a key role in
proving Theorem
1.3
. We refer to Corollary
5.12
and
Theorem
11.8
for the precise statements of these results.

There is a canonical map
N_{X}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to k^{\times}
.
We let
C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}
denote the kernel of this map.
Let
\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0}
denote the induced reciprocity map

C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}
.
We let
{\rm Coker}_{\rm top}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
denote the topological cokernel of
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}
.
Let
r
be as in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 1.4
.

The groups
({\rm Image}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X}))_{\rm tor}
and
{\rm Image}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})
are
torsion groups of finite exponents whose prime-to-
{\rm char}(k)
parts are finite.
If
X
is geometrically connected over
k
, then we have the following.

(1)

{\rm Image}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})
is finite.

(2)

{\rm Coker}_{\rm top}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
is direct sum of
\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{r}
and a finite group.

The above theorem was proven by
Hiranouchi for geometrically connected smooth curves. But the first claim of the
theorem is new even for curves.

The following result was shown by Yamazaki
[
111
, Thm. 6.5]

under the assumption that
{\rm char}(k)=0
and

\overline{X}
is a geometrically connected rational surface over
k
.

Theorem 1.5
.

If
X
is birational to a smooth projective
k
-scheme which
admits a good reduction, then the reciprocity map induces an isomorphism

\rho_{X}\colon{C(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m

for very integer
m
prime to
{\rm char}(k)
. In particular,
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})

is divisible by every integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.
The same holds also for
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}
.

Remark 1.6
.

Suppose that
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
. In this case, the above results deal with the
tame class field theory of a smooth quasi-projective variety
X
over
k
away from

p
. However, we show in Corollary
7.7
that the
p
-part of the
tame class field theory of
X
is the same as the
p
-part of the class field
theory of
\overline{X}
. It is therefore sufficient to work only with projective varieties
in order to understand the
p
-part of the tame class field theory over

k
.

We end the description of our main results with the following which predicts a refined
version of Theorem
1.3
. We refer to §
14
for some evidence.

Conjecture 1.7
.

The kernel of the map
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}\colon E^{\rm t}(X)\twoheadrightarrow J^{\rm t}(X)
is
divisible.

1.3.
Extensions of results of Tate and Akhtar

Let
X
be as in §
1.2
.
The tame class field theory provides a tool to study
(at least, the prime-to-
{\rm char}(k)
part) of
the motivic cohomology group with compact support
H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}(d+1))

in terms of the abelian tame fundamental group of
X
.
As a byproduct of our proofs, we obtain the following result regarding the
motivic cohomology groups
H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}(d+n))
for
n\geq 2
.
Note that the study of
H^{2d}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d))
is much harder and is related
to the study of the Brauer group of
X
.

To motivate our results, we recall a result of Akhtar
[
2
, Thm. 1.1]
.
It says that if

X
is a smooth and projective variety of pure dimension
d
over a finite field, then

H^{2d+m}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}(d+m))
is zero for
m\geq 2
. The key ingredient in the proof of
this result is the well known fact that the Milnor
K
-group of a finite field is
zero in degrees more than one.
This is no longer true for local fields. Therefore, we can not expect the
vanishing of the
above motivic cohomology groups over local fields.
On the other hand, Tate
[
99
]
showed that if
k
is a local field, then

{\rm CH}^{2}(k,2)\cong K^{M}_{2}(k)
is a direct sum of a finite and a divisible group. The
following result therefore should be the optimal generalization of
Akhtar’s result to varieties over local fields.

Theorem 1.8
.

We have the following.

(1)

There is a decomposition
H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d+2))\cong F\bigoplus D
, where

F
is a finite group of order prime to
{\rm char}(k)
and
D
is divisible by
every integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.

(2)

H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d+n))
is divisible by
every integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
if
n\geq 3
.

(3)

{\rm CH}^{d+n}(X,d)
is divisible if
n\geq 3
and
X
is projective over
k
.

Since the motivic cohomology coincides with Bloch’s higher Chow groups over
perfect base fields, Theorem
1.8
actually generalizes
Tate’s theorem to higher dimensional smooth projective schemes over

p
-adic fields, as the following corollary will show.
This is the first instance of a generalization of Tate’s theorem
for local fields, as much as we are aware.

Corollary 1.9
.

If
X
is smooth projective of pure dimension
d
over a
p
-adic field, then

{\rm CH}^{d+2}(X,2)\cong F\bigoplus D
, where

F
is a finite group and
D
is a divisible group.

1.4.
Outline of proofs

We give a brief outline of the layout of this paper and ideas behind the proofs.
The idea behind the definition of the tame class group used in this paper came
from our understanding of the known constructions for projective varieties over local
fields and Wiesend’s class groups of arbitrary smooth varieties over finite fields.

To prove our main results, we use two group theoretic results of Jannsen-Saito

[
47
]
(see Lemmas
11.1
and
11.2
)
to reduce the proof of Theorem
1.2
to showing that the
reciprocity map has good properties when we work with finite coefficients prime
to
{\rm char}(k)
, where
k
is the base field which we assume to be a local field.
The key idea to prove this is to identify the tame class group and the abelian tame
fundamental group with finite coefficients with the motivic cohomology (à la
Suslin-Voevodsky) and étale cohomology with compact support (à la
Grothendieck), respectively.

In order for the above
strategy to be useful, we have to show that our identification
is compatible with the reciprocity map between the class group and fundamental
group on one hand, and the étale realization map between the motivic cohomology and
étale cohomology on the other hand. Showing this compatibility is one major part of
the heart of our proofs. This is especially delicate in positive characteristic because
we have to then deal with an imperfect base field. We achieve this by means
of purity theorems for motivic cohomology groups
(à la Cisinski-Déglise
[
15
]
) and the theory of Gysin morphisms and
fundamental classes of regular closed immersions over imperfect fields
(à la Déglise-Jin-Khan

[
21
]
and Navarro
[
77
]
). We use these results and a descent argument
to eventually reduce the problem to perfect base fields, where we use
a result of Yamazaki
[
111
]

connecting the tame class group with the motivic homology group to finish the proof
of the compatibility.

The next key step in the proof is to uniformly bound the motivic and étale
cohomology (with and without compact support) as we vary our finite coefficients
(but fix the twist).
We have to do this for singular varieties as well because we apply this to the
complement of the underlying smooth variety in a chosen smooth compactification,
and this complement is generally singular. In order to achieve this, we prove
a general finiteness result for motivic and étale cohomology over local fields,
which is of independent interest. This was proven earlier over finite fields by
Colliot-Théléne-Sansuc-Soulé
[
18
]
and Kahn
[
49
]

(who also proved some finiteness results over
p
-adic fields).
We prove the finiteness over arbitrary
local fields by reducing to the case of finite fields.
Using this finiteness theorem, we reduce the proof of Theorem
1.2
to the
known case of smooth projective varieties by means of localization sequences for
motivic and étale cohomology.

The above argument only proves parts (1) and (2) of
Theorem
1.2
. To prove the stronger results for surfaces, we use our
theorem on the finiteness of étale cohomology to reduce the problem to the
case of a projective surface which is a result of Jannsen-Saito
[
47
]
.
Another key step in our proof of the full divisibility of the kernel of the reciprocity
map for surfaces in characteristic zero
is a result of Tate
[
99
]
saying that
K^{M}_{2}(k)
is a
direct sum of a divisible and a finite group if
k
is a local field.
We prove the
p
-divisibility of the kernel of the reciprocity
map for curves in characteristic
p>0
by reducing to the case of projective curves
(which was considered by Kato-Saito
[
54
]
)
using Corollary
7.7
.
The proof of Theorem
1.1
is reduced to the earlier results of
Grothendieck and Yoshida
[
112
]
for projective schemes
using the finiteness of
J^{\rm t}(X)
. The latter result is proven again using the
finiteness theorem for étale cohomology.

To prove Theorem
1.3
, the key step is to show that
E^{\rm t}(X)
and
J^{\rm t}(X)

can be written as quotients of similar groups for smooth curves. To prove this claim
for
E^{\rm t}(X)
, we first replace
C(\overline{X})
by a (a priori different) group
which we denote by
C^{\rm nr}(X)
. It is easy to show using Proposition
2.13
that
the kernel of the map
C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm nr}(X)
has the desired property. We then
use the moving lemma for the cycle complex of Bloch to show that

C(\overline{X})
is isomorphic to
C^{\rm nr}(X)
. We prove the result for
J^{\rm t}(X)
via a
combination of the ramification theory à la Abbes-Saito
[
1
]
,
the characterization of tame
coverings by Kerz-Schmidt
[
62
]
and the finiteness of
J^{\rm t}(X)
.
Once we have the descriptions of
E^{\rm t}(X)
and
J^{\rm t}(X)
as above, the proof of
Theorem
1.3
reduces to the case of curves. We prove this special case by bounding
these groups by the subquotients of
K^{M}_{2}(K)
and
{\rm Gal}(K)
, where
K
runs through
2-dimensional local fields. We then use Kato’s reciprocity theorem for
such fields (see
[
51
]
and
[
52
]
)
plus the divisibility theorem of Tate to complete the proof.

We prove Theorem
1.5
by induction on the dimension of the underlying
smooth scheme. We use the Lefschetz arguments and Bertini theorems of
Jannsen-Saito
[
48
]
and Ghosh-Krishna
[
32
]

to reduce to the case of surfaces.
To prove the latter case, the key step is to prove a surjectivity statement for the
étale realization map for certain motivic cohomology of a smooth projective
surface over a local field. Using a spectral sequence argument, the proof of
this surjectivity is reduced to showing that in certain indices, the
specialization map between the Kato homology of the generic and special fibers of
a smooth model of the projective surface is an isomorphism, part of a set of
conjectures of Kato. But this special case of Kato’s conjectures was already solved
in
[
47
]
. This is the step where one has to use the good reduction hypothesis.

We now briefly describe the contents of various sections.
In Sections
2
and
3
, we recall all material and results
regarding the tame étale fundamental group and prove our key results regarding
this group. In Sections
4
and
5
, we
introduce the class groups and prove some functorial properties of these
groups.
In Section
6
, we establish a description of the tame class group
in terms of algebraic cycles with modulus and cohomology of relative Milnor

K
-theory sheaves. Section
7
contains the construction of the
reciprocity maps and proofs of their functorial properties.
In Section
8
, we establish the isomorphism between
the tame class group and certain motivic cohomology group.
In Section
9
, we show the equivalence between the reciprocity and
étale realization maps. In Section
10
, we prove the finiteness theorem
for motivic and étale cohomology over local fields. In Sections
11
and

12
, we complete the proofs of Theorems
1.1
,

1.2
,
1.3
and
1.4
. In Section
13
,
we prove Theorem
1.5
. In Section
14
, we provide some
computations for rational curves to give evidence of Conjecture
1.7
.

1.5.
Notations

For a Noetherian scheme
X
, we shall denote the category of
separated schemes which are of finite type over
X
by
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{X}
.
We shall let
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
denote the category of smooth schemes over
X
.
If
X
is reduced,
X_{\rm sing}
will denote the singular locus of
X
with reduced closed
subscheme structure and
X_{\rm reg}
the complementary open subscheme.

In this paper,
k
will be the base field of characteristic
p\geq 0
.
For most parts, this will be a local field.
For a field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, we let
X_{k^{\prime}}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
.
For a reduced
k
-scheme
X
, we shall let
X_{n}
denote the normalization of
X

and
k(X)
the total ring of fractions for
X
. We shall let
X_{\rm red}
denote the
largest reduced closed subscheme of
X
. We shall let
X^{(i)}
(resp.
X_{(i)}
) denote
the set of codimension (resp. dimension)
i
points on
X
.
For any
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, we let
{\mathcal{C}}(X)
be the set of closed integral curves in
X
.

For a Noetherian scheme
X
endowed with a topology
\tau\in\{{\rm zar},{\rm nis},{\text{\'{e}t}}\}
,
a closed subset
Y\subset X
, and a

\tau
-sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
, we let
H^{*}_{\tau,Y}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
denote the
\tau
-cohomology groups of

{\mathcal{F}}
with support in
Y
. If
\tau
is fixed in a subsection, we shall drop it from the
notations of the cohomology groups. We shall write
H^{*}_{\tau,Y}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
as

H^{*}_{\tau}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
whenever
Y=X_{\rm red}
.
If
X={\rm Spec\,}(A)
is affine, we may write
H^{*}_{\tau}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
as

H^{*}_{\tau}(A,{\mathcal{F}})
. We shall write
H^{q}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(A,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-1))
as
H^{q}(A)
for
any
q\geq 0
(see
[
52
, § 3.2, Defn. 1]
).

We shall let
{\mathbf{Ab}}
denote the category of abelian groups.
For
A,B\in{\mathbf{Ab}}
, the notation
A\otimes B
will indicate
tensor product of
A
and
B
over
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For an abelian group
A
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let

{}_{m}A
(resp.
A/m
) denote the kernel (resp. cokernel)
of the multiplication map
A\xrightarrow{m}A
. For a prime
p
,
we shall let
A\{p\}
(resp.
A\{p^{\prime}\}
) denote the
p
-primary (resp. prime-to-
p

primary) component of
A
.
For a set
\mathbb{M}
of prime numbers, we shall let
A_{\mathbb{M}}
denote the
inverse limit
\varprojlim_{m}A/{m}
, where the limit runs through all natural numbers

m
whose prime divisors lie in
\mathbb{M}
. We shall call
A_{\mathbb{M}}
the

{\mathbb{M}}
-completion of
A
.

For a Noetherian scheme
X
, we shall let
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)

denote the abelian étale fundamental group of
X
and consider it as a
topological group with its profinite topology. We shall write
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}(F))

interchangeably as
G_{F}
, where the latter is the abelian absolute Galois group of a
field
F
.
We shall let
I(p)\subset{\mathbb{N}}
denote the set of natural numbers not divisible by the
prime
p
. We shall let
{\mathbf{Tab}}
denote the category of topological abelian groups with
continuous homomorphisms.

2.
The tame fundamental group

In this section, we shall recall the definition of the tame étale fundamental group
of an integral normal scheme and show its relation with various other
étale fundamental groups.
We shall also recall a result of Kerz-Schmidt
[
62
]
which provides
several equivalent notions of tame étale coverings.

We begin by fixing some notations which will be in use throughout this paper.
For a Noetherian local ring
A
, we let
A^{h}
(resp.
\widehat{A}
)
denote the Henselization (resp. completion) of
A
with respect to its maximal ideal.
For a discrete valuation field
K
, we shall let

{\mathcal{O}}_{K},{\mathfrak{m}}_{K},{\mathfrak{f}}
and
v_{K}
denote the ring of integers of
K
, the maximal ideal of

{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, the residue field of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and the normalized valuation of
K
, respectively.
Recall from
[
1
]
(see also

[
84
]
and
[
39
, § 6.1]
) that
for a Henselian discrete valuation field
K
,

G_{K}
has an upper numbering
non-logarithmic decreasing filtration
\{G^{(r)}_{K}\}_{r\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{\geq-1}}
by closed subgroups
such that
G^{(-1)}_{K}=G_{K}
and
G^{(0)}_{K}
(resp.
G^{(1)}_{K}
) is the inertia group

{\rm Gal}({K^{\rm ab}}/{K^{\rm nr}})
(resp. the wild inertia group) of
G_{K}
.
We shall call
G^{(\bullet)}_{K}
the ramification filtration of
G_{K}
.

2.1.
Tame étale coverings

Recall that a finite Galois extension
L/K
of Henselian discrete valuation fields
is said to be tamely ramified if the order of the inertia subgroup of
{{\rm Gal}}(L/K)

is prime to the residue characteristic of
K
. Equivalently,
the ramification index of
L/K
is prime to the residue characteristic of
K
and the
induced extension of the residue fields is separable. A finite separable
extension
L/K
of Henselian discrete valuation fields is said to be tamely ramified if
the Galois closure of
L
inside a separable closure of
K
is tamely ramified over

K
. Since the ramification index and residue field remain invariant under completion,
we see that a finite separable
extension
L/K
of Henselian discrete valuation fields is tamely ramified if and only if
the corresponding extension of complete discrete valuation fields is tamely
ramified.

We now recall the notions of tame étale covers and tame fundamental group from

[
97
, Defn. 5.7.15]
. For a Noetherian integral normal
scheme
X
with function field
K
and a codimension
one point
x\in X
, we let
K_{x}
denote the completion of
K
with respect to
its discrete valuation given by
x
(i.e., the fraction field of
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}
).
Let
\overline{X}
be a Noetherian integral normal
scheme and let
X\subset\overline{X}
be an open subscheme.
We say that a connected finite étale cover
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is tamely ramified
along
\overline{X}\setminus X
if for each codimension one point
x\in\overline{X}
not lying in

X
and each closed point
x^{\prime}
in the normalization of
\overline{X}
in the function field

K^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}
lying over
x
,
the extension of discrete valuation fields
{K^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}}/{K_{x}}
is tamely ramified.

Suppose now that
k
is a field and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
is an integral normal scheme.
By a normal (resp. regular, if
X
is regular) compactification of
X
,
we shall mean an integral normal (resp. regular)
k
-scheme

\overline{X}
which is proper over
k
and contains
X
as an open subscheme.
By
[
76
]
, a normal compactification of
X
always exists.
We say that a connected finite étale cover
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is tamely ramified
if for every normal compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
, the map
f
is tamely ramified
along
\overline{X}\setminus X
. This definition extends to non-connected finite étale
covers by considering the function fields of irreducible components of
X^{\prime}
.

We fix a point
x\in X
together with the canonical map

\overline{x}\to\{x\}={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
, where
\overline{x}
is the
spectrum of a separable closure of
k(x)
.
Let
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\rm t}}(X,x)
be the category of pointed tamely ramified étale covers.
Let
F\colon{\mathcal{U}}_{\rm t}(X,x)\to{\mathbf{Sets}}
be the functor
which assigns to any
(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{\rm Ob}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\rm t}}(X,x))
, the finite set

f_{\overline{x}}:={\rm Hom}_{X}(\overline{x},X^{\prime})
.
Then
({\mathcal{U}}_{\rm t}(X,x),F)
is a Galois category with fiber functor
F
in the sense of

[
34
, § 4, 5, Exposé V]
. The automorphism group of this fiber functor
is called the
tame fundamental group
of
X
(with respect to the base point
x
)
and is denoted by
\pi^{{\rm t}}_{1}(X,x)
. We let
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
denote the
abelianization of
\pi^{{\rm t}}_{1}(X,x)
. One knows that
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
does not depend
on the base point
x
and is the automorphism group of the fiber functor of
the Galois category consisting of tame abelian Galois covers of
X
.
It follows from the definition that there is a canonical surjection
of topological abelian groups
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
.
We let
W^{\rm t}({X})
be the kernel of this map.

The structure map
\pi\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
induces a continuous homomorphism

\pi_{X}\colon\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to G_{k}
.
As is easy to check, this map is surjective if

X
is geometrically connected. We let
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)_{0}={\rm Ker}(\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\to G_{k})

and
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}={\rm Ker}(\pi^{\rm ab,t}(X)\to G_{k})
.
For a general (not integral) normal
k
-scheme
X
, we define
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
as the direct sum of the abelian
tame fundamental groups of all irreducible components of
X
.

2.2.
Comparison with Wiesend’s tameness

We now recall the notion of tame étale covers due to Wiesend
[
110
, § 2.3]
.
Let the field
k
and the connected normal scheme
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be as above.
Recall that a regular integral curve
C\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
has a unique
regular integral compactification
\overline{C}
over
k
. We let
\Delta(C)=\overline{C}\setminus C
endowed with the reduced induced closed subscheme structure.
One says that a finite étale cover
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is curve-tame if for every
morphism
C\to X
from a regular curve
C
over
k
, the base change

X^{\prime}\times_{X}C\to C
is tamely ramified (necessarily along
\Delta(C)
)
according to the previous definition. One checks by reduction to the case of curves that
the category of curve-tame finite étale covers form a Galois category and
we denote the corresponding abelian fundamental group by

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm ctm}}_{1}(X)
.

The tame fundamental group based on curve-tameness satisfies the
following covariant functoriality. This is not a priori clear for
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism between integral normal
k
-schemes. Then
the induced continuous homomorphism between the étale fundamental groups
fits into a commutative diagram

(2.1)

\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm ctm}}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm ctm}}_{1}(X).}

Proof.

This is immediate from the definition of curve-tame étale coverings.
∎

In general, there is no direct connection between
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
and

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm ctm}}_{1}(X)
. But we can identify them when
X
is regular.
We shall need the following key result from
[
62
, Thm. 4.4]

Theorem 2.2
.

(
Kerz-Schmidt, 2010
)

Assume that
X
is regular. Then a finite étale cover
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X

is tamely ramified if and only if it is curve-tame.
If
X
admits a regular compactification
\overline{X}
such that
\overline{X}\setminus X

is the support of a simple normal crossing divisor, then
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is
tamely ramified if and only if it is tamely ramified along

\overline{X}\setminus X
.

The following consequence of Theorem
2.2
is clear.

Corollary 2.3
.

If
X
is regular, then there is a natural isomorphism of profinite topological groups

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\cong\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm ctm}}_{1}(X)
.

In this paper, we shall use
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
as the common notation for the
two fundamental groups when
X
is regular.
As a consequence of Proposition
2.1
and Corollary
2.3
,
we obtain the following.

Corollary 2.4
.

Given a morphism of regular
k
-schemes
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
, the push-forward map

f_{*}\colon\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{\prime})\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
descends to a continuous homomorphism

f_{*}\colon\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X^{\prime})\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X).

As yet another consequence of Theorem
2.2
, we get the following simpler version of
the curve-tameness.

Corollary 2.5
.

A finite étale cover
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
of regular
k
-schemes
is tamely ramified if and only if for every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, the pull-back

X^{\prime}\times_{X}C_{n}\to C_{n}
is tamely ramified.

Proof.

We only need to prove the ‘if’ part. So suppose that
f\colon C\to X
is
any morphism from an integral regular curve over
k
. If
f
is constant, there
is nothing to prove. Otherwise,
f
must be quasi-finite. In particular,
the Zariski main theorem implies that there is a factorization

C\hookrightarrow C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}X
, where the first map is an open immersion and

f^{\prime}
is finite.
It suffices to show that the pull-back
X^{\prime}\times_{X}C^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
is tamely ramified.
We can therefore assume that
f
is a finite morphism.

Now, the finiteness of
f
implies that it has a factorization
C\xrightarrow{g}C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{h}X
, where
C^{\prime}
is the normalization of the scheme-theoretic image of

f
. By our hypothesis,
f^{\prime}\colon C^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
is a tamely ramified finite
étale cover. We let
Y^{\prime}=C^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
.
Since the support of
\overline{C^{\prime}}\setminus C^{\prime}
is a simple normal crossing divisor where

\overline{C^{\prime}}
is the unique regular compactification of
C^{\prime}
, it follows from
Theorem
2.2
that
f^{\prime}
is curve-tame. In particular, the pullback

C\times_{C^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\to C
is a tamely ramified finite étale cover.
Since
C\times_{C^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\cong C\times_{X}X^{\prime}
, we are done.
∎

Let
I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
(resp.
I^{\rm ab,\rm nr}_{X}
) be the directed system of finite abelian
extensions
L/K
which are unramified over
X

(i.e., the normalization
f\colon{X}^{\prime}\to{X}
of
X
in
L
is étale over
X
)
and its pull-back to
C_{n}
is tamely ramified (resp. unramified over

\overline{C}_{n}
) for every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
. We let
I^{\rm ab}_{X}
be the directed system of
finite abelian extensions
L/K
which are unramified over
X
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If
X
is regular, then there is a canonical isomorphism

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}}{\varprojlim}}{\rm Gal}(L/K).

Moreover, if
\overline{X}
is a regular compactification of
X
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{L\in I^{\rm ab,\rm nr}_{X}}{\varprojlim}}{\rm Gal}(L/K).

Proof.

Recall that if
L/K
is a finite Galois extension and
X_{L}\subset X

is the largest open subscheme such that
L/K
is unramified along
X_{L}
, then
normalization
X^{\prime}_{L}
of
X_{L}
in
L
is a Galois cover of
X_{L}

whose Galois group
{\rm Aut}_{X_{L}}(X^{\prime}_{L})
coincides with
{{\rm Gal}}(L/K)
.

It follows from
Corollary
2.5
that
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
is the automorphism group of the fiber
functor
associated to the Galois category consisting of finite abelian covers
X^{\prime}\to X

such that for every integral curve
C\subset X
, the pull-back

X^{\prime}\times_{X}C_{n}\to C_{n}
is tamely ramified.
If we let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{\rm t}(X)
denote this Galois category, then we get

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{X^{\prime}\in{\rm Ob}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{\rm t}(X))}{\varprojlim}}{\rm Aut}_{X}(X^{\prime}).

Since
{\rm Aut}_{X}(X^{\prime})={{\rm Gal}}({k(X^{\prime})}/{K})
for every

X^{\prime}\in{\rm Ob}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{\rm t}(X))
(see
[
39
, Lem. 7.8]
),
the first isomorphism of the lemma would follow if we show
that the correspondence
X^{\prime}\mapsto k(X^{\prime})
defines an
isomorphism between the directed systems
{\rm Ob}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{\rm t}(X))
and

I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
. But this is immediate.

To prove the second isomorphism, we use

[
62
, Prop. 4.1]

which implies
that
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})
is the automorphism
group of the fiber functor associated to the Galois category consisting of finite
abelian covers
X^{\prime}\to X
such that for every integral curve
C\subset X
,
the pull-back

X^{\prime}\times_{X}C_{n}\to C_{n}
is unramified over
\overline{C}_{n}
.
The rest of the proof is
exactly as in the tame case.
∎

2.3.
Zariski-Nagata purity and birational invariance

We shall now prove the following version of
Zariski-Nagata purity theorem and birational invariance for tame fundamental group.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
j\colon X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X
be an open immersion of regular
k
-schemes whose complement has
codimension at least two and is nowhere dense in
X
. Then the map

j_{*}\colon\pi^{\rm t}_{1}(X^{\prime},x)\to\pi^{\rm t}_{1}(X,x)
is an isomorphism.
If
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a proper birational morphism between regular quasi-projective

k
-schemes, then

f_{*}\colon\pi^{\rm t}_{1}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\to\pi^{\rm t}_{1}(X,f(x^{\prime}))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We can assume all schemes to be integral. We let
K
denote the function field of

X
. We shall ignore the base points in the proof. To prove the first part,
let
f\colon U\to X
be a curve-tame finite étale cover. Then

U\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is clearly a curve-tame finite étale cover.
The main point is show the converse. So let
f^{\prime}\colon U^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
be a
curve-tame finite étale cover. By the classical Zariski-Nagata purity theorem
(e.g., see
[
97
, Cor. 5.2.14]
),

f^{\prime}
uniquely extends to a finite étale cover
f\colon U\to X
.
We now let
\overline{X}
be any normal compactification of
X
and

x\in\overline{X}\setminus X
any codimension one point.
Let
u\in\overline{U}
be a point lying over
x
under
the map
\overline{U}\to\overline{X}
, where
\overline{U}
is the normalization of
\overline{X}
in
the ring of total quotients of
U
. Let
K^{\prime}
be the function field of the
irreducible component of
\overline{U}
containing
u
.

Since
f^{\prime}=f|_{X^{\prime}}
is curve-tame over
X^{\prime}
, it follows from Theorem
2.2
that
it is tamely ramified over
X^{\prime}
. Since
\overline{X}
is also a
normal compactification of
X^{\prime}
such that
x\notin X^{\prime}
, we get that the extension

{K^{\prime}_{u}}/{K_{x}}
is tamely ramified. It follows that
f
is tamely ramified along

\overline{X}\setminus X
. Since
\overline{X}
was an arbitrary normal compactification of

X
, we apply Theorem
2.2
one more time to conclude that
f
is curve-tame.
This proves the first part (Zariski-Nagata purity theorem for tame covers) of the
proposition.

We now let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a proper birational morphism of
regular quasi-projective integral
k
-schemes. We can then assume by
[
42
, Thm. II.7.17]
that there exists a nowhere dense
closed subscheme
Z\subset X
such that
f
is the blow-up with center
Z
.
Since
X
is regular, we can assume that every point of
Z
has codimension at least
two in
X
. We let
U=X\setminus Z
and
U^{\prime}=f^{-1}(U)
. We then get a
commutative diagram

(2.2)

\textstyle{\pi^{{\rm t}}_{1}(U^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{{\rm t}}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{{\rm t}}_{1}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{{\rm t}}_{1}(X),}

where the horizontal arrows are induced by open immersions.
Since all schemes are normal and integral, we see that all arrows in this diagram
are surjective. On the other hand, the bottom horizontal arrow is an
isomorphism by the Zariski-Nagata purity theorem we just proved above.
The left vertical arrow is clearly an isomorphism since
U^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}U
.
We conclude that the right vertical arrow is injective too.
This concludes the proof.
∎

2.4.
The Pontryagin dual of
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)

Recall from
[
39
, § 7.4]
that for a topological abelian group
G
,
the Pontryagin dual of
G
is
G^{\vee}:={\rm Hom}_{\rm cont}(G,{\mathbb{T}})
, where
{\mathbb{T}}
is the circle
group with the Euclidean topology. The Pontryagin dual
G^{\vee}
is a Hausdorff
topological abelian group with the compact-open topology.
When
G
is either profinite or discrete torsion, then

G^{\vee}\cong{\rm Hom}_{\rm cont}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
, where
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}={\mathbb{T}}_{\rm tor}
has the
discrete topology. Furthermore, the compact-open topology of
G^{\vee}
coincides with the
topology of pointwise convergence of continuous functions in the latter case.
If
G
is profinite, then
G^{\vee}
is a discrete torsion group and vice-versa.
Furthermore, the Pontryagin duality holds in the sense that the evaluation map

{\rm ev}_{G}\colon G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism of topological groups.

Let
K
be a Henselian discrete valuation field with a separable closure
\overline{K}
.
Let
K^{\rm ab}
be the maximal abelian extension of
K
inside
\overline{K}
and

K^{\rm nr}
the maximal unramified subextension of
K^{\rm ab}
.
For any integer
n\geq-1
, we let
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)=\{\chi\in H^{1}(K)|\chi(a)=0\ \forall\ a\ \in\ G^{(n)}_{K}\}
.
This defines an increasing filtration
{\rm fil}_{\bullet}H^{1}(K)
of
H^{1}(K)
, which is
exhaustive.
There is a canonical isomorphism of topological groups

G^{\vee}_{K}\cong H^{1}(K)
. Furthermore, the exactness of the Pontryagin dual functor
on the category
{\rm{\bf{Pfd}}}

of profinite and discrete torsion abelian groups (see
[
39
, Lem. 7.11]
)
implies that there is a canonical isomorphism of topological groups

(2.3)

\left(\frac{G_{K}}{G^{(n)}_{K}}\right)^{\vee}\cong{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)

for every
n\geq 0
. In particular,

{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(K)=H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{K})
is the subgroup of
unramified characters.

For any prime
\ell
, we let
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K)\{\ell\}=H^{1}(K)\{\ell\}\cap{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)=H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})\cap{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)
.
It follows from
[
39
, Thm. 6.1]
that

(2.4)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)&=&H^{1}(K)\{p^{\prime}\}\bigoplus{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K)\{p\}\\
&=&\left({\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})\right)\bigoplus\left({\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K)\{p\}\right),\end{array}

where recall that
p
denotes the characteristic
exponent of
K
,

For the rest of §
2
,
we fix a field
k
(of characteristic exponent
p
)
and an integral regular
k
-scheme
X
of dimension

d\geq 1
.
We fix a normal compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
such
that
\overline{X}\setminus X
is the support of a Weil divisor

D_{0}:=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}D_{i}
, where
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{r}\}
is
the set of irreducible components of
\overline{X}\setminus X
, all of dimension
d-1
.
We allow
D_{0}
to be empty.
Note that given any integral normal
k
-scheme
X
, we can use Nagata compactification
to always find a normal compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
satisfying the above properties
by means of repeated blow-ups and normalizations (see
[
76
]
).
We let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion. Let
K
denote the function field of

X
. For any
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, we let
\overline{C}
be the scheme theoretic closure of
C
in

\overline{X}
.

For a Weil divisor
D=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}D_{i}
, we let

{\rm fil}_{D}H^{1}(K)
be the subgroup of characters
\chi
of
G_{K}
satisfying the property
that
\chi\in H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\subset H^{1}(K)
and
the image of
\chi
under the canonical map
H^{1}(K)\to H^{1}(K_{i})
lies in

{\rm fil}_{n_{i}}H^{1}(K_{i})
, where
K_{i}
is the completion of
K
along the discrete
valuation defined by
D_{i}
. We shall write
{\rm fil}_{D}H^{1}(K)
as
{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(K)
if

n_{i}=0
for every
1\leq i\leq r
(or equivalently,
D_{0}
is empty).
Note that
K
is not a discrete valuation field and hence there is no ambiguity
in the definition of
{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(K)
. It follows from (
2.4
) that

(2.5)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm fil}_{D}H^{1}(K)&=&H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\{p^{\prime}\}\bigoplus{\rm fil}_{D}H^{1}(K)\{p\}\\
&:=&H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\{p^{\prime}\}\bigoplus\left(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})\cap{\rm fil}_{D}H^{1}(K)\right).\end{array}

A notion of étale fundamental group with modulus
\pi^{\rm div}_{1}(\overline{X},D)
was
introduced
in
[
39
, § 7]
. This is the automorphism group of the fiber functor
associated to the Galois category of finite étale covers of
X
whose ramification
along
D_{0}
is bounded by the Weil divisor
D
(using the Artin conductor).
The abelianized étale fundamental group with modulus is denoted by

\pi^{{\rm adiv}}_{1}(\overline{X},D)
. There are canonical surjections

(2.6)

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{{\rm adiv}}_{1}(\overline{X},D)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{{\rm adiv}}_{1}(\overline{X},D_{0})\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X}).

A direct consequence of the definition of
\pi^{\rm div}_{1}(\overline{X},D)
,
Theorem
2.2
and
[
39
, Thm. 7.17]
is the following.

Lemma 2.8
.

The surjection
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{{\rm adiv}}_{1}(\overline{X},D)
induces an
isomorphism

\pi^{{\rm adiv}}_{1}(\overline{X},D)^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm fil}_{D}H^{1}(K)

of topological groups via the Pontryagin duality.
If
\overline{X}
is regular and
D_{0}
is a simple normal crossing divisor, then
there is a canonical isomorphism

\pi^{{\rm adiv}}_{1}(\overline{X},D_{0})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X).

For each
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, we consider

H^{\rm t}(C_{n}):=\frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}
as a
discrete
topological abelian group and
H^{\rm t}(X):={\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}\ H^{\rm t}(C_{n})
as a topological abelian group with the product topology.

We let

(2.7)

{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)={\rm Ker}\left(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\xrightarrow{\Phi^{\rm t}_{X}}{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}\ \frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}\right)

and

(2.8)

{\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K)={\rm Ker}\left(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\xrightarrow{\Phi^{\rm nr}_{X}}{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}\ \frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}\right),

where
\Phi^{\rm t}_{X}
and
\Phi^{\rm nr}_{X}
are obtained by taking the products of
pull-backs on étale cohomology via
C_{n}\to X
.
Note that
H^{\rm t}(X)
is a Hausdorff topological abelian group.
Moreover, there are inclusions of closed subspaces
{\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K)\subseteq{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)\subseteq H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\subseteq H^{1}(K)
.

It follows from (
2.5
) that

(2.9)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)&=&\begin{array}[]{l}{\rm Ker}\left(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})\xrightarrow{\Phi^{\rm t}_{X}}{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}\ \frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})}{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})\cap{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}\right)\\
\hskip 142.26378pt\bigoplus\\
\hskip 113.81102ptH^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\{p^{\prime}\}\end{array}\\
&=&{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)\{p\}\bigoplus H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\{p^{\prime}\}.\end{array}

In particular,

(2.10)

{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)\cong H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ \mbox{if}\ {\rm char}(k)=0.

An immediate consequence of Lemma
2.8
is the following.

Lemma 2.9
.

Assume that
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
topological abelian groups
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}
. In particular,

W^{\rm t}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}\left(\frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)}\right)^{\vee}.

Remark 2.10
.

It is easy to see from Lemma
2.9

that for
X
as in (
2.7
), the group

{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)
coincides with the first tame
cohomology group
H^{1}_{t}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
considered in
[
31
, § 2]
.
The latter is part of the general theory of tame cohomology introduced in

[
45
]
.

For a finite abelian extension
L/K
, we let
H^{1}(L/K)
be the kernel of the canonical
map
H^{1}(K)\to H^{1}(L)
. Using
[
39
, Lem. 7.11]
and the fact that
abelianization is a right exact functor (because it is a left adjoint)
on the category of groups, we see that this kernel is the Pontryagin dual of the
finite group
{\rm Gal}(L/K)
. We now let
L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
and let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
.
Let
\nu\colon\overline{C}_{n}\to\overline{X}
be the canonical map.
It follows from Corollary
2.4
and Lemma
2.6
that there are
canonical maps
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(C_{n})\xrightarrow{\nu_{*}}\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Gal}(L/K)
.
Taking the Pontryagin duals and using Lemma
2.9
, we see that the
composite map
H^{1}(L/K)\hookrightarrow H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to\frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}
is zero.
In particular,
H^{1}(L/K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)
. A similar (and simpler) argument
shows that
H^{1}(L/K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K)
for every
L\in I^{\rm ab,\rm nr}_{X}
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
L/K
be a finite abelian extension. Then the following hold.

(1)

L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
if and only if
H^{1}(L/K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)
.

(2)

L\in I^{\rm ab,\rm nr}_{X}
if and only if
H^{1}(L/K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K)
.

Proof.

We only need to prove the ‘if’ parts. We shall prove this in case (1) as
the proof in the other case is almost identical.
Suppose now that
L/K
is a finite abelian extension
such that
H^{1}(L/K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)
. In particular, there is a
surjection
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow{{\rm Gal}}(L/K)
. Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
and let

\nu\colon{C}_{n}\to{X}

be the induced morphism. Our assumption implies that
the composite homomorphism

(2.11)

H^{1}(L/K)\hookrightarrow H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\xrightarrow{\nu^{*}}\frac{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}

is zero.

We let
\pi\colon{X}^{\prime}\to{X}
be the canonical morphism from the
normalization of
{X}
in
L
.
Since
{\rm Aut}_{X}(X^{\prime})={{\rm Gal}}(L/K)
, it follows by taking the dual of
(
2.11
) and using Lemma
2.9
that the
composite map

W^{\rm t}(C_{n})\xrightarrow{\nu_{*}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Aut}_{X}(X^{\prime})

is zero. Equivalently, there is a factorization

(2.12)

\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(C_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(C_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm Aut}_{X}(X^{\prime}).}

If we let
C^{\prime}_{n}=C_{n}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
, then the projection morphism
\psi\colon C^{\prime}_{n}\to C_{n}
is a finite (possibly disconnected) Galois cover with Galois group

{\rm Aut}_{C_{n}}(C^{\prime}_{n})\cong{\rm Aut}_{X}(X^{\prime})\cong{\rm Gal}(L/K)
. We let

C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{r}

be the connected components of
C^{\prime}_{n}
. Then each
C^{\prime}_{i}
is a finite étale cover of

C_{n}
and
{\rm Gal}(L/K)
permutes these components. In particular, if
F
is the
compositum of
k(C^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,k(C^{\prime}_{r})
inside a separable closure of
k(C_{n})
,
then
F/{k(C_{n})}
is the Galois closure of each of the function fields
k(C^{\prime}_{i})
.
Moreover, there is a canonical homomorphism
{\rm Aut}_{C_{n}}(C^{\prime}_{n})\to{\rm Gal}(F/{k(C_{n})})
.

We conclude from (
2.12
) that the Galois group
{\rm Gal}(F/{k(C_{n})})

has the property that the canonical surjection
\pi_{1}(C_{n})\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Gal}(F/{k(C_{n})})

admits a factorization

\pi_{1}(C_{n})\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(C_{n})\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(C_{n})\to{\rm Gal}(F/{k(C_{n})}).

This implies that the last arrow is surjective, and consequently,
the function field of each connected component of
C^{\prime}_{n}
is tamely ramified over

k(C_{n})
. Since
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
was chosen arbitrarily, it follows that

L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

The inclusions
{\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)\subset H^{1}(K)
induce
isomorphisms

{\underset{L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}}{\varinjlim}}\ H^{1}(L/K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K);\ \ \ {\underset{L\in I^{\rm ab,\rm nr}_{X}}{\varinjlim}}\ H^{1}(L/K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K).

Proof.

Using Lemma
2.11
, we only need to show that the two homomorphisms are
surjective. We shall prove this for the first map as a similar proof applies
for the second map too.

Let
F={H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}/{{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)}
so that there is an exact
sequence of discrete torsion abelian groups

(2.13)

0\to{\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K)\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to F\to 0.

Since
{\rm Hom}_{{\rm cont}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\cong{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})

for a discrete torsion group
G
, it follows from
[
39
, Lem. 7.11]

that

(2.14)

0\to F^{\vee}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\to({\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}\to 0

is an exact sequence of continuous homomorphisms of profinite abelian groups
(see
[
81
, Thm. 2.9.6]
).
Note that
F^{\vee}
is closed in
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
because
({\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}

is Hausdorff.

It follows from
[
81
, Prop. 2.1.4(d)]
that

F^{\vee}
is an intersection of open (hence closed) subgroups of
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
.
Let
J
be the set of all open subgroups of
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)

which contain
F^{\vee}
. Then
J
is a directed set whose
order is given by declaring
H_{1}\leq H_{2}
if
H_{1}\subset H_{2}
.
The above statement then says that
F^{\vee}={\underset{H\in J}{\varprojlim}}H
.
Hence, we get a short exact sequence of inverse systems of
profinite groups

(2.15)

0\to\{H\}_{H\in J}\to\{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\}_{H\in J}\to\{{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/H\}_{H\in J}\to 0,

where the middle term is a constant inverse system.
Note that each
{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/H
is a discrete profinite group and hence finite.
We conclude from
[
81
, Prop. 2.2.4]
that (
2.15
)
remains exact after taking the inverse limits.
But this is equivalent by (
2.14
) to saying that each map

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/H
factors through

({\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/H
and induces an isomorphism of
profinite groups

(2.16)

({\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{H\in J}{\varprojlim}}\ {\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/H.

For every
H\in J
, let
H^{\prime}=p^{-1}_{X}(H)
, where
p_{X}\colon{\rm Gal}(K^{\rm ab}/K)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
is the canonical surjection. Then Galois theory of
field extensions tells us that there is a unique finite abelian
subextension
L_{H}/K
such that
{\rm Gal}(L_{H}/K)={{\rm Gal}(K^{\rm ab}/K)}/{H^{\prime}}={\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/{H}
. Any such
L_{H}
must lie in
I^{\rm ab}_{X}
.
Moreover,
({\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{H\in J}{\varprojlim}}{\rm Gal}(L_{H}/K)
.
Using the Pontryagin duality and
[
81
, Lem. 2.9.3]
,
we get

(2.17)

{\underset{H\in J}{\varinjlim}}H^{1}(L_{H}/K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K).

The surjection
({\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/H\cong{\rm Gal}(L_{H}/K)
implies that the homomorphism
H^{1}(L_{H}/K)\to{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)
is
an inclusion. In particular,
L_{H}\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
by Lemma
2.11
.
Since
H^{1}(L/K)\subset{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)
for every
L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}
by
Lemma
2.11
, we conclude that
the map
{\underset{L\in I^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{X}}{\varinjlim}}H^{1}(L/K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)
is an isomorphism. This concludes the proof.
∎

The principal objective of §
2.4
was to prove the following.

Proposition 2.13
.

There is a canonical isomorphism of topological abelian groups

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm fil}^{{\rm t}}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}.

If
\overline{X}
is regular, then there is a canonical isomorphism of topological abelian
groups

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm fil}^{\rm nr}H^{1}(K))^{\vee}.

Proof.

The proposition is simply a combination of Lemmas
2.6
and
2.12

using the Pontryagin duality between profinite and discrete torsion abelian
groups (see
[
39
, § 7.4]
).
∎

3.
Relation with
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
and

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})

The goal of this section is to establish some explicit relations of the tame
fundamental group of a smooth scheme with its ordinary abelian
étale fundamental group
and the abelian étale fundamental groups of its regular compactifications
(whenever they exist).

3.1.
Topologies on direct sum and product

In this paper, we shall endow a direct sum

G={\underset{\lambda\in I}{\bigoplus}}G_{\lambda}
of topological abelian groups

\{G_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in I}
with the direct sum topology. Recall that this
is the final topology on
G
with respect to the inclusions of
finite direct sums (each of which is endowed with the product topology) into
G
.
We shall endow the product
G^{\prime}:={\underset{\lambda\in I}{\prod}}G_{\lambda}

with the product topology. It is classical that
G
and
G^{\prime}
are topological abelian
groups. The following is an easy exercise.

Lemma 3.1
.

With the direct sum topology,
G
is the coproduct of

\{G_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in I}
in the category of topological abelian groups.

Proof.

Let
A
be a topological abelian group and let
f_{\lambda}\colon G_{\lambda}\to A
be
a continuous homomorphism for each
\lambda\in I
. The lemma asserts that there
exists a unique continuous homomorphism
f\colon G\to A
such that

f|_{G_{\lambda}}=f_{\lambda}
. Now, the universal property of the algebraic direct
sum yields a homomorphism
f\colon G\to A
. To show that
f
is continuous, it
suffices to show that its restriction to each finite direct sum is continuous.
But this is obvious.
∎

Corollary 3.2
.

For any topological abelian group
A
, the canonical homomorphism of abelian groups

\theta_{I}\colon{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}({\underset{\lambda\in I}{\bigoplus}}G_{\lambda},A)\to{\underset{\lambda\in I}{\prod}}{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(G_{\lambda},A)

is an isomorphism.

Remark 3.3
.

We do not know if
\theta_{I}
is continuous with respect to
the compact-open topology on its left and product of compact-open topologies on
its right. It is however known that
\theta_{I}
is a topological isomorphism
if
A
is the circle group and
we endow the direct sum with a different topology, called the asterisk topology
(see
[
50
]
). In particular,
\theta_{I}
is a topological isomorphism if

I
is countable and
A={\mathbb{T}}
.

3.2.
Relation between three fundamental groups

We fix a field
k
of characteristic exponent
p

and an integral regular
k
-scheme
X
.
We keep the notations of §
2.4
.
For an integral regular curve
C
over
k
and integer
i\geq 0
, we let

G^{(i)}_{\Delta(C)}={\underset{x\in\Delta(C)}{\bigoplus}}G^{(i)}_{x}
,
where recall that
G_{x}
denotes the abelian absolute Galois group of
the completion
k(C)_{x}
of the function field
k(C)
at the point
x
.
Recall that a sequence of topological abelian groups
A\xrightarrow{\alpha}B\xrightarrow{\beta}C
is
topologically
exact if
C
is Hausdorff and

{\rm Ker}(\beta)
coincides with the closure
of
{\rm Image}(\alpha)
.

Proposition 3.4
.

There is a topologically exact sequence of topological abelian groups

(3.1)

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}G^{(1)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\tau_{X}}\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to 0.

Proof.

We first assume that
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. By Lemma
2.9
, we then have a short exact sequence

(3.2)

0\to\left(\frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)}\right)^{\vee}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to 0.

On the other hand, we have a commutative diagram of exact sequences

(3.3)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ker}(\alpha)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in\Delta(X)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{H^{1}(K_{x})}{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(K_{x})},}

where
\alpha
is the restriction map. The right vertical arrow is injective
by definition of
{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)
. It follows that

{\underset{x\in\Delta(X)}{\bigoplus}}\left(\frac{H^{1}(K_{x})}{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(K_{x})}\right)^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow\left(\frac{H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)}\right)^{\vee}.

We are now done because the summand corresponding any
x\in\Delta(X)

on the left hand side of this surjection is
G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}
by (
2.3
).

We now consider the general case. It follows from Corollary
2.4
and the
dimension one case shown above that we have a complex

(3.4)

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}W^{\rm t}(C_{n})\xrightarrow{\tau^{\prime}_{X}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\tau_{X}}\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to 0,

in which
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
and
\tau_{X}
are continuous and there is a surjection

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}G^{(1)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}\twoheadrightarrow{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}W^{\rm t}(C_{n})
.
Hence, it suffices to show that (
3.4
) is topologically exact.

We let
H
be the closure of the image of
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
.
We let
A
be the image of
\Phi^{\rm t}_{X}
in (
2.7
) with the quotient
topology (which is discrete) so that
[
39
, Lem. 7.11]
and
Proposition
2.13
together yield a short exact sequence of continuous
homomorphisms

(3.5)

0\to A^{\vee}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\tau_{X}}\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\to 0.

It remains to show that the inclusion
H\hookrightarrow A^{\vee}
is actually an isomorphism.

We endow
H
with the
subspace topology induced from the profinite topology of
A^{\vee}
so that

H^{\vee}\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(H,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
.
By
[
39
, Lem. 7.12]
, it suffices to show that the dual
map
A\to H^{\vee}
is injective.
Since the maps
{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}W^{\rm t}(C_{n})\to H\to A^{\vee}

are continuous, we have the dual homomorphisms (not necessarily continuous)

A\to H^{\vee}\to{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}W^{\rm t}(C_{n})^{\vee}
by Corollary
3.2
.
It suffices therefore to show that the composite map is injective.
Equivalently, we need to show that the right vertical arrow in the diagram

(3.6)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\rm t}_{X}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\tau^{\prime}_{X})^{\vee}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}\frac{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})}{{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\prod}}W^{\rm t}(C_{n})^{\vee}}

is injective.
But this follows immediately from (
2.7
) because one easily checks
(by projecting to each factor of the products on the bottom) that this diagram
is commutative.
∎

We now let
\overline{X}
be a regular compactification of
X
and let

j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion.
Let
J^{\rm t}(X)
denote the kernel of the canonical surjection

\tau_{\overline{X}}\colon\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})
.
Then
J^{\rm t}(X)
is a closed subgroup of
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
and hence is a profinite
group. The structure of
J^{\rm t}(X)
will play a crucial role in our
exposition. In this section, we shall obtain a partial understanding of
this structure. We shall obtain a refined statement in a later section.
We let
p\geq 1
be the characteristic exponent of
k
and
{\mathbb{P}}
the set of prime
numbers different from
p
.

Proposition 3.5
.

There is a topologically exact sequence of topological
abelian groups

(3.7)

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{G^{(0)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}}{G^{(1)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}}\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\to 0.

Proof.

Using Proposition
2.13
and repeating the proof of
Proposition
3.4
, we get a topologically exact sequence

(3.8)

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}G^{(0)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\to 0.

We combine this with (
3.1
) to
conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The proof of Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
show that (
3.1
) and (
3.7
) are algebraically
exact if
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. We do not expect this to be true in general even
if we do not know of a counterexample. We shall in fact show in
Theorem
11.8
that (
3.7
) is algebraically exact
in all dimensions if
k
is a local field.

For an abelian group
G
and a prime
\ell
, we let
{G}_{\ell}={\underset{n\geq 1}{\varprojlim}}\ {G}/{\ell^{n}}
.
Suppose now that
G
is a profinite abelian group. Then
mG
is a closed
subgroup of
G
for every integer
m
because the multiplication map

G\xrightarrow{m}G
is continuous and
G
is compact Hausdorff.
In particular,
{G}/{m}
is a profinite abelian group and hence so is

{G}_{\ell}
for every prime
\ell
under the inverse limit topology. Moreover, the
completion map
G\to{G}_{\ell}
is continuous.
The following result should be known to experts.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
G
be a profinite abelian group. Then the canonical map

G\to{\underset{\ell}{\prod}}{G}_{\ell}
is a topological isomorphism,
where the product runs through all prime numbers.

Proof.

Since
G\to{\underset{\ell}{\prod}}{G}_{\ell}
is a continuous homomorphism between
compact Hausdorff spaces, it suffices to show that this map is bijective.
To prove this, we have to equivalently show that the canonical homomorphism

G\to{\underset{m\geq 1}{\varprojlim}}\ {G}/{m}
is bijective.
We let
\phi_{G}
denote this homomorphism. Then
\phi_{G}
is surjective by

[
81
, Cor. 1.1.6]
. To show that it is injective, note that

{\rm Ker}(\phi_{G})={\underset{m\geq 1}{\bigcap}}mG
is the subgroup of divisible
elements of
G
. But it is a standard fact that a profinite abelian group can not have
a non-trivial divisible element. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
G
be a profinite abelian group and
\ell
any prime number. Then the canonical
map
{G}/{\ell^{n}}\to{G_{\ell}}/{\ell^{n}}
is a topological isomorphism for every
integer
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

The composition
G\to{G_{\ell}}\to{G}/{\ell^{n}}
induces maps

{G}/{\ell^{n}}\to{G_{\ell}}/{\ell^{n}}\to{G}/{\ell^{n}}
whose composition is identity.
On the other hand, Lemma
3.7
implies that

{G}/{\ell^{n}}\to{G_{\ell}}/{\ell^{n}}
is surjective.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

For
p\geq 2
,
the inclusion
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\hookrightarrow H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
induces
a bijection

H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm fil}^{\rm t}H^{1}(K)\{p\}.

Proof.

By (
2.8
), (
2.9
) and Proposition
2.13
, it suffices to show that
if
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
, then

H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})\cap{\rm fil}_{\Delta(X)}H^{1}(K)=H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
.
Equivalently,

(3.9)

{\rm Ker}\left({\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}_{p}\twoheadrightarrow{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}_{p}\right)={\rm Ker}\left({\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}_{p}\twoheadrightarrow{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}_{p}\right).

This in turn is equivalent to showing that the canonical map

{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}_{p}\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}_{p}
is an
isomorphism. To show this, it suffices to show that
the map
{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/{p^{n}}\twoheadrightarrow{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}
is
an isomorphism for all integers
n\geq 1
.
Using Proposition
3.4
and Remark
3.6
,
it suffices to show that
\frac{G^{(0)}_{\Delta(X)}}{G^{(1)}_{\Delta(X)}}
is

p^{n}
-divisible. But the latter group is in fact uniquely
p^{n}
-divisible
by
[
62
, § 3, p. 650]
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

The canonical maps

{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\to{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}_{{\mathbb{P}}}
and

{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}_{p}\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}_{p}

are topological isomorphisms.
In particular, there is a canonical topological isomorphism between profinite groups

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\bigoplus{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}_{p}.

Proof.

The
p=1
case of the proposition is (
2.10
).
The
p\geq 2
case follows by
combining (
2.9
), Proposition
2.13
, Lemma
3.7
and
Lemma
3.9
.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
m
be an integer not divisible by
p
and
n\geq 1
any integer.
Then the canonical maps

{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m\to{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/m;

{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/{p^{n}}\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}

are isomorphisms of topological abelian groups.

Proof.

Combine Corollary
3.8
and Proposition
3.10
.
∎

3.3.
Structure of
J^{\rm t}(X)
when
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1

In order to understand
J^{\rm t}(X)
via Proposition
3.5
, we need to
have some information about
{G^{(0)}_{F}}/{G^{(1)}_{F}}
for a two-dimensional
local field
F
. Lemma
3.12
below serves this purpose.

For any commutative ring
A
, we let
K^{M}_{*}(A)
denote the direct sum

{\underset{n\geq 0}{\oplus}}K^{M}_{n}(A)
of Milnor
K
-groups of
A

(e.g., see
[
55
, Defn. 1.3]
). For an ideal
I\subset A
, we let

K^{M}_{n}(A,I)={\rm Ker}(K^{M}_{n}(A)\to K^{M}_{n}(A/I))
. Recall that if

F
is a discrete valuation field, then
F^{\times}
is a topological abelian group with
its ‘canonical topology’ for which
\{1+{\mathfrak{m}}^{m}_{F}\}_{m\geq 0}
form a base of
open neighborhoods of 0 (where
1+{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}_{F}:={\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{F}
).
In this paper,
F^{\times}
will always be considered a topological group with
its canonical topology.
For any integers
m\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, let

U^{\prime}_{m}K^{M}_{n}(F)
be the subgroup
\{1+{\mathfrak{m}}^{m}_{F},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{F},\cdots,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{F}\}\subset K^{M}_{n}(F)
. Note that
U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{n}(F)=K^{M}_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{F})
. We let

U^{\prime}_{-1}K^{M}_{n}(F)=K^{M}_{n}(F)
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
F
be a complete discrete valuation field whose residue field is a local
field
{\mathfrak{f}}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

If
{{\rm char}}({\mathfrak{f}})>0
, then
{G^{(0)}_{F}}/{G^{(1)}_{F}}
is a finite group of order
prime to
{{\rm char}}({\mathfrak{f}})
.

(2)

If
{{\rm char}}({\mathfrak{f}})=0
, then
G^{(1)}_{F}=0
and
G^{(0)}_{F}
is a finite group.

Proof.

We first assume that
{{\rm char}}({\mathfrak{f}})=p>0
.
Using the Pontryagin duality between profinite and discrete torsion groups,
it suffices to show that

\left({G^{(0)}_{F}}/{G^{(1)}_{F}}\right)^{\vee}\cong\frac{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(F)}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(F)}

is finite of order prime to
{{\rm char}}({\mathfrak{f}})
.

By
[
52
, § 3.1, 3.5]
, the cup product in Galois cohomology and the
Norm-residue map from Milnor
K
-theory to the Galois cohomology give rise
to a continuous pairing

(3.10)

K^{M}_{2}(F)\times H^{1}(F)\to H^{3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(F,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(2))\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

with respect to ‘Kato’s topology’ on
K^{M}_{2}(F)
. By definition, it is the finest
topology for which the product map
F^{\times}\times F^{\times}\to K^{M}_{2}(F)
is
continuous.

By the main result and Remark 4 of
[
52
, § 3.5]
, the pairing
(
3.10
) induces homomorphisms

(3.11)

H^{1}(F)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(K^{M}_{2}(F),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\hookrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(K^{M}_{2}(F),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}),

where the first map is an isomorphism of topological abelian groups.
Let
\psi_{F}
denote the composite homomorphism.
By
[
39
, Thm. 6.1, 6.3]
and
[
85
, Thm. 3.1]
(also see Proposition
7.1
), it follows that
\psi_{F}
induces
a compatible system of homomorphism of abelian groups

(3.12)

\psi_{F}\colon{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(F)\to{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(\frac{K^{M}_{2}(F)}{U^{\prime}_{n}K^{M}_{2}(F)},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})

for all
n\geq 0
. Taking the graded quotients, we get a homomorphism

(3.13)

\overline{\psi}_{F}\colon\frac{{\rm fil}_{n+1}H^{1}(F)}{{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(F)}\to{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(\frac{U^{\prime}_{n}K^{M}_{2}(F)}{U^{\prime}_{n+1}K^{M}_{2}(F)},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})

for every
n\geq 0
.
Moreover,
[
39
, Thm. 6.3]
and
[
85
, Thm. 3.1]
imply that this map is injective.
Since
\frac{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(F)}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(F)}\xrightarrow{\cong}K^{M}_{2}({\mathfrak{f}})
, we
conclude that
\frac{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(F)}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(F)}
is a subgroup of

{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(K^{M}_{2}({\mathfrak{f}}),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
.

Next, the main result of

[
73
]
implies that
K_{2}^{M}({\mathfrak{f}})=H_{1}\oplus H_{2}
, where
H_{1}
is a
uniquely divisible group and
H_{2}
is the group of roots of unity in
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
Furthermore, it is well known that
H_{2}\cong{{\mathbb{Z}}}/(q-1)
, where
q
is the
cardinality of the residue field of
{\mathfrak{f}}

(e.g., see
[
79
, Prop. 5.7 (ii)]
). We thus get an inclusion

\frac{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(F)}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(F)}\hookrightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(H_{1},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\oplus{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(H_{2},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(H_{1},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\oplus{{\mathbb{Z}}}/(q-1).

Since
\frac{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(F)}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(F)}
is a torsion group and

{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(H_{1},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
is uniquely divisible, it follows that

\frac{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(F)}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(F)}\hookrightarrow{{\mathbb{Z}}}/(q-1)
.
This finishes the proof of part (1) of the lemma.

We now assume that
{{\rm char}}({\mathfrak{f}})=0
. In this case, one knows that

G^{(1)}_{F}=0
(e.g., see
[
91
, Chap. IV, Cor. 2]
).
To understand the structure of
G^{(0)}_{F}
, we use the short exact sequence

(3.14)

0\to H^{1}({\mathfrak{f}})\to H^{1}(F)\to H^{0}({\mathfrak{f}})\to 0

of discrete abelian groups by
[
52
, § 3, Lem. 2]
.
This sequence implies that
G^{(0)}_{F}={\rm Ker}(G_{F}\twoheadrightarrow G_{\mathfrak{f}})=(H^{0}({\mathfrak{f}}))^{\vee}
.
On the other hand, we have
H^{0}({\mathfrak{f}})=H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1))\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(\mu_{\infty}({\mathfrak{f}}),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
, where
\mu_{\infty}({\mathfrak{f}})
is the group of roots of
unity in
{\mathfrak{f}}
. Since
\mu_{\infty}({\mathfrak{f}})
is a finite
group (e.g., see
[
79
, Prop. 5.7(i)]
),
it follows that
G^{(0)}_{F}
is finite.
∎

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
X
be as in Proposition
3.5
with
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. Then
J^{\rm t}(X)
is
a finite group of order prime to the
characteristic exponent of
k
.

Proof.

Combine Remark
3.6
and
Lemma
3.12
.
∎

4.
The tame class group

In this section, we shall introduce the tame class group for normal
varieties over an infinite field and prove its functorial properties.
We fix an arbitrary infinite field
k
throughout this
section.

4.1.
Lower Milnor
K
-theory of semilocal rings

In order to define the tame class group, we need some results about the lower Milnor

K
-theory of
semilocal rings. For a Noetherian scheme
X
, we let
K_{j}(X)
denote the
Quillen
K
-groups of the exact category of locally free sheaves on
X
.
We shall usually write
K_{j}(X)=K_{j}(A)
when
X={\rm Spec\,}(A)
is an affine scheme.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
A
be a Noetherian semilocal ring containing
k
. Then the canonical map

K^{M}_{j}(A)\to K_{j}(A)
is an isomorphism for
j\leq 2
.

Proof.

The lemma is trivial for
j=0
and follows from
[
109
, Chap. III, Lem. 1.4]

for
j=1
. To prove it for
j=2
, we consider the maps

(4.1)

K^{M}_{2}(A)\to K_{2}(A)\to\frac{H_{2}(GL(A),{\mathbb{Z}})}{H_{2}(GL_{1}(A),{\mathbb{Z}})},

where the right arrow is the 2nd Hurewicz map for
BGL^{+}(A)
. A theorem of
Suslin (which was shown for semilocal rings by Levine in

[
69
, § 3, Thm. 1]
)
says that the composite map is an isomorphism. On the other hand,
the Hurewicz map is an isomorphism by
[
109
, Chap. III, Lem. 1.4]
and

[
78
, Lem. 4.2]
. It follows that the left arrow in
(
4.1
) is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that
A
is the semilocal ring at a finite set of closed points

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
on a regular integral curve over
k
. Let
F
denote the
quotient field of
A
. Then the Gersten sequence

0\to K^{M}_{j}(A)\to K^{M}_{j}(F)\xrightarrow{\partial_{F}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K^{M}_{j-1}(k(x_{i}))\to 0

is exact for every
j\leq 2
.

Proof.

The lemma follows immediately from Lemma
4.1
and

[
80
, Thm. 5.11]
given the known fact that
\partial_{F}
coincides
with the corresponding differential in the Gersten complex for Quillen
K
-theory
(e.g., see
[
37
