, Lem. A1]
).
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
A
be as in Lemma
4.2
and let
I\subset A
be a nonzero
radical ideal.
Let
A_{i}
be the localization of
A
at
x_{i}
and let

{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
be the maximal ideal of
A_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
.
Then we have the following relations between the subgroups of
K^{M}_{j}(F)
for

j\leq 2
.

K^{M}_{j}(A)=\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigcap}}}K^{M}_{j}(A_{i})\ \mbox{and}\ K^{M}_{j}(A,I)=\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigcap}}}K^{M}_{j}(A_{i},IA_{i}).

Proof.

The first equality follows directly by comparing the exact sequence of
Lemma
4.2
for
A
with similar exact sequences for
A_{i}
’s.
To prove the other equality, note that it already follows from
Lemma
4.2
that
K^{M}_{j}(A,I)\subseteq\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigcap}}}K^{M}_{j}(A_{i},IA_{i})
.
It is also clear that
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigcap}}}K^{M}_{j}(A_{i},IA_{i})\subset\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigcap}}}K^{M}_{j}(A_{i})=K^{M}_{j}(A)
.
To finish the proof, we only need to observe that the canonical map

A/I\to\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{A_{i}}/{IA_{i}}
is a ring
isomorphism so that we have
K^{M}_{j}(A/I)\xrightarrow{\cong}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K^{M}_{j}({A_{i}}/{IA_{i}})
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
A
be a discrete valuation ring containing an infinite field
whose quotient field is
F
and the maximal ideal is
{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Let
\widehat{A}
denote the
{\mathfrak{m}}
-adic completion of
A
and
\widehat{F}
the fraction field
of
\widehat{A}
. Let
I\subset{\mathfrak{m}}

be a nonzero ideal. Then the commutative squares

(4.2)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(A,I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(\widehat{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(\widehat{F})}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(\widehat{A},I\widehat{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{j}(\widehat{A})}

are Cartesian for every
j\geq 0
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the Gersten resolution for
Milnor
K
-theory of regular local rings (e.g., see
[
57
]
)
once we observe that the canonical map
A/I\to{\widehat{A}}/{I\widehat{A}}\cong A/I\otimes_{A}\widehat{A}\cong\widehat{A/I}
is
an isomorphism because
A/I
is
{\mathfrak{m}}
-adically complete.
∎

Let
C
be an integral regular curve over
k
.
For an effective Cartier divisor
D\subset{C}
and a closed point

x\in{C}
, we let
\widehat{I_{D,x}}\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}}
be the
ideal of the scheme theoretic inverse image of
D
under the
canonical composite map
{\rm Spec\,}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{C,x}})\to{\rm Spec\,}({{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}})\to C
.
We let
I_{D,x}
be the extension of the ideal of
D
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{C,x}
.
Consider the map
K^{M}_{j}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}\left(K^{M}_{j-1}(k(x))\oplus K^{M}_{j}(k(x))\right)
,
whose
x
-component is defined by
a\mapsto(\partial_{x}(a),\partial_{x}(\{\pi_{x}\}\cup a))
for a fixed choice of uniformizing parameter

\pi_{x}
at
x
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
I_{D}\subset A_{D}:={\mathcal{O}}_{C,D}
be the ideal of
D
and let
j\leq 2
be any integer.
Then the canonical maps

\begin{array}[]{lll}K^{M}_{j}(A_{D},I_{D})&\to&{\rm Ker}\left(K^{M}_{j}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}\frac{K^{M}_{j}(k(C))}{K^{M}_{j}({{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}},I_{D,x})}\right)\\
&\to&{\rm Ker}\left(K^{M}_{j}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}\frac{K^{M}_{j}(k(C)_{x})}{K^{M}_{j}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}},\widehat{I_{D.x}})}\right)\end{array}

are isomorphisms. If
D
is reduced, then we also have

K^{M}_{j}(A_{D},I_{D})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Ker}\left[K^{M}_{j}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}\left(K^{M}_{j-1}(k(x))\oplus K^{M}_{j}(k(x))\right)\right].

Proof.

The first part of the lemma follows directly by combining
Corollary
4.3
and Lemma
4.4
.
To prove the second part, we can use

{\rm Ker}\left(K^{M}_{j}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}\frac{K^{M}_{j}(k(C))}{K^{M}_{j}({{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}},I_{D,x})}\right)
instead of

K^{M}_{j}(A_{D},I_{D})
. The desired isomorphism is then
an easy exercise using the Gersten exact sequence for Milnor
K
-theory
and some properties of the boundary map in the Gersten sequence.
∎

For an effective Cartier divisor
D\subset{C}
and integer
j\geq 0
, we let

(4.3)

{\rm fil}_{D}K^{M}_{j}(k(C))={\rm Ker}\left(K^{M}_{j}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}\frac{K^{M}_{j}(k(C)_{x})}{K^{M}_{j}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}},\widehat{I_{D,x}})}\right).

It follows from Lemma
4.5
that

{\rm fil}_{D}K^{M}_{j}(k(C))=K^{M}_{j}(A_{D},I_{D})
if
j\leq 2
.

The following generalization of Lemma
4.2
for smooth
k
-algebras
will be useful later on in this paper (see Theorem
5.14
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Assume that
A
is the semilocal ring at a finite set of closed points

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
on a smooth integral curve over
k
. Let
F
denote the
quotient field of
A
. Then the Gersten sequence

0\to K^{M}_{j}(A)\to K^{M}_{j}(F)\xrightarrow{\partial_{F}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K^{M}_{j-1}(k(x_{i}))\to 0

is exact for every
j\geq 0
. In particular, Corollary
4.3
and
Lemma
4.5
hold for every
j\geq 0
.

Proof.

Combine
[
25
, Prop. 4.3]
and
[
57
, Thm. 1.3]
.
∎

4.2.
Tame class group and class group with modulus

For an integral curve
C
over
k
, recall that
\overline{C}_{n}
denotes the
unique normal compactification of
C_{n}
and
\Delta(C_{n})=\overline{C}_{n}\setminus C_{n}
.
Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be an integral normal scheme.
For
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X),\ x\in C_{(0)}
and
j\geq 0
, there is a boundary homomorphism

\partial_{C,x}\colon K^{M}_{j+1}(k(C))\to K^{M}_{j}(k(x))
(see
[
53
]
)
which is defined to be the composite map

K^{M}_{j+1}(k(C))\cong K^{M}_{j+1}(k(C_{n}))\xrightarrow{\partial}{\underset{y\in\pi^{-1}(x)}{\oplus}}K^{M}_{j}(k(y))\xrightarrow{N}K^{M}_{j}(k(x))
, where
\pi\colon C_{n}\to C

is the normalization map,
\partial
is the
classical tame symbol and
N
is the sum of norm maps.
Since
\partial_{C,x}(\alpha)=0
for almost all
x
(depending on
\alpha
) , we get
a map
\partial_{C}\colon K^{M}_{j+1}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\oplus}}K^{M}_{j}(k(x))
.
Taking the sum over all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, we get a boundary homomorphism

\partial^{j}_{X}\colon{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\oplus}}K^{M}_{j+1}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\oplus}}K^{M}_{j}(k(x))
.
The following definition is due to Yamazaki
[
111
, Defn. 1.1]
.

Definition 4.7
.

The tame class group of
X
is defined to be the cokernel of the boundary homomorphism

\partial^{1}_{X}\colon{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))\longrightarrow{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))

and it is denoted by
C^{\rm t}(X)
.

Remark 4.8
.

Yamazaki uses a group
UK^{M}_{2}(k(C))
instead of
{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
.
But the two groups coincide by Lemma
4.5
.

Note that
{\mathcal{C}}(X)=\emptyset
when
X
is 0-dimensional, and hence

C^{\rm t}(X)=K^{M}_{1}(k(X))
. Note also that when
k
is a local field, then
k(x)
is a
local
field so that
K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
is a topological
abelian group with its canonical topology, for every
x\in X_{(0)}
.
In particular, the right hand side of
\partial^{1}_{X}
is a topological abelian group
endowed with the direct sum topology.
We can therefore endow
C^{\rm t}(X)
with the quotient topology. It follows that

C^{\rm t}(X)
is a topological abelian group when
k
is a local field.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\overline{X}
be an integral normal
k
-scheme and
D\subset\overline{X}
an effective
Cartier divisor with complement
X
. The idele class group with modulus of the pair

(\overline{X},D)
is defined to be the group

SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)={\rm Coker}\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{1}_{X}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\right),

where
\nu\colon\overline{C}_{n}\to\overline{X}
is the canonical map from the normalization of

\overline{C}\subset\overline{X}
for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
.

If
k
is a local field, we can endow
SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
with the
structure of a topological
abelian group in the same way as we did for
C^{\rm t}(X)
.

It follows from Lemma
4.2
that for any effective divisor

E\subset\overline{C}_{n}
having support
\Delta(C_{n})
, the boundary map

{\rm fil}_{E}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\to{\underset{x\in\overline{X}_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}\ K^{M}_{1}(k(x))

factorizes as

{\rm fil}_{E}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\to{\underset{x\in{X}_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}\ K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\hookrightarrow{\underset{x\in\overline{X}_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}\ K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
.
This implies that for any effective Cartier divisors
D\leq D^{\prime}\subset\overline{X}

whose complement is
X
, there are canonical homomorphisms

(4.4)

SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi_{\overline{X}|D}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}C^{\rm t}(X)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}),

where we let
SK_{1}(\overline{X}):=SK_{1}(\overline{X}|\emptyset)
. These are continuous
homomorphisms of topological abelian groups when
k
is a local
field. We shall see later in this paper that the last arrow is also surjective
when
\overline{X}
is smooth and projective over
k
(see Theorem
5.13
).
The following result provides a relation between the idele class group with
modulus and the tame class group.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
R=[\tfrac{1}{p}]
if
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
and
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
with
m\neq 0
if

{\rm char}(k)=0
. Then the map

\psi_{\overline{X}|D}\colon SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\otimes R\to C^{\rm t}(X)\otimes R

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We first observe that the map
\partial^{1}_{X}
is defined on all of

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))
. Using this observation,
it suffices to show that
{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))
and
{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))
have the same image in
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\otimes R
for every

C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
.
To this end, it is enough to show that the map

\frac{K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})}{K^{M}_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},x}},I_{D,x})}\otimes R\to\frac{K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})}{K^{M}_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},x}},I_{\Delta(C_{n}),x})}\otimes R

is an isomorphism for every
x\in\Delta(C_{n})
.
But this follows from the well known fact that if

A
is a Noetherian local ring containing
k
and
I\subset A
is a
nilpotent ideal, then
K^{M}_{2}(A,I)\otimes R=0

(e.g., apply
[
108
, Thm. 1.3]
to
{I^{i}}/{I^{i+1}}
for all
i\geq 1
).
∎

In the sequel, we shall use the following notations for brevity.

(4.5)

F^{0}_{1}(X)={\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x)),\ F^{1}_{2}(\overline{X}|D)={\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\ \ \mbox{and}

F^{1}_{2}(X)={\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})).

4.3.
Covariance of tame class group

In the rest of §
4
, we shall prove some functorial properties of the tame
class group. We fix an infinite field
k
. To show that the tame class group is a
covariant functor on the category of certain
k
-schemes, we need a lemma.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
A
be the local ring at a closed point of an integral regular curve over
k

and let
f\colon A\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}
be a finite morphism of semilocal Dedekind domains.
Let
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) denote the Jacobson radical of
A
(resp.
A^{\prime}
).
Let
F
(resp.
F^{\prime}
) denote the quotient field of
A
(resp.
A^{\prime}
). Then the norm
map
f_{*}\colon K^{M}_{2}(F^{\prime})\to K^{M}_{2}(F)
restricts to

f_{*}\colon K^{M}_{2}(A^{\prime})\to K^{M}_{2}(A)\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ f_{*}\colon K^{M}_{2}(A^{\prime},J^{\prime})\to K^{M}_{2}(A,J).

Proof.

To prove the lemma, we can replace
K^{M}_{2}(-)
by
K_{2}(-)
using
Lemma
4.1
.
It follows from
[
39
, Lem. 3.4]
that
f_{*}
induces the norm map

f_{*}\colon K_{2}(A^{\prime})\to K_{2}(A)
. Using Lemmas
4.1
,

4.2
and
[
37
, Lem. 11.3]
,
we see that these norm maps coincide with the push-forward maps between
Quillen
K
-groups associated to the finite morphisms of rings and fields.

To show that the push-forward map
f_{*}
preserves the relative
K
-groups,
we let
\widehat{A}
and
\widehat{A^{\prime}}
be the completions with respect to the Jacobson
ideals. Let
{\mathfrak{f}}
be the residue field of
A
and
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{f}}_{r}
the
residue fields of
A^{\prime}
. Let
A^{\prime}_{i}
be the localization of
A^{\prime}
at the maximal
ideal corresponding to the residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}
. Let
e_{i}
denote the ramification
index of the extension
A\to A^{\prime}_{i}
.

Since
J^{\prime}=\sqrt{JA^{\prime}}
, it follows that
\widehat{A^{\prime}}
is the
J
-adic completion
of
A^{\prime}
when treated as an
A
-module. Since
A^{\prime}
is finite over
A
, it
follows from
[
71
, Thm. 8.7, 8.15]
that the maps

\widehat{A}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}\to\widehat{A^{\prime}}\to\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}\widehat{A^{\prime}_{i}}

are isomorphisms, where
\widehat{A^{\prime}_{i}}
is the completion of
A^{\prime}_{i}
with respect to its
maximal ideal. Equivalently, the diagram of rings

(4.6)

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\widehat{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}\widehat{A^{\prime}_{i}}}

is co-Cartesian whose all arrows are flat and the vertical arrows are
finite. Let
\widehat{f}_{i}\colon\widehat{A}\hookrightarrow\widehat{A^{\prime}_{i}}
and
f^{\prime}_{i}\colon{\mathfrak{f}}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}

be the inclusions.

We now consider the diagram

(4.7)

\textstyle{K_{j}(A^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\oplus}}}K_{j}(\widehat{A^{\prime}_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}_{*}}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\oplus}}}K_{j}({\mathfrak{f}}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{i}e_{i}(f^{\prime}_{i})_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{j}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{j}(\widehat{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{j}({\mathfrak{f}})}

of
K_{*}(A)
-modules for
j\geq 0
, in which the horizontal arrows are the
canonical pull-back maps in
K
-theory. To finish the proof of the lemma,
it suffices to show that the outer rectangle in (
4.7
) is
commutative. Using (
4.6
), it
follows from
[
80
, § 7, Prop. 2.11]
that the left square is commutative.
It remains to show that the right square in (
4.7
) is commutative.
Since the middle vertical arrow is the same as the map
{\sum_{i}(\widehat{f}_{i})_{*}}
,
this commutativity follows by applying Lemma
4.12
for
i=2
,
in which case the Milnor and Quillen
K
-groups of the underlying rings coincide.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
f\colon A\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}

be a finite extension of complete discrete valuation rings
containing
k
with ramification index
e
.
Let
f^{\prime}\colon{\mathfrak{f}}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}
be the induced map between the residue fields.
Then the square

(4.8)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}(A^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}({\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ef^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}({\mathfrak{f}})}

is commutative for all
i\geq 0
if we let

\phi\colon A\to{\mathfrak{f}}
and
\phi^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}
be
the quotient maps.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be a uniformizer for
A
and let
F
(resp.
F^{\prime}
) denote the
quotient field of
A
(resp.
A^{\prime}
). Consider the diagram

(4.9)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}(A^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i+1}(F^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}({\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i+1}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{F}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{i}({\mathfrak{f}}),}

where
\alpha(a)=\{\pi\}\cup a
and
\alpha^{\prime}(b)=\{\pi\}\cup b
.
It follows from the projection formula for the norm maps between the Milnor

K
-theory of fields that the left square is commutative. The right square
is commutative by
[
39
, Lem. 3.4]
.
In particular, the outer rectangle
is commutative. It remains to show that this rectangle is nothing but
the diagram (
4.8
).

Now, we first note that the composite map

\partial_{F}\circ\alpha\circ f_{*}
in (
4.9
)
coincides with
\phi^{*}\circ f_{*}
. To compute
f^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial_{F^{\prime}}\circ\alpha^{\prime}
,
we write
\pi=u\pi^{\prime e}
for some
u\in{A^{\prime}}^{\times}
, where
\pi^{\prime}
is a
uniformizer for
A^{\prime}
. We then get

f^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial_{F^{\prime}}\circ\alpha^{\prime}(a)=f^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial_{F^{\prime}}(\{\pi\}\cup a)=f^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial_{F^{\prime}}(u\cup a)+ef^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial_{F^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}\cup a)=ef^{\prime}_{*}\circ\phi^{\prime*}(a),

where the last equality holds because the composite
K^{M}_{i+1}(A^{\prime})\to K^{M}_{i+1}(F^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\partial_{F^{\prime}}}K^{M}_{i}({\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime})
is zero (being part of the Gersten complex for
the Milnor
K
-theory of
A^{\prime}
). This concludes the proof.
∎

We shall now prove the covariance property of the tame class group.
Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism of integral normal
k
-schemes.
For every
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{(0)}
, we have the norm map
f_{*}\colon K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\to K^{M}_{1}(k(f(x)))
. This induces a homomorphism
f_{*}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})\to F^{0}_{1}(X)
.

Proposition 4.13
.

f_{*}
descends to a push-forward homomorphism

f_{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime})\to C^{\rm t}(X),

which is continuous when
k
is a local field.

Proof.

Let
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}(X^{\prime})
and let
\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n}
be the unique normal compactification of
C^{\prime}_{n}
.
The scheme theoretic image of
C^{\prime}
in
X
is either a closed
point or a closed integral curve.
If
f(C^{\prime})
is a closed point, then
f
uniquely defines a morphism

f^{\prime}\colon\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n}\to X
whose image is a closed point, say
x
. We let

k^{\prime}=k(x)
.

We now have a commutative diagram

(4.10)

\textstyle{{\rm fil}^{1}_{\Delta(C^{\prime}_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(C^{\prime}_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{1}(k^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X).}

Since
\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n}
is complete and regular, Suslin’s reciprocity law
(see
[
94
]
)
tells us that the composition of the first two bottom horizontal arrows in
(
4.10
) is trivial. Hence, our assertion follows in this case.

Suppose now that the scheme theoretic image of
C^{\prime}
is a closed integral curve

C\subset X
.
We then get a commutative diagram

(4.11)

\textstyle{{C}^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{C}_{n},}

in which the right vertical arrow is necessarily a finite morphism and
\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n}=f^{-1}(C_{n})
so that
the square is necessarily Cartesian and
\Delta(\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n})=f^{-1}(\Delta(C_{n}))
.
By
[
39
, Lem. 3.4]
, it follows that for any

\alpha\in K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n}))
, one has
f_{*}\circ\partial(\alpha))=\partial\circ f_{*}(\alpha)
.
It remains therefore to show that
f_{*}(\alpha)\in{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))

if
\alpha\in{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C^{\prime}_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n}))
. Note that it follows from (
4.3
) that

{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C^{\prime}_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n}))\subset{\rm fil}_{\Delta(\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n}))
.

Let
x\in\Delta(C_{n})
be any point.
By Lemma
4.5
, it suffices to show
that
f_{*}(\alpha)\in K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},x},{\mathfrak{m}}_{x})
. To this end, we let

S=f^{-1}(\{x\})\subset\Delta(\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n})
. It follows from
Corollary
4.3
and Lemma
4.5
that

\alpha\in K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n},S},J_{S})
, where
J_{S}
is the Jacobson radical of

{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n},S}
. We now apply Lemma
4.11
to the finite extension

{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n},S}
to conclude that
f_{*}(\alpha)\in K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},x},{\mathfrak{m}}_{x})
. This concludes the proof of the assertion that

f_{*}
descends to the level of the tame class groups.

Suppose now that
k
is a local field. Since the tame class groups are endowed with the
quotient topologies, it suffices to show that the map
f_{*}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})\to F^{0}_{1}(X)

is continuous. Since the source and the target of the latter map have direct sum
topologies
induced by the canonical topology of each summand, and since the canonical topology of
each summand is a subgroup topology, it follows from
[
39
, Lem 2.7]

that
F^{0}_{1}(X)
and
F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})
have subgroup topologies. It follows therefore from

[
39
, Lem 2.8]
that
f_{*}
will be continuous if we can show that
the inverse image of a generating open subgroup is open.

Let
U\subset F^{0}_{1}(X)
be an open subgroup and let
V=f^{-1}_{*}(U)
.
To show that
V
is open, it suffices to show that for any finite set of closed
points
S^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
, the intersection
V\cap K_{1}(S^{\prime})
is open in
K_{1}(S^{\prime})
.
Letting
S=f(S^{\prime})
and
U_{S}=U\cap K_{1}(S)
, it suffices to show that

f^{-1}_{*}(U_{S})
is open under the push-forward map
f_{*}\colon K_{1}(S^{\prime})\to K_{1}(S)
.
Hence, it suffices to show that for every closed point
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
, the norm map

K^{M}_{1}(k(x^{\prime}))\to K^{M}_{1}(k(f(x^{\prime})))
is continuous with respect to the canonical
topologies
on both sides. The whole problem is thus reduced to showing the independent statement
that if
{F^{\prime}}/F
is a finite extension of local fields, then the norm map

N_{{F^{\prime}}/F}\colon K^{M}_{1}(F^{\prime})\to K^{M}_{1}(F)
is continuous. But this is well known, and
easily follows using the compatibility of the norm map with the
normalized valuation maps for the two local fields (e.g., see

[
39
, Cor. 5.6]
). This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.4.
Contravariance of tame class group

Let
k
be an infinite field of characteristic
p>0

and
\overline{X}
a complete integral normal
k
-scheme. Let

{k^{\prime}}/k
be a purely inseparable algebraic extension of fields. Suppose that
there is an increasing sequence of finite field extensions
{k_{i}}/k
inside
k^{\prime}
such
that
k=k_{0}
and
k^{\prime}=\cup_{i}k_{i}
. For any
i\geq 1
, we let

\overline{X}_{i}
be the normalization of
(\overline{X}_{k_{i}})_{\rm red}
. We let
g_{i}\colon\overline{X}_{k_{i}}\to\overline{X}
and
f_{i}\colon\overline{X}_{i}\to\overline{X}
denote the projection morphisms.
We shall denote the restriction of
g_{i}
to
(\overline{X}_{k_{i}})_{\rm red}
also by
g_{i}
.
We let
\overline{X}^{\prime}
denote the normalization of
(\overline{X}_{k^{\prime}})_{\rm red}
.
Let
g\colon\overline{X}_{k^{\prime}}\to\overline{X}
and
f\colon\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
be the
projection morphisms. We fix a nonempty open immersion
u\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
.
We define
X_{i}
and
X^{\prime}
analogous to the definitions of
\overline{X}_{i}
and
\overline{X}^{\prime}
.
Let
f^{\prime}_{i}\colon\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}_{i}
be the projection morphism.
The following lemma is an elementary exercise. We refer to
[
93
, Tag 01YV]

for a proof.

Lemma 4.14
.

The inverse system of schemes
\{\overline{X}_{k_{i}}\}_{i\geq 0}
satisfy the following.

(1)

\{\overline{X}_{k_{i}}\}
is an inverse system of complete irreducible

k
-schemes with finite transition maps whose inverse limit is
the irreducible
k^{\prime}
-scheme
\overline{X}_{k^{\prime}}
.

(2)

\{(\overline{X}_{k_{i}})_{\rm red}\}
is an inverse system of complete
integral
k
-schemes with finite
transition maps whose inverse limit is the integral
k^{\prime}
-scheme
(\overline{X}_{k^{\prime}})_{\rm red}
.

(3)

\{\overline{X}_{i}\}
is an inverse system of complete integral normal
k
-schemes with
finite transition maps whose inverse limit is the complete integral normal

k^{\prime}
-scheme
\overline{X}^{\prime}
.

(4)

The square

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\overline{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\overline{X}}

is Cartesian.

Let
f\colon\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
be as above. Assume that the transition map

f_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i}
is flat for each
j\geq i\geq 0
.
For
x\in X_{(0)}
, we let
S_{x}
be the scheme theoretic
inverse image of
x
under
f
and let
S^{\prime}_{x}
be the set theoretic inverse image
(with the reduced subscheme structure).
Our assumption implies that
S_{x}
is a 0-dimensional
k^{\prime}
-scheme. In particular,

S^{\prime}_{x}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
is a finite set. We let

S_{x}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\coprod}}}S^{i}_{x}
, where
(S^{i}_{x})_{\rm red}={\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime}(x_{i}))
for
1\leq i\leq s
.
Let
\iota_{x}\colon S^{\prime}_{x}\hookrightarrow S_{x}
be the inclusion and let
f^{\prime}_{x}=f_{x}\circ\iota_{x}
.
We then have a pull-back map

f^{*}_{x}\colon K_{n}(k(x))\to G_{n}(S_{x})
and the push-forward map

\iota_{x*}\colon K_{n}(S^{\prime}_{x})\xrightarrow{\cong}G_{n}(S_{x})
.
We let
f^{*}\colon K_{n}(k(x))\to K_{n}(S^{\prime}_{x})\cong\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K_{n}(k^{\prime}(x_{i}))
be the map
\iota^{-1}_{x*}\circ f^{*}_{x}
. For
n=0
, a straightforward application of the Devissage theorem
shows that
f^{*}_{x}(1)=f^{*}_{x}([k(x)])=[{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{x}}]=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\ell({S^{i}_{x}})[k^{\prime}(x_{i})]=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\ell({S^{i}_{x}})\iota_{x*}([k^{\prime}(x_{i})])=\iota_{x*}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\ell({S^{i}_{x}})f_{x,i}^{*}(1))
, where
f_{x,i}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime}(x_{i}))\to{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
is the projection.
Here, for an Artinian local ring
R
, we let
\ell({\rm Spec\,}(R))
denote
\ell(R)
.

Since
f^{*}_{x},f^{*}_{x,i}
and
\iota_{x*}
are known to be
K_{*}(k(x))
-linear, it follows
that for any
n\geq 0
and
\alpha\in K_{n}(k(x))
, one has

f^{*}_{x}(\alpha)=f^{*}_{x}(\alpha\cdot 1)=\alpha\cdot f^{*}_{x}(1)=\iota_{x*}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\ell({S^{i}_{x}})f^{*}_{x,i}(\alpha))
.
We conclude that

(4.12)

f^{*}=(\ell({S^{1}_{x}})f^{*}_{x,1},\cdots,\ell({S^{s}_{x}})f^{*}_{x,s}).

Taking
n=1
and summing over
x\in X_{(0)}
, we get

f^{*}\colon{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\to{\underset{x\in X^{\prime}_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
.

Proposition 4.15
.

f^{*}
descends to a homomorphism

f^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime})

such that
f^{*}=f^{\prime*}_{i}\circ f^{*}_{i}
for every
i\geq 0
.
Moreover, the map
f^{*}_{i}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X_{i})
is continuous when
k
is a
local field.

Proof.

We now let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
and let
\overline{C}
be the closure of
C
in
\overline{X}
.
We let
\nu\colon\overline{C}_{n}\to\overline{X}
be the canonical map from the normalization of

\overline{C}
. We let
\overline{C}^{\prime}=\overline{C}\times_{\overline{X}}\overline{X}^{\prime}
and let

\overline{C}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}^{\prime}_{r}
be the irreducible components of
\overline{C}^{\prime}
.
Since
\overline{X}^{\prime}
is an inverse limit of integral schemes with finite transition maps,
it is an easy exercise to check that each
C^{\prime}_{i}\subset\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}
is dense, if
we let
C^{\prime}_{i}=\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}\cap X^{\prime}
. We set

\overline{Y}=\overline{C}_{n}\times_{\overline{X}}\overline{X}^{\prime}\cong\overline{C}_{n}\times_{\overline{C}}\overline{C}^{\prime}
and
let
\widetilde{g}\colon\overline{Y}\to\overline{C}_{n}
and
\nu^{\prime}\colon\overline{Y}\to\overline{X}^{\prime}
be the
projections. Let
\nu^{\prime}_{i}\colon\overline{Y}_{i}:=(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i})_{n}\to\overline{X}^{\prime}
and

\widetilde{g}_{i}\colon\overline{Y}_{i}\to\overline{C}_{n}
be the canonical maps. It is clear that
each
\widetilde{g}_{i}
is flat. We let

Z=\coprod_{i}\overline{Y}_{i}=(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{\rm red})_{n}\xleftarrow{\cong}(\overline{Y}_{\rm red})_{n}
.
The last isomorphism holds because
\overline{Y}\to\overline{C}^{\prime}
is a finite birational
morphism of curves over
k^{\prime}
.

We let
W
(resp.
W_{i}
) be the spectrum of the semilocal ring

{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},T}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Y}_{i},T_{i}}
), where
T=\nu^{-1}(\overline{X}\setminus X)
and

T_{i}=\nu^{\prime-1}_{i}(\overline{X}^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime})=\widetilde{g}^{-1}_{i}(T)
.
We let
W^{\prime}={\rm Spec\,}(k(C))\times_{\overline{C}_{n}}\overline{Y}
so
that
{\rm Spec\,}(k(Z))\xrightarrow{\cong}W^{\prime}_{\rm red}
via the map
Z\to\overline{Y}
.
Let
I\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},T}
be the ideal of
T
and
I_{i}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Y}_{i},T_{i}}
the ideal of
T_{i}
. Note that
W^{\prime}_{\rm red}=W^{\prime}\times_{\overline{Y}}\overline{Y}_{\rm red}

because the inclusion
W^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{Y}
is a limit of open immersions.
We write
W^{\prime}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\coprod}}}W^{\prime}_{i}
, where
(W^{\prime}_{i})_{\rm red}={\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime}(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}))
. We let
\pi_{i}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime}(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}))\hookrightarrow W^{\prime}
be the
inclusion. Let
g^{\prime}
(resp.
g^{\prime}_{i}
) be the restriction of
\widetilde{g}
to

W^{\prime}_{\rm red}
(resp.
{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime}(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}))
).

We fix
\alpha\in K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},T},I)
and let
\Sigma\subset C_{n}
be the
smallest 0-dimensional reduced closed subscheme such that
\partial(\alpha)\in K_{1}(\Sigma)\subset F^{0}_{1}(C_{n})
. Let
S=\nu(\Sigma)
and
S^{\prime}=S\times_{X}X^{\prime}
. We let

\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\times_{C_{n}}\nu^{\prime-1}(X^{\prime})
. We let

\pi\colon W^{\prime}_{\rm red}\hookrightarrow W^{\prime},\ \tau^{\prime}\colon\Sigma^{\prime}_{\rm red}\hookrightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}
and

\tau\colon S^{\prime}_{\rm red}\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
be the inclusion maps.
To show that
f^{*}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X)\to F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})
takes
\partial^{1}_{X}(F^{1}_{2}(X))

into
\partial^{1}_{X^{\prime}}(F^{1}_{2}(X^{\prime}))
, we need to show that

\tau^{-1}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{S}\circ\nu_{*}(\partial(\alpha))
lies in the image of

\partial^{1}_{X^{\prime}}\colon F^{1}_{2}(X^{\prime})\to F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})
, where
f_{S}\colon S^{\prime}\to S
is
the restriction of
f
to
S^{\prime}
.

To show the above, we consider the diagram

(4.13)

\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K_{2}(W_{i},T_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{1}}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Y}_{i},T_{i}},I_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{2}}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigoplus}}}K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{{3}}
\textstyle{K_{2}(W^{\prime}_{\rm red})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{i}\ell({W^{\prime}_{i}})\pi_{i*}}
\textstyle{K_{2}(W,T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},T},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oplus_{i}g^{\prime*}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(C))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\oplus_{i}g^{\prime*}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{{4}}
\textstyle{G_{2}(W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{{5}}
\textstyle{K_{2}(W^{\prime}_{\rm red})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{1}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{6}}
\textstyle{G_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{7}}
\textstyle{K_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\rm red})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{1}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{S}}
\textstyle{G_{1}(S^{\prime})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(S^{\prime}_{\rm red})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

The squares (1) and (2) in this diagram commute by the contravariant functoriality of

K
-theory. The computations of (
4.12
) show that the square (3) commutes.
The squares (4) and (5) commute because the localization sequence in
G
-theory
commutes
with the flat pull-back and proper push-forward. Note that all horizontal arrows
in (3), (4) and (6) are flat pull-back maps (because
\Sigma
and
S
are spectra of
products of fields). The square (6) commutes because the flat pull-back
commutes with the proper push-forward in
G
-theory induced by
a Cartesian square of schemes (see
[
80
, Prop. 2.11]
). The square (7) clearly
commutes because all its arrows are the proper push-forward maps in
G
-theory.
The lone triangle clearly commutes. The horizontal arrows in (1) are surjective by
Lemma
4.1
.

It follows from the commutativity of the square (6) that

f^{*}_{S}\circ\nu_{*}(\partial(\alpha))=\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\widetilde{g}^{*}(\partial(\alpha))
.
On the other hand, the commutativity of other squares in (
4.13
) shows
that
\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\widetilde{g}^{*}(\partial(\alpha))=\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial\circ\widetilde{g}^{*}(\alpha)=\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial(\sum_{i}\pi_{i*}(\ell({W^{\prime}_{i}})g^{\prime*}_{i}(\alpha)))
.
Letting
\beta_{i}=\ell({W^{\prime}_{i}})g^{\prime*}_{i}(\alpha)\in K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Y}_{i},T_{i}},I_{i})\subset K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{C}^{\prime}_{i}))
, we conclude that

(4.14)

f^{*}_{S}\circ\nu_{*}(\partial(\alpha))=\sum_{i}\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial\circ\pi_{i*}(\beta_{i}).

Hence, we get

\begin{array}[]{lll}\tau^{-1}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{S}\circ\nu_{*}(\partial(\alpha))&=&\sum_{i}\tau^{-1}_{*}\circ\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial\circ\pi_{i*}(\beta_{i})\\
&=&\sum_{i}\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\tau^{\prime-1}_{*}\circ\partial\circ\pi_{i*}(\beta_{i})\\
&=&\sum_{i}\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial\circ\pi^{-1}_{*}\circ\pi_{i*}(\beta_{i})\\
&=&\sum_{i}\nu^{\prime}_{*}\circ\partial(\beta_{i})\\
&=&\sum_{i}(\nu^{\prime}_{i})_{*}\circ\partial(\beta_{i}).\end{array}

Since
\beta_{i}\in{\rm fil}_{\Delta((C^{\prime}_{i})_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{i}))\subset F^{1}_{2}(X^{\prime})
and

(\nu^{\prime}_{i})_{*}\circ\partial(\beta_{i})
lies in the image of the map

\partial^{1}_{X^{\prime}}\colon F^{1}_{2}(X^{\prime})\to F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})
for every
1\leq i\leq r
, we are done.

To show that
f^{*}=f^{\prime*}_{i}\circ f^{*}_{i}
for each
i
, one easily reduces to checking it
on
K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
for a closed point
x\in X
. In the latter case, we have to only
check that for a fixed
i\geq 1
, a closed point
x_{i}\in f^{-1}_{i}(x)
and
a closed point
x^{\prime}\in f^{\prime-1}_{i}(x_{i})
, one has
\ell(S_{x}(x^{\prime}))=\ell(W_{x}(x_{i}))\ell(T_{x_{i}}(x^{\prime}))
, where
S_{x}(x^{\prime})
is the factor of the scheme theoretic
inverse image
S_{x}
of
{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
in
X^{\prime}
whose support is
\{x^{\prime}\}
,

W_{x}(x_{i})
is the factor of the scheme theoretic
inverse image
W_{x}
of
{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
in
X_{i}
whose support is
\{x_{i}\}
and

T_{x_{i}}(x^{\prime})
is the factor of the scheme theoretic
inverse image
T_{x_{i}}
of
{\rm Spec\,}(k_{i}(x_{i}))
in
X^{\prime}
whose support is
\{x^{\prime}\}
.
But this follows from Lemma
4.16
.

To show that
f^{*}_{i}
is continuous when
k
is a local field, we can verbatim repeat
the argument for the continuity of
(f_{i})_{*}
to reduce it to showing that if

{F^{\prime}}/F
is a finite extension a local fields, then
the map
K^{M}_{1}(F)\to K^{M}_{1}(F^{\prime})
, given by
a\mapsto a^{m}
, is continuous with
respect to the canonical topologies for every integer
m\geq 1
. But this is obvious
and easily follows by the compatibility of the valuation maps for the two fields.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
A_{1}\to A_{2}
be a flat homomorphism of
Artinian local algebras over a field such that
J_{1}A_{2}\subset J_{2}
, where
J_{i}

is the maximal ideal of
A_{i}
. Let
A^{\prime}_{2}={A_{2}}/{J_{1}A_{2}}
. Then

\ell(A_{2})=\ell(A_{1})\ell(A^{\prime}_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
k_{i}
be the residue field of
A_{i}
for
i=1,2
. We let
I_{2}=J_{1}A_{2}
.
By the Cohen structure theorem, each
A_{i}
is a finite-dimensional

k_{i}
-algebra such that the composite
k_{i}\hookrightarrow A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow k_{i}
is identity.
In this case, one checks that
\ell(A_{i})=\text{\rm dim}_{k_{i}}(A_{i})
(e.g., see

[
29
, Ex. A.1.1]
)

Since
A_{1}\to A_{2}
is a flat extension, it follows that
J^{n}_{1}\otimes_{A_{1}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\cong}I^{n}_{2}
for every
n\geq 0
. In particular, we get

{J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{A_{1}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\cong}{I^{n}_{2}}/{I^{n+1}_{2}}
.
This yields

{I^{n}_{2}}/{I^{n+1}_{2}}\cong{J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{A_{1}}A_{2}\cong({J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{k_{1}}k_{1})\otimes_{A_{1}}A_{2}\cong{J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{k_{1}}A^{\prime}_{2}.

We let
M_{n}={J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{k_{1}}k_{2}
for
n\geq 0
. We then get

\begin{array}[]{lll}\ell(A_{2})\ {=}^{1}\ \ell(I_{2})+\ell(A^{\prime}_{2})&=&{\underset{n\geq 1}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}({I^{n}_{2}}/{I^{n+1}_{2}})+\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&{\underset{n\geq 1}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}({J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{k_{1}}A^{\prime}_{2})+\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&{\underset{n\geq 0}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}({J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{k_{1}}A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&{\underset{n\geq 0}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(({J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}}\otimes_{k_{1}}k_{2})\otimes_{k_{2}}A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&{\underset{n\geq 0}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(M_{n}\otimes_{k_{2}}A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&\left({\underset{n\geq 0}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(M_{n})\right)\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&\left({\underset{n\geq 0}{\sum}}\text{\rm dim}_{k_{1}}({J^{n}_{1}}/{J^{n+1}_{1}})\right)\text{\rm dim}_{k_{2}}(A^{\prime}_{2})\\
&=&\ell(A_{1})\ell(A^{\prime}_{2}),\end{array}

where the identity
{=}^{1}
follows from
[
29
, Lem. A.1.1]
.
This proves the lemma.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

If one assumes in Proposition
4.15
that
\overline{X}
is smooth over
k
(which
is the assumption for the main results of this paper), then
its proof can be much simplified using that
f
becomes bijective in this case.

In proving Proposition
4.15
, we have in fact shown the following.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a finite and flat map between normal
k
-schemes.
Then the map
f^{*}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X)\to F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})
as defined in (
4.12
)
descends to a homomorphism

f^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime}).

Corollary 4.19
.

Let
X
be an integral smooth
k
-scheme and let
f\colon X^{\prime}:=X_{k^{\prime}}\to X
be the induced morphism where
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a finite field
extension. Then there exists a pull-back map
f^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime})
such
that
f_{*}\circ f^{*}
is multiplication by
[k^{\prime}:k]
on
C^{\rm t}(X)
.

Proof.

The existence of
f^{*}
follows from Proposition
4.18
because
f
is
necessarily flat.
To prove the other assertion of
the corollary, we let
x\in X_{(0)}
and let
S_{x}={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\times_{X}X^{\prime}
.
Then one knows that the composite map
K_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{f^{*}}G_{1}(S_{x})\xrightarrow{f_{*}}K_{1}(k(x))
is multiplication by
[f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{S_{x}})]\in K_{0}(k(x))\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
. But this is clearly multiplication by

\text{\rm dim}_{k(x)}(S_{x})=[k^{\prime}:k]
. This implies the desired assertion.
∎

Corollary 4.20
.

Let
X
be a smooth and integral
k
-scheme where
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
.
Let
{k^{\prime}}/k
be a purely inseparable algebraic extension of fields and let

X^{\prime}=X_{k^{\prime}}
. Then

f^{*}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X)\to F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})
as defined in (
4.12
)
descends to a homomorphism

f^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime}).

Proof.

We can write
k^{\prime}
as a union of
k_{i}:=k^{1/{p^{i}}}
.
We choose an integral normal compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
and let

u\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion. We now apply Proposition
4.15

and note that
X_{i}=X_{k_{i}}
for every
i\geq 0
. Similarly,

X^{\prime}=X_{k^{\prime}}
because of the smoothness of
X
over
k
.
The latter also implies that each
f_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i}
is finite and
flat, as we observed in the proof of Corollary
4.19
.
∎

4.5.
Continuity of tame class group

Let
k
be an imperfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
k^{\heartsuit}
denote a fixed
perfect closure of
k
. For
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, we let

X^{\heartsuit}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\heartsuit})
. Note that

\pi\colon X^{\heartsuit}\xrightarrow{\cong}\lim X_{\bullet}
,
where the pro-scheme
X_{\bullet}=\{X_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
is defined by taking

k_{i}=k^{1/{p^{i}}}
and
X_{i}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k_{i})
.
For every
j\geq i
, the projection map
f_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i}

is a finite flat radicial morphism of
degree
p^{j-i}
. We let
g_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X,\ f_{i}\colon X^{\heartsuit}\to X_{i}
and

f\colon X^{\heartsuit}\to X
be the projection maps. If
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
is integral, then

X_{i}
is an integral smooth
k_{i}
-scheme for each
i
and
X^{\heartsuit}
is an
integral smooth
k^{\heartsuit}
-scheme. In particular, we have a homomorphism

{\underset{i\geq 0}{\varinjlim}}\ C^{\rm t}(X_{i})\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})

by Corollary
4.20
. We denote this by
\pi^{*}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Assume that
X
is a smooth and integral
k
-scheme.
Then the pull-back map

\pi^{*}\colon\ {\underset{i\geq 0}{\varinjlim}}\ C^{\rm t}(X_{i})\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose that
y\in X^{\heartsuit}_{(0)}
and
y_{j}=f_{j}(y)
for any
j\geq 0
.
Then there will exist an integer
j\gg 0
and a closed subscheme
Y_{j}\subset X_{j}

such that
Y_{j}\times_{X_{j}}X^{\heartsuit}={\rm Spec\,}(k(y))
. The support of such
Y_{j}
must
be the closed point
y_{j}
of
X_{j}
.
Furthermore, the fppf descent implies that
Y_{j}
must be reduced.
In other words,
Y_{j}={\rm Spec\,}(k(y_{j}))
. The same argument implies that

{\rm Spec\,}(k(y_{j}))\times_{X_{j}}X_{l}={\rm Spec\,}(k(y_{l}))
and

{\rm Spec\,}(k(y_{j}))\times_{X_{j}}X^{\heartsuit}={\rm Spec\,}(k(y))
for every
l\geq j
. It follows from this and the definition of

f^{*}_{l}
(see §
4.4
)
that
f^{*}_{l}(a_{l})=a_{l}
for every
l\geq j
and
a_{l}\in K^{M}_{1}(k(y_{l}))
.
Since Milnor
K
-theory is a continuous functor on fields, it follows that
every
a\in K^{M}_{1}(k(y))
has the property that there exists
l\geq j
and

a_{l}\in K^{M}_{1}(k(y_{l}))
such that
f^{*}_{l}(a_{l})=a
. This shows that
\pi^{*}
is
surjective.

To prove that
\pi^{*}
is injective, we suppose that there exists
\alpha\in\varinjlim_{j}\ C^{\rm t}(X_{j})
such that
\pi^{*}(\alpha)=0
.
We can then find an integer
j\geq 0
, a 0-dimensional reduced closed subscheme

S_{j}\subset X_{j}
such that
\alpha
is represented by an element of
K_{1}(S_{j})
.
We let
S^{\prime}
be the set theoretic inverse image of
S_{j}
in
X^{\heartsuit}

(with the reduced closed subscheme structure) so that

f^{*}_{j}(\alpha)\in K_{1}(S^{\prime})
. As explained in the proof of surjectivity of

\pi^{*}
, we can find
l\gg j
and a reduced closed subscheme
S_{l}\subset X_{l}
such
that
S_{l}\times_{X_{l}}X^{\heartsuit}=S^{\prime}
. We can replace
\alpha
by

f^{*}_{jl}(\alpha)\in K_{1}(S_{l})
(note that
S^{\prime}\to S_{l}\to S_{j}
are bijections). We
can also replace
k
by any
k_{l}
and
\alpha
by
f^{*}_{jl}(\alpha)

with
l\gg j
. We can thus assume that there
exists a 0-dimensional reduced closed subscheme

S_{0}\subset X
such that
\alpha
is represented by an element of
K_{1}(S_{0})
.
Furthermore,
S^{\prime}=S_{0}\times_{X}X^{\heartsuit}
. In particular, if we let

S_{j}=f_{j}(S^{\prime})
, then
S_{j}=S_{0}\times_{X}X_{j}
and

S^{\prime}=S_{j}\times_{X_{j}}X^{\heartsuit}
for every
j\geq 0
.

We fix an integral compactification
u\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
and
let
u^{\prime}\colon X^{\heartsuit}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}
be the induced open immersion.
Since
X^{\heartsuit}\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k^{\prime}}
, we see that
u^{\prime}
has factorization

X^{\heartsuit}\hookrightarrow(\overline{X}^{\heartsuit})_{\rm red}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}
. We let
\overline{X}_{j}:=\overline{X}_{k_{j}}
.
Since
f^{*}(\alpha)
lies in the image of
\partial^{1}_{X^{\heartsuit}}\colon F^{1}_{2}(X^{\heartsuit})\to F^{0}_{1}(X^{\heartsuit})
, we can find integral curves

C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}\subset X^{\heartsuit}
satisfying the following.
Let
\overline{C}_{i}
be the closure of
C_{i}
in
\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}
with the reduced (hence integral)
closed subscheme structure and let

\nu^{\prime}_{i}\colon\overline{Y}_{i}:=(\overline{C}_{i})_{n}\to\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}
be the canonical map.
Let
T^{\prime}_{i}=(\nu^{\prime}_{i})^{-1}(\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}\setminus X^{\heartsuit})
and

A^{\prime}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Y}_{i},T^{\prime}_{i}}
. Let
J^{\prime}_{i}
be the Jacobson radical of

A^{\prime}_{i}
. Then, there exist elements
\beta^{\prime}_{i}\in K^{M}_{2}(A^{\prime}_{i},J^{\prime}_{i})\subset K^{M}_{2}(k^{\prime}(C_{i}))
(in view of Lemma
4.5
) such that

f^{*}(\alpha)=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\partial(\beta^{\prime}_{i})\in K_{1}(S^{\prime})
.

We can now find an integer
j\gg 0
and closed subschemes
\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}\subset\overline{X}_{j}

such that

(4.15)

\overline{C}_{i}\cong\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}\times_{\overline{X}_{j}}\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}\ \forall\ 1\leq i\leq r.

In particular,
\overline{C}_{i}\to\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}
is faithfully flat.
By the fppf descent, each
\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}
must be an integral
curve in
\overline{X}_{j}
. By the same argument, it follows that

\overline{Z}^{l}_{i}:=\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}\times_{\overline{X}_{j}}\overline{X}_{l}
is an integral curve in
\overline{X}_{l}

for every
l\geq j
and
1\leq i\leq r
. Moreover,

\overline{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{l\geq j}{\varprojlim}}\ \overline{Z}^{l}_{i}
.
We let
Z^{l}_{i}=\overline{Z}^{l}_{i}\bigcap X_{l}
. Since
\overline{Z}^{l}_{i}\subset(\overline{X}_{l})_{\rm red}\subset((\overline{X}_{j})_{\rm red})_{l}

for each
l
and
i
and similarly
\overline{C}_{i}\subset(\overline{X}^{\heartsuit})_{\rm red}\subset((\overline{X}_{j})_{\rm red})^{\heartsuit}
,
we can pass from
\overline{X}
to
(\overline{X}_{j})_{\rm red}
which allows us to assume that

j=0
. Note that
(\overline{X}_{j})_{\rm red}
is an integral compactification of
X_{j}

for every
j\geq 0
.

Let
\overline{Y}^{j}_{i}
(resp.
Y^{j}_{i}
) be the normalization of
\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}
(resp.
Z^{j}_{i}
)
and let
\nu^{j}_{i}\colon\overline{Y}^{j}_{i}\to\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}
be the normalization maps.
Since the normalization is a functor in the category of integral schemes with dominant
morphisms and this functor commutes with projective limits of integral schemes with
affine transition morphisms, it follows that there is a commutative diagram

(4.16)

\textstyle{\overline{Y}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}^{l}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{jl}}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{l}_{i}}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}^{j}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{j}_{i}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{l}}
\textstyle{\overline{Z}^{l}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{jl}}
\textstyle{\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}}

for every
l\geq j\geq 0
and
\overline{Y}_{i}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{j\geq 0}{\varprojlim}}\ \overline{Y}^{j}_{i}
.
If we let
T^{j}_{i}=(\nu^{j}_{i})^{-1}(\overline{Z}^{j}_{i}\setminus Z^{j}_{i})=(\nu^{j}_{i})^{-1}(\overline{X}_{j}\setminus X_{j})
and
A^{j}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Y}^{j}_{i},T^{j}_{i}}
, then we
moreover have

T^{l}_{j}=(\phi_{jl})^{-1}(T^{j}_{i})
and
T^{\prime}_{i}=(\phi_{j})^{-1}(T^{j}_{i})
for every

l\geq j\geq 0
. This implies that
{\underset{j\geq 0}{\varinjlim}}\ A^{j}_{i}\xrightarrow{\cong}A^{\prime}_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
.

Since the transition maps of
\{A^{j}_{i}\}_{j\geq 0}
are faithfully flat and
J^{\prime}_{i}
is
finitely generated, we can find

j\gg 0
such that
J^{\prime}_{i}=J^{j}_{i}A^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i
. In particular,

J^{\prime}_{i}=\varinjlim_{j}J^{j}_{i}
.
By the continuity of relative Milnor
K
-theory, it follows
that
K^{M}_{2}(A^{\prime}_{i},J^{\prime}_{i})=\varinjlim_{j}K^{M}_{2}({A}^{j}_{i},{J}^{j}_{i})
for every
i
.
By passing from
X
to
X_{j}
with
j\gg 0
, we can therefore assume that
there exist

(4.17)

\beta^{0}_{i}\in K^{M}_{2}(A^{0}_{i},J^{0}_{i})\subset K^{M}_{2}(k(Z^{0}_{i}))\ \mbox{such \ that}\ \beta^{\prime}_{i}=f^{*}(\beta^{0}_{i})\ \forall\ i.

We now observe that the map
\phi_{0}\colon C_{i}\to Z^{0}_{i}
is flat by
(
4.15
). Hence, for every open subscheme
U\subset Z^{0}_{i}
and

U^{\prime}=(\phi_{0})^{-1}(U)
, there is a commutative diagram of Quillen
K
-theory
localization sequences

\textstyle{K_{2}(Z^{0}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}_{0}}
\textstyle{K_{2}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}_{0}}
\textstyle{K_{1}(Z^{0}_{i}\setminus U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}_{0}}
\textstyle{K_{2}(C_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{2}(U^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K_{1}(C_{i}\setminus U^{\prime}),}

where the vertical arrows are flat pull-back maps.
Taking the limit over
U
and noting that
{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime}(C_{i}))=C_{i}\times_{Z^{0}_{i}}{\rm Spec\,}(k(Z^{0}_{i}))
, we get a commutative diagram

(4.18)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(Z^{0}_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}_{0}}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(Z^{0}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}_{0}}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k^{\prime}(C_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(C_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X^{\heartsuit}),}

where we have identified the Milnor
K_{i}
and their boundary maps for fields with
Quillen
K_{i}
and their boundary maps for
i\leq 2
.
We conclude from this that

f^{*}\left(\alpha-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}(\partial(\beta^{0}_{i}))\right)=\phi^{*}_{0}\left(\alpha-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}(\partial(\beta^{0}_{i}))\right)=0\ \mbox{in}\ K_{1}(S^{\prime}).

Using the continuity property of
K_{1}(-)
again, it follows that

g^{*}_{j}(\alpha-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}(\partial(\beta^{0}_{i})))=\phi^{*}_{0j}((\alpha-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}(\partial(\beta^{0}_{i})))=0
in
K_{1}(S_{j})
for all
j\gg 0
.
Equivalently,
g^{*}_{j}(\alpha)=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}g^{*}_{j}(\partial(\beta^{0}_{i}))
in
F^{0}_{1}(X_{j})
for all
j\gg 0
.
Since we have shown in (
4.17
) that

\beta^{0}_{i}\in K^{M}_{2}(A^{0}_{i},J^{0}_{i})\subset K^{M}_{2}(k(Z^{0}_{i}))
for every
i
, it follows from
Proposition
4.15
that
g^{*}_{j}(\alpha)
dies in
C^{\rm t}(X_{j})
.
We have thus shown that
\pi^{*}
is injective.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

If
k
is a local field of characteristic
p>0
, then Lemma
4.21

allows one to endow
C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})
with the direct limit topology. It is then clear
that the map
f^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})
is continuous. Henceforth, we shall
always assume
C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})
to be a topological abelian group in this manner whenever

k
is a local field.

Corollary 4.23
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of Lemma
4.21
. Then the pull-back
map

\pi^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})[\tfrac{1}{p}]

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
4.21
, it suffices to show that if
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a finite
purely inseparable extension and
X^{\prime}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
, then
the pull-back map
\pi^{*}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime})[\tfrac{1}{p}]

is an isomorphism if
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is the projection.
In this case, the surjectivity follows from Lemma
4.24
and the
injectivity from Corollary
4.19
.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
{k^{\prime}}/k
be a purely inseparable field extension and let
m
be an integer prime to

p
. Then the pull-back maps

\pi^{*}\colon{K^{M}_{n}(k)}[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to{K^{M}_{n}(k^{\prime})}[\tfrac{1}{p}]\ \mbox{and}\ \pi^{*}\colon{K^{M}_{n}(k)}/m\to{K^{M}_{n}(k^{\prime})}/m

are isomorphisms for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

By a limit argument, we can easily reduce to the case when
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a
finite extension.
In this case, one knows that the composite map

{K^{M}_{n}(k)}\to{K^{M}_{n}(k^{\prime})}\xrightarrow{N_{{k^{\prime}}/k}}{K^{M}_{n}(k)}
is
multiplication by
p^{r}
for some
r\geq 0
by
[
7
, § 5.4]
.
This implies that
\pi^{*}
are injective. For surjectivity,
we can use the surjection
K^{M}_{1}(k^{\prime})\otimes\cdots\otimes K^{M}_{1}(k^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow K^{M}_{n}(k^{\prime})

(where the tensor product is taken
n
times) to reduce to showing the surjectivity
for
n=1
. On the other hand, it is easy to see that
{K^{M}_{1}(k^{\prime})}/{K^{M}_{1}(k)}
is
annihilated by
p^{r}
for some
r\geq 0
. In particular, it is uniquely
m
-divisible.
∎

5.
The idele class group

In this section, we shall define the idele class group of integral normal schemes
following Wiesend’s approach
and present the tame class group as a canonical quotient of the idele class group.
We fix a local field
k
throughout this section. Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be an
integral normal
k
-scheme.

5.1.
Wiesend style idele class groups

For
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
and
\overline{C}_{n}
the unique compactification of
C_{n}
,
we let
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})={\underset{x\in\Delta(C_{n})}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})
,
where recall that
k(C_{n})_{x}
is the completion of
k(C_{n})
with respect to the
discrete valuation defined by
x
. We let
U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})={\underset{x\in\Delta(C_{n})}{\bigoplus}}U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})
(see §
3.3
).
We make the following definitions.

Definition 5.1
.

(1)

We let

I(X)=\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})\right)\bigoplus\left({\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\right).

(2)

If
\overline{X}
is a complete integral normal
k
-scheme with an effective Cartier
divisor
D
such that
X=\overline{X}\setminus D
, we let

I(\overline{X},D)=\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}{{\rm fil}_{D}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}\right)\bigoplus\left({\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\right),

where
{\rm fil}_{D}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})={\underset{x\in\Delta(C_{n})}{\bigoplus}}K_{2}^{M}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{{C},x}},\widehat{I_{D,x}})
.

(3)

We let

I^{{\rm t},W}(X)=\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}\right)\bigoplus\left({\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\right).

We shall call
I(X)
the ‘idele group’ of
X
. We endow
I(X)
with the structure of a
topological abelian group with the direct sum topology, where each
K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
is
endowed with its canonical topology and each
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{y})
is endowed with
Kato’s topology if
{\rm char}(k)>0
and discrete topology if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.
We call
I(\overline{X},D)
the ‘idele group with modulus’ of
(\overline{X},D)

and
I^{{\rm t},W}(X)
the ‘tame idele group’ of
X
. These are endowed with the quotient
topologies.

For
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, the product of inclusions into the completions along the points of

\Delta(C_{n})
induces
\iota_{C}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\to K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})
.
We also have the boundary map

\partial_{C}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\to F^{0}_{1}(C)
(see §
4.2
).
We thus get a homomorphism
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\xrightarrow{(\iota_{C},\partial_{C})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})\bigoplus F^{0}_{1}(C)\hookrightarrow K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})\bigoplus F^{0}_{1}(X)
.
We let
F^{1,W}_{2}(X)={\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
.

Definition 5.2
.

The idele class group of
X
is defined to be the cokernel of the homomorphism

\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}(\iota_{C},\partial_{C})\colon F^{1,W}_{2}(X)\to I(X).

It is a topological abelian group with respect to the quotient topology.
We denote this group by
C(X)
.
We let

C(\overline{X},D)={\rm Coker}\left(F^{1,W}_{2}(X)\to I(\overline{X},D)\right)=\frac{C(X)}{{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{{\rm fil}_{D}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}}

and

C^{{\rm t},W}(X)={\rm Coker}\left(F^{1,W}_{2}(X)\to I^{{\rm t},W}(X)\right)=\frac{C(X)}{{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}}.

These are topological abelian groups endowed with the quotient topologies.
There are canonical continuous surjections

(5.1)

C(X)\twoheadrightarrow C(\overline{X},D)\twoheadrightarrow C^{{\rm t},W}(X).

These groups are the local field analogues of the various idele class groups of
varieties over finite fields due to Wiesend (see
[
61
, § 7]
)

5.2.
Covariance of idele class group

We keep the above notations.
We have the following covariant functoriality property of the idele class group
for a proper morphism between integral normal schemes.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a proper morphism between integral normal
k
-schemes. Then
there is a push-forward homomorphism

f_{*}\colon C(X^{\prime})\to C(X).

Proof.

For any
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{(0)}
with
x=f(x^{\prime})
, there is a norm map

N_{{k(x^{\prime})}/{k(x)}}\colon K^{M}_{1}(k(x^{\prime}))\to K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
.
Taking the sum, we get a push-forward map
f_{*}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})\to F^{0}_{1}(X)
,
which is clearly continuous. We now let
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}(X^{\prime})
. If
f(C^{\prime})=x\in X_{(0)}
,
we let
f_{*}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n})_{\infty})\xrightarrow{\partial}{\underset{x^{\prime}\in\Delta(C^{\prime}_{n})}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{\sum_{x^{\prime}}N_{{k(x^{\prime})}/{k(x)}}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
be the
composition of the boundary and the norm maps.
If
f(C^{\prime})=C
is an integral curve, then the map
f\colon C^{\prime}\to C
must be finite.
In particular, it induces a Cartesian square

(5.2)

\textstyle{C^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\overline{C}^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{C}_{n},}

where the vertical arrows are finite flat and the horizontal arrows are open immersions.
Since
f^{-1}(\Delta(C_{n}))=\Delta(C^{\prime}_{n})
, we get the norm map

N_{{C^{\prime}}/C}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n})_{\infty})\to K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})
.
We have thus defined a homomorphism
f_{*}\colon I(X^{\prime})\to I(X)
.

To show that
f_{*}
descends to the level of the idele class groups, let

C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}(X^{\prime})
. To show that the composition

(5.3)

K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n}))\to K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n})_{\infty})\bigoplus F^{0}_{1}(C^{\prime}_{n})\to K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n})_{\infty})\bigoplus F^{0}_{1}(C^{\prime})\to C(X)

is zero, we can replace
X^{\prime}
by
C^{\prime}_{n}
. We can therefore assume that
X^{\prime}
is
an integral normal curve over
k
.

Suppose first that
f(X^{\prime})=x\in X_{(0)}
so that there is a
factorization
X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X
of
f
. Since the
composition
K^{M}_{2}(k(X^{\prime}))\to K^{M}_{2}(k(X^{\prime})_{\infty})\bigoplus F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}_{*}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
is zero by Suslin’s reciprocity law
[
94
]
,
it follows that
f_{*}
descends to a homomorphism
f_{*}\colon C(X^{\prime})\to K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\to C(X)
.

We now assume that
f(X^{\prime})
is an integral curve
C
. Then
f\colon X^{\prime}\to C

is a finite map. In particular, we have a Cartesian square such as
(
5.2
) (where
C^{\prime}=X^{\prime}
). In order to show that the
composite map in (
5.3
) is zero, we can replace
X
by
C_{n}
.
We can therefore assume that
f:X^{\prime}\to X
is a finite morphism between
integral normal curves over
k
.

We now consider the diagrams

(5.4)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{X}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{X}^{\prime})_{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{X}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{X})_{\infty})}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k(\overline{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X),}

where the vertical arrows are the norm maps, left horizontal arrows are induced by
completions and the right horizontal arrows are the residue maps.
To finish the proof of the lemma, it remains to show that the two squares commute.

Now, the right square commutes by
[
39
, Lem. 3.4]
.
To show that the left square commutes, we can replace Milnor
K_{2}
by the
Quillen
K_{2}
-functor on fields and replace the norm map on Milnor
K
-theory by the
transfer map on Quillen
K
-theory by
[
52
, § 2.4, Cor. 2]
.
We now let
x\in\Delta(X)
and
S=f^{-1}(\{x\})\subset\Delta(X^{\prime})
.
Let
A={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X},x}
and
A^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X}^{\prime},S}
. Then
A\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}
is a finite map
of semilocal Dedekind domains.
Let
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) denote the Jacobson radical of

A
(resp.
A^{\prime}
). Let
\widehat{A}
(resp.
\widehat{A^{\prime}}
) denote the
J
-adic (resp.

J^{\prime}
-adic) completion of
A
(resp.
A^{\prime}
).
Since
\sqrt{JA^{\prime}}=J^{\prime}
, we see that
\widehat{A^{\prime}}
coincides with the
JA^{\prime}
-adic
completion of
A^{\prime}
. Since the latter is isomorphic to
\widehat{A}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
, we
conclude that
\widehat{A^{\prime}}\cong\widehat{A}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
. In particular,

\widehat{A}\to\widehat{A^{\prime}}
is a finite map of regular semilocal rings of dimension one.

Let
F
(resp.
F^{\prime}
) denote the quotient field of
A
(resp. quotient field of

A^{\prime}
). We let
\widehat{F}

(resp.
\widehat{F^{\prime}}
) denote the quotient field of
\widehat{A}
(resp. ring of total quotients
of
\widehat{A^{\prime}}
). We then get

\widehat{F}\otimes_{F}F^{\prime}=\widehat{F}\otimes_{F}F\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}\cong\widehat{F}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}\cong\widehat{F}\otimes_{\widehat{A}}\widehat{A}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}\cong\widehat{F}\otimes_{\widehat{A}}\widehat{A^{\prime}}\cong\widehat{F^{\prime}}.

This shows that

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{F^{\prime}}}

is a co-Cartesian square of fields, where the vertical inclusions are finite.
It follows from
[
80
, § 7, Prop. 2.11]
that the induced square of Quillen
K
-theory, where the vertical
arrows are finite push-forward and horizontal arrows are flat pull-back, commutes.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The properness assumption in Proposition
5.3
can be removed. In order to
do this, one needs to consider one more case while defining
f_{*}
on generators.
Without properness, it may happen that we have a dominant morphism of curves

f\colon C^{\prime}\to C
and a
point
x^{\prime}\in\Delta(C^{\prime}_{n})
such that
f(x^{\prime})=x\notin\Delta(C_{n})
. In this case,
we define
f_{*}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(C^{\prime}_{n})_{x^{\prime}})\to K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
as the composition of the
boundary and the norm maps. With this modification, one can show following the
above proof that
f_{*}
descends to the level of the idele class groups. We leave
it as an exercise.

Remark 5.5
.

We do not know if the norm map between Milnor
K_{2}
of discrete valuation fields is
continuous with respect to Kato’s topology. If it were, it would follow that
the map
f_{*}
in Proposition
5.3
is continuous.

5.3.
Relation between
C^{\rm t}(X)
and
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)

We now compare the two notions of idele class groups with modulus and the tame class
groups we defined above.
Let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be an open immersion into a complete integral normal

k
-scheme such that the complementary closed subset
is the support of an effective Cartier divisor
D
.
It is easy to check that the identify map of
F^{0}_{1}(X)
induces canonical continuous
homomorphisms

(5.5)

\delta_{\overline{X}|D}\colon SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\to C(\overline{X},D);\ \ \ \delta_{X}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{{\rm t},W}(X).

Lemma 5.6
.

The maps
\delta_{\overline{X}|D}
and
\delta_{X}
are isomorphisms of abelian groups.
These are isomorphisms of topological abelian groups if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.

Proof.

We shall prove that
\delta_{X}
is an isomorphism as the proof for
\delta_{\overline{X}|D}

is identical modulo some obvious modifications. We first show that
\delta_{X}
is
injective.
Suppose that
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
are distinct curves
and
\alpha_{i}\in K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{i}))
such that

\sum_{i}\iota_{C_{i}}(\alpha_{i})+\sum_{i}\partial_{C_{i}}(\alpha_{i})=\beta\in F^{0}_{1}(X)
.
Then we must have
\iota_{C_{i}}(\alpha_{i})\in U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k((C_{i})_{n})_{\infty})\subset K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{i})_{\infty})
for every
i
.
Using Lemma
4.4
and Corollary
4.3
, this implies that

\alpha_{i}\in{\rm fil}_{\Delta((C_{i})_{n})}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{i}))
for every
i
.
It follows from this that
\alpha:=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\in F^{1}_{2}(X)
and

\partial^{1}_{X}(\alpha)=\beta
. We have thus shown that
\delta_{X}

is injective.

To show that
\delta_{X}
is surjective, it suffices to show that the natural map

K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\to\frac{K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})}
is surjective for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
. But this is a direct consequence of the weak approximation theorem for
Milnor
K
-theory
[
58
, Lem. 6.3]
once we note that for all
x\in\Delta(C_{n})
, the subgroup
U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})\subset K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})

is open in latter’s canonical topology.

Finally to show that
\delta_{X}
is an isomorphism of topological abelian groups if

{\rm char}(k)=0
, it suffices to show that
\delta_{X}
is open in this case.
To show the latter, note that the source and the target of
\delta_{X}
have quotient
topologies. We now recall an easy to prove general fact that if

f\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H
is a
surjective homomorphism of topological groups (with
H
having the quotient topology),
then
f
is an open map. Using this, it suffices to show that
the inclusion of
F^{0}_{1}(X)
in
I^{{\rm t},W}(X)
is an open mapping. But this is clear
because the other summand of
I^{{\rm t},W}(X)
has discrete topology.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Recall that in the definition of the topology on
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
, we have used Kato’s
topology on each
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})
(where
x\in\Delta(C_{n})
).
However, the latter group also has another topology,
namely, the canonical topology which is generated by the open subgroups

\{U^{\prime}_{m}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})\}_{m\geq 0}
. Using the direct sum of the canonical topologies
of
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})
, we can endow
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
with another topology. Let us
call it the ‘canonical topology’. We can define the canonical
topology on
C(\overline{X},D)
in a similar manner. The
proof of Lemma
5.6
then shows that
\delta_{X}
is an isomorphism
of topological abelian groups in any characteristic if we endow
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
with its
canonical topology. The same is true for
\delta_{\overline{X}|D}
too.

5.4.
Relation between
C^{\rm t}(X)
and
C(X)

The following result provides a comparison between the idele and tame class groups of
normal schemes. This is the analogue of the first isomorphism of
Corollary
3.11
for class groups.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be an integral normal scheme. Let

m\in k^{\times}
be a positive integer. Then the canonical surjection

\kappa_{X}\colon C(X)\twoheadrightarrow C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
induces
an isomorphism of topological abelian groups

\kappa_{X}\colon{C(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}{C^{{\rm t},W}(X)}/m.

In particular, there is a canonical isomorphism of abelian groups

{C(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
5.6
, we only need to prove the first part of
the lemma.
Now, we note that
{C^{{\rm t},W}(X)}/m
is a topological quotient of
{C(X)}/m
.
This implies that
\kappa_{X}
is a continuous open map
(see the proof of Lemma
5.6
). Hence,
\kappa_{X}
will be
an isomorphism of topological abelian groups with
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
-coefficients if it is
bijective.

Now, we see from Definition
5.2
that there is an exact sequence

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})\to C(X)\to C^{{\rm t},W}(X)\to 0.

We can therefore apply Lemma
5.9
to conclude the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
A
be a Henselian local Noetherian ring containing an infinite field and let

{\mathfrak{m}}
be the maximal ideal of
A
. Let
I\subset{\mathfrak{m}}
be an
{\mathfrak{m}}
-primary ideal
and let
m
be an integer prime to the residue characteristic of
A
. Then

{K^{M}_{n}(A,I)}/m=0
for all integers
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

We can assume
n\geq 1
because the lemma is trivial otherwise.
We have a short exact sequence

(5.6)

0\to K^{M}_{n}(A,I)\to K^{M}_{n}(A,{\mathfrak{m}})\to K^{M}_{n}(A/I,{\mathfrak{m}}/I)\to 0.

Since
{\mathfrak{m}}=\sqrt{I}
, it follows from
[
40
, Lem. 2.6]
that

K^{M}_{n}(A/I,{\mathfrak{m}}/I)
is uniquely
m
-divisible if the residue characteristic of

A
is positive. The same also holds if the residue characteristic of

A
is zero as a consequence of
[
36
, Theorem 2.12]
. Indeed, since
A/I
is
{\mathfrak{m}}
-adic complete local ring
with residue
field
A/{\mathfrak{m}}
, the quotient map
A/I\to A/{\mathfrak{m}}
admits a splitting by Cohen structure theorem. Since a local ring
containing an infinite field is weakly
n
-stable for all
n\geq 1
, we can now apply
[
36
, Theorem 2.12]
to
conclude that

K^{M}_{n}(A/I,{\mathfrak{m}}/I)
is uniquely
m
-divisible.
We can therefore assume that

I={\mathfrak{m}}
.

Now, there is a surjection

K^{M}_{1}(A,{\mathfrak{m}})\otimes(A^{\times}\otimes\cdots\otimes A^{\times})\twoheadrightarrow K^{M}_{n}(A,{\mathfrak{m}})

by
[
55
, Lem. 1.3.1]
, where the tensor product of
A^{\times}
is taken

(n-1)
times. It suffices therefore to show that
{K^{M}_{1}(A,{\mathfrak{m}})}/m=0
.
Since
K^{M}_{1}(A,{\mathfrak{m}})\cong(1+{\mathfrak{m}})\subset A^{\times}
, the desired statement follows
easily from Hensel’s lemma, which is applicable since
m\in A^{\times}
. We skip its
proof.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\overline{X}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be a complete and integral normal
scheme. Let
D\subset\overline{X}
be an effective Cartier divisor with complement
X
.
Let
m\in k^{\times}
be a positive integer.
Then the canonical surjection
C(X)\twoheadrightarrow C(\overline{X},D)
induces an
isomorphism of topological abelian groups

{C(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}{C(\overline{X},D)}/m.

In particular, there is a canonical isomorphism of abelian groups

{C(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}{SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)}/m
.

Proof.

Combine (
5.1
), Lemma
5.6
and
Lemma
5.8
.
∎

Let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be an open immersion of integral normal
k
-schemes such
that
\overline{X}
is complete. Let
Z=\overline{X}\setminus X
with the reduced closed
subscheme structure.
We have the canonical map
\vartheta_{\overline{X}}\colon I^{{\rm t},W}(X)\to I(\overline{X})

which is identity on
F^{0}_{1}(X)
and the boundary map

K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})\to F^{0}_{1}(Z)
(which kills
U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})
)
for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
. It is easy to check that the diagram

(5.7)

\textstyle{F^{1,W}_{2}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{C}\partial_{C}}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1,W}_{2}(\overline{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{C}\partial_{C}}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{1}(\overline{X})}

is commutative. This shows that
\vartheta_{\overline{X}}
descends to a homomorphism

\vartheta_{\overline{X}}\colon C^{{\rm t},W}(X)\to C(\overline{X})=SK_{1}(\overline{X})
.
The composition
C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{{\rm t},W}(X)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X})

coincides with the one in (
4.4
).

5.5.
Relation between
C^{\rm t}(X)
and
C(\overline{X})

We shall now prove a theorem relating
C^{\rm t}(X)
with
C(\overline{X})
when

\overline{X}
is a smooth projective compactification of
X
.
Before we do this, we shall give a more refined presentation of
C(\overline{X})

than the one given in its definition. We shall use Bloch’s higher Chow groups for
this purpose. In this subsection, we let
k
be any field and do not
assume it to be local until Theorem
5.13
. We note here that
Definition
4.7
of
C^{\rm t}(X)
makes sense over any field.

We let
\overline{X}
be an integral smooth projective
k
-scheme of dimension
d\geq 1

and let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be an open immersion with the complementary
closed subset
Z
(having the reduced closed subscheme structure).
We let
\square^{n}_{k}=({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}\setminus\{1\})^{n}
and
\overline{\square}^{n}_{k}=({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k})^{n}
.
For any closed subscheme
Y\subset\overline{X}\times\overline{\square}^{n}_{k}
, we let

g^{i}_{Y}\colon Y\to\overline{\square}^{1}_{k}
be the projection to the
i
-th factor of

\overline{\square}^{n}_{k}
and let
f_{Y}\colon Y\to\overline{X}
be the projection to
\overline{X}
.
We recall the following from
[
63
, § 1]
.
Let
z^{d+1}(\overline{X},\bullet)
denote the cubical cycle complex of
\overline{X}

à la Bloch of codimension
(d+1)
-cycles.
The differential of this complex at level
n
is given by

\partial=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}(-1)^{i}(\partial^{i}_{\infty}-\partial^{i}_{0})
.
The homology of this complex (modulo the subcomplex of degenerate cycles)
at level
n
is the higher Chow group
{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X},n)
.

Let
