(z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},\bullet),\partial_{Z})
be the subcomplex of

(z^{d+1}(\overline{X},\bullet),\partial)

such that
z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},n)
is the free abelian group generated by
admissible integral cycles which intersect
Z\times F
properly for each face

F
of
\square^{n}_{k}
.
Since there can be no degenerate 0-cycles and degenerate cycles are preserved under
the boundary maps, one checks that the homology of
z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},\bullet)

(modulo the subcomplex of degenerate cycles) at level one is the the cokernel of
the map
\partial_{Z}\colon z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},2)\to z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},1)
, where

z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},1)
is the free abelian group generated by closed points
of
\overline{X}\times\square^{1}_{k}
which neither lie in
Z\times\square^{1}_{k}
nor in
any proper face of
\overline{X}\times\square^{1}_{k}

and
z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},2)
is the free abelian group generated by integral
curves in
\overline{X}\times\square^{2}_{k}
which intersect all faces of

X\times\square^{2}_{k}
and
Z\times\square^{2}_{k}
properly. In particular, they
do not meet any proper face of
Z\times\square^{2}_{k}
.
By Bloch’s moving lemma
[
11
]
(see
[
63
, Thm. 1.10]

for cubical version), the canonical map

(5.8)

\frac{z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},1)}{\partial_{Z}(z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},2))}\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X},1)

is an isomorphism.

Recall from §
4.2
that
C(\overline{X})
is the cokernel of the map

\partial^{1}_{\overline{X}}\colon F^{1}_{2}(\overline{X})={\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(\overline{X})}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))\to F^{0}_{1}(\overline{X})
.
It follows from the Gersten complex for the Milnor
K
-theory of regular local rings
(e.g., see Lemma
4.2
) that for any
C\in{\mathcal{C}}({X})
, one has

\partial^{1}_{\overline{C}_{n}}(K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},\Delta(C_{n})}))\subset F^{0}_{1}(C_{n})
. In particular,

\partial^{1}_{\overline{X}}(K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},\Delta(C_{n})}))\subset F^{0}_{1}(X)
.
We write
K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{C}_{n},\Delta(C_{n})})
as
{\rm fil}_{\rm nr}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
for

C\in{\mathcal{C}}({X})
.
We let
F^{1,\rm nr}_{2}({X})={\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}({X})}{\bigoplus}}{\rm fil}_{\rm nr}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
and let
C^{\rm nr}({X})
be the cokernel of the map

\partial^{1}_{X}\colon F^{1,\rm nr}_{2}({X})\to F^{0}_{1}(X)
.
Our key result is the following.

Lemma 5.11
.

The canonical map
C^{\rm nr}({X})\to C(\overline{X})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For any closed point
x\in z^{d+1}(\overline{X},1)
, we let
y
be the image of

x
in
\overline{X}
and let
\Psi_{1}([x])=N_{{k(x)}/{k(y)}}(g^{1}_{x})\in K^{M}_{1}(k(y))\subset F^{0}_{1}(\overline{X})
. For an integral curve
Y\in z^{d+1}(\overline{X},2)
, we let

\Psi_{2}([Y])=0
if
f_{Y}\colon Y\to\overline{X}
is constant.
If the scheme-theoretic image
f_{Y}(Y)
is an integral curve
\overline{Y^{\prime}}
, we let
Y^{\prime}=\overline{Y^{\prime}}\cap X
. In this case, we let

\Psi_{2}([Y])=N_{{k(Y)}/{k(Y^{\prime})}}(\{g^{1}_{Y},g^{2}_{Y}\})\in K^{M}_{2}(k(Y^{\prime}))\subset F^{1}_{2}(\overline{X})
.
By
[
67
, Thm. 2.5]
,
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
define a morphism of complexes

(5.9)

\Psi\colon\left(z^{d+1}(\overline{X},2)\xrightarrow{\partial}z^{d+1}(\overline{X},1)\right)\to\left(F^{1}_{2}(\overline{X})\xrightarrow{\partial^{1}_{\overline{X}}}F^{0}_{1}(\overline{X})\right).

such that the induced map
\Psi\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X},1)={\rm Coker}(\partial)\to{\rm Coker}(\partial^{1}_{\overline{X}})=C(\overline{X})
is an isomorphism.

Since a 0-cycle in
z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},1)
is supported in
X\times\square^{1}_{k}
,
we see that
\Psi_{1}(z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},1))\subset F^{0}_{1}(X)
.
If
Y
is an integral 1-cycle in
z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},2)
, then
Y
does not lie in

Z\times\square^{2}_{k}
and does not meet any proper face of
X\times\square^{2}_{k}

inside
Z\times\square^{2}_{k}
.
This implies that the image of
\{g^{1}_{Y},g^{2}_{Y}\}
dies in
K^{M}_{1}(k(y))
for every

y\in\nu^{-1}(Z)
, where
\overline{Y}
is the closure of
Y
in

\overline{X}\times\overline{\square}^{2}_{k}
and
\nu\colon\overline{Y}_{n}\to\overline{X}\times\overline{\square}^{2}_{k}\to\overline{X}
is the composite (finite) map.
It follows from
[
39
, Lem. 3.4]
that the image of

\Psi_{2}([Y])=N_{{k(Y)}/{k(Y^{\prime})}}(\{g^{1}_{Y},g^{2}_{Y}\})
dies in

K^{M}_{1}(k(y^{\prime}))
for every
y^{\prime}\in\Delta(Y^{\prime}_{n})
.
An application of Lemma
4.2
shows
that
\Psi_{2}([Y])\in{\rm fil}_{\rm nr}K^{M}_{2}(k(Y^{\prime}))
.
We conclude that
\Psi_{2}(z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},2))\subset F^{1,\rm nr}_{2}({X})
.
It follows that
(
5.9
) induces a commutative diagram of complexes

(5.10)

\textstyle{\left(z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},2)\xrightarrow{\partial_{Z}}z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},1)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\left(F^{1,\rm nr}_{2}({X})\xrightarrow{\partial^{1}_{X}}F^{0}_{1}({X})\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(z^{d+1}(\overline{X},2)\xrightarrow{\partial}z^{d+1}(\overline{X},1)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\left(F^{1}_{2}(\overline{X})\xrightarrow{\partial^{1}_{\overline{X}}}F^{0}_{1}(\overline{X})\right),}

where the vertical arrows are the canonical inclusions.

Taking the homology, we get a commutative diagram

(5.11)

\textstyle{{\rm Coker}(\partial_{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{{\rm Coker}(\partial^{1}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Coker}(\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{{\rm Coker}(\partial^{1}_{\overline{X}}).}

The left vertical arrow in (
5.11
)
is an isomorphism by (
5.8
) and the bottom
horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by (
5.9
). It follows that
the top horizontal arrow is injective.
To prove that this arrow is surjective, we fix
x\in X_{(0)}
.
Taking the graph of a rational function defines a homomorphism

\Gamma_{x}\colon K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\to{\rm CH}^{1}(k(x),1)
(see
[
102
, § 2]
).
Bloch’s push-forward map on the cycle complexes
corresponding to inclusion
{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
induces a morphism

z^{1}(k(x),\bullet)\to z^{d+1}(\overline{X},\bullet)
which clearly factors through

z^{d+1}_{Z}(\overline{X},\bullet)
. We thus have homomorphisms

K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm CH}^{1}(k(x),1)\to{\rm Coker}(\partial_{Z})\xrightarrow{\Psi}{\rm Coker}(\partial^{1}_{X})
whose composition is identity on

K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
.
This shows that the top horizontal arrow in (
5.11
) is surjective.
We have thus shown that all arrows in (
5.11
) are isomorphisms.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

There is an exact sequence

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X),x\in\Delta(C_{n})}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{2}(k(x))}\to{C^{\rm t}(X)}\to{C(\overline{X})}\to 0.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Definition
4.7
and
Lemma
5.11
.
∎

We can now prove the following analogue of the second isomorphism of
Corollary
3.11
for the class groups.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
k
be a local field of characteristic
p>0
. Let

\overline{X}
be an integral smooth projective
k
-scheme of dimension
d\geq 1

and let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be an open immersion.
Then
\vartheta_{\overline{X}}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C(\overline{X})
is surjective.
The map

\vartheta_{\overline{X}}\colon{C^{\rm t}(X)}/{p^{n}}\to{C(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}

is an isomorphism of topological abelian groups for every integer
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

The first part of the theorem follows directly from Corollary
5.12
.
We now show the second part.
In view of Proposition
4.13
, we only have to show the group isomorphism.
By Corollary
5.12
, we have an exact sequence

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X),x\in\Delta(C_{n})}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{2}(k(x))}/{p^{n}}\to{C^{\rm t}(X)}/{p^{n}}\to{C(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}\to 0.

It suffices therefore to show that
{K^{M}_{2}(k(x))}/{p^{n}}=0
if
x
is a closed point
of a curve over
k
. To this end, we use the main result of

[
73
]
, which says that

K^{M}_{2}(k(x))
is the direct sum of a (uniquely) divisible group and the group
of roots of unity in
k(x)
. Since the latter group is cyclic of order prime to

p
(e.g., see
[
79
, Prop. II.5.7]
), we are done.
∎

Scholium:
For
m\geq 0
, we let
C^{\rm t}_{m}(X)
be the cokernel of the residue map

\partial^{m}_{X}\colon{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}{\rm fil}_{\Delta(C_{n})}K^{M}_{m+1}(k(C))\to{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{m}(k(x)).

This is the generalized version of the tame class group
(see
[
111
, Defn. 1.1]
).
Note that
C^{\rm t}_{1}(X)=C^{\rm t}(X)
and

C^{\rm t}_{0}(X)
is canonically isomorphic to the Suslin homology

H^{S}_{0}(X)
, introduced by Suslin-Voevodsky
[
96
]

(see
[
88
, Thm. 5.1]
for this isomorphism). Then the proof of
Lemma
5.11
actually shows the following.
The only modifications one needs to make are:

(1)

Use
[
2
, Thm. 1.1]
to conclude that
the lower horizontal arrow in (
5.11
) is an isomorphism
for any
m\geq 0
.

(2)

Use Lemma
4.6

instead of Lemma
4.2
in the second paragraph and note
that every regular curve over a perfect field is smooth.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer. Then one has an exact sequence

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X),x\in\Delta(C_{n})}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{m+1}(k(x))}\to{C^{\rm t}_{m}(X)}\to{\rm CH}^{d+m}(\overline{X},m)\to 0

for
m=0,1
. This is exact for every value of
m
if
k
is perfect.

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
k
be any perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. Then the canonical map

{H^{S}_{0}(X)}/{p^{n}}\to{{\rm CH}^{d}(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}

is an isomorphism

Proof.

Use Theorem
5.14
and the fact that the Milnor
K
-theory of a perfect
field of characteristic
p>0
is
p^{n}
-divisible for every
n\geq 1
.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

If
k
is a finite field, one can easily show using the diagram
(
13.11
) (with
(2d+1,d+1)
replaced by
(2d,d)
),
Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture (now a theorem) and the theorem of
Kerz-Saito on Kato’s conjecture (see
[
59
]
) that

{H^{S}_{0}(X)}/{m}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/{m}
for every

m\in k^{\times}
. In combination with Corollary
5.15
and
the class field theory of Kato-Saito
[
54
]
, this
gives a very short proof of the finite coefficient version of the
class field theory of Schmidt-Spiess
[
89
]
.

5.6.
Norm zero Idele class groups

Let
k
be a local field and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
an integral normal scheme.
To define the norm map on the idele class group with modulus, we let
\overline{X}
be a
complete and integral normal
k
-scheme with an effective Cartier divisor
D
whose
complement is
X
.

For every
x\in X_{(0)}
, there is a continuous
norm map
N_{{k(x)}/k}\colon K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\to K^{M}_{1}(k)
.
Taking the sum over all closed points of
X
, we get a homomorphism

N_{X}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X)\to k^{\times}
, which is continuous by Lemma
3.1
.
If
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
and
x\in\Delta(C_{n})
, we have continuous homomorphisms

K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})\xrightarrow{\partial_{C_{n},x}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{N_{{k(x)}/k}}K^{M}_{1}(k)
. Note that the continuity of the residue map
\partial_{C_{n},x}
strongly
uses Kato’s topology on
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})
. Taking the direct sum, we get
a continuous homomorphism
N_{X}\colon I(X)\to k^{\times}
.

If
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, then the composite map

K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\xrightarrow{(\iota_{C},\partial_{C})}I(X)\xrightarrow{N_{X}}k^{\times}

is the same as the composite map
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n}))\xrightarrow{\partial_{\overline{C}_{n}}}{\underset{x\in(\overline{C}_{n})_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{N_{\overline{C}_{n}}}K^{M}_{1}(k)
. But this composite map is zero by Suslin’s reciprocity law for Milnor

K
-theory
[
94
]
. It follows that there is a continuous homomorphism

N_{X}\colon C(X)\to k^{\times}
. Since the map
K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})\to K^{M}_{1}(k)
factors
through the residue map
\partial_{C_{n},x}
which is trivial on
U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{x})
,
it follows that
N_{X}
actually factors through
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
.
Hence, we get continuous norm homomorphisms

(5.12)

C(X)\xrightarrow{N_{X}}k^{\times};\ \ C(\overline{X},D)\xrightarrow{N_{\overline{X}|D}}k^{\times};\ \ C^{\rm t}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}C^{{\rm t},W}(X)\xrightarrow{N_{X}}k^{\times}.

We let
C(X)_{0}\subset C(X),\ C(\overline{X},D)_{0}\subset C(\overline{X},D),\ C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}\subset C^{\rm t}(X)
and
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)_{0}\subset C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
be the kernels of the above norm
maps. We shall call them the norm zero subgroups.
Note that these subgroups are closed in their ambient class groups because the canonical
topology of
k^{\times}
is Hausdorff.

6.
Idele class groups via cycles with modulus

In this section, we shall present a relation of the idele class group with modulus and
the tame class group with the higher Chow groups with modulus. This will provide
a cycle theoretic description of these class groups. As a byproduct, we shall
prove an analogue of Bloch’s formula for higher Chow groups with modulus for curves.
We fix an arbitrary infinite field
k
throughout this section.

6.1.
Higher Chow group with modulus and class groups

Let
\overline{\square}^{1}_{k}={\rm Proj}(k[Y_{0},Y_{1}])
and let
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}\cong{\rm Spec\,}(k[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}])
be the open subset of
\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}
, where

(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
denotes the coordinate system of
\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}
with

y_{j}={Y^{j}_{1}}/{Y^{j}_{0}}
.
Given
Y\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, a rational map
f\colon Y\dashedrightarrow\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
and a point
y\in Y
in the domain of definition of

f
, we shall let
f_{i}(y)=(y_{i}\circ f)(y)
, where
y_{i}\colon\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}\to\overline{\square}^{1}_{k}
is the
i
-th projection. For any
1\leq i\leq n
and

t\in\overline{\square}^{1}_{k}(k)
, we let
F^{t}_{n,i}

denote the closed subscheme of
\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}
given by
\{y_{i}=t\}
. We let

F^{t}_{n}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\ F^{t}_{n,i}
.

Let
\overline{X}
be an integral normal
k
-scheme of dimension
d
and

D\subset\overline{X}
an effective Cartier divisor with complement
X
. We let

D_{0}=D_{\rm red}
and
D_{+}=D+D_{0}
.
Recall that the cycle complex with modulus

z^{q}(\overline{X}|D,\bullet)
is the subcomplex of Bloch’s cycle complex
z^{q}(\overline{X},\bullet)
,
where each integral cycle
Z\in z^{q}(\overline{X},n)
is required to additionally satisfy the
following (modulus) condition.
If
\overline{Z}
is the closure of
Z
in
\overline{X}\times\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}
and

\nu:\overline{Z}_{n}\to\overline{X}\times\overline{\square}_{k}^{n}

is the canonical map from the normalization of
\overline{Z}
, then the inequality

(6.1)

\nu^{*}(D\times\overline{\square}_{k}^{n})\leq\nu^{*}(\overline{X}\times F^{1}_{n})

holds in the group of Weil divisors on
\overline{Z}_{n}
.
The higher Chow group with modulus
{\rm CH}^{q}(\overline{X}|D,i)
is the
i
-th homology
of the complex
z^{q}(\overline{X}|D,\bullet)
(modulo the subcomplex of degenerate cycles).
By definition, there is a canonical homomorphism

{\rm CH}^{q}(\overline{X}|D,i)\to{\rm CH}^{q}(\overline{X},i)
.
If
\overline{X}
is complete of dimension
d
,
there is a push-forward map
{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X},1)\to{\rm CH}^{1}({\rm Spec\,}(k),1)\cong k^{\times}
.
Composing the two maps, we get
N_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)\to k^{\times}
. We let
{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)_{0}={\rm Ker}(N_{\overline{X}|D})
.

Our goal in this subsection is to prove the following result.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
\overline{X}
is complete. Then we have the following.

(1)

There is a canonical isomorphism of pro-abelian groups

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon\{{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|nD,1)\}_{n\geq 1}\xrightarrow{\cong}\{SK_{1}(\overline{X}|nD)\}_{n\geq 1}.

(2)

There is a canonical isomorphism of abelian groups

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)\otimes R\xrightarrow{\cong}SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\otimes R,

where
R=[\tfrac{1}{p}]
if
{\rm char}(k)=p>0

and
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
with
m\neq 0
if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.

We begin by constructing a cycle class map
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},1)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
. This does not require
\overline{X}
to be
complete. The map
\Psi_{1}
of (
5.9
) restricts a
cycle class homomorphism
\Psi_{1}\colon z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{0},1)\to F^{0}_{1}(X)
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The map
\Psi_{1}\colon z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{0},1)\to F^{0}_{1}(X)
descends to a homomorphism

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},1)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D).

Proof.

Using (
5.9
), it suffices to
show that if
Z\in z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},2)
is an integral cycle, then

\Psi_{2}([Z])
is zero if
\pi_{\overline{X}}(\overline{Z})
is a closed point and

\Psi_{2}([Z])\in{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
if
\pi_{\overline{X}}(\overline{Z})=\overline{C}
is an
integral curve in
\overline{X}
, where
\overline{Z}
is the closure of
Z
in

\overline{\square}^{2}_{\overline{X}}
,
C=\overline{C}\cap X
,
\pi_{\overline{X}}\colon\overline{\square}^{2}_{\overline{X}}\to\overline{X}
is the projection and
\nu\colon\overline{C}_{n}\to\overline{X}
is the normalization morphism for
\overline{C}
.

To show the above, we fix an integral cycle
Z\in z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},2)
.
If
\pi_{\overline{X}}(\overline{Z})
is a closed point, then the modulus condition implies that

\overline{Z}\cap\overline{\square}^{2}_{D}=\emptyset
. In this case, we have already seen
in the proof of Lemma
5.11
that
\Psi_{2}([Z])=0
.
Suppose now that
\pi_{\overline{X}}(\overline{Z})
is not a closed point. Then
\pi_{\overline{X}}\colon\overline{Z}\to\overline{X}
must be a finite morphism. We let
\overline{C}\subset\overline{X}
be the
scheme theoretic image of this finite morphism and let
C=\overline{C}\cap X
.
Then the modulus condition for
Z
implies that
C\subset\overline{C}
is dense open.
As in §
5.5
, let
g^{i}_{Z}\colon\overline{Z}\to\overline{\square}_{k}
be the projection to the
i
-th factor of
\overline{\square}^{2}
and let
g=\{g^{1}_{Z},g^{2}_{Z}\}\in K^{M}_{2}(k(Z))
.
Note that neither
g^{1}_{Z}
nor
g^{2}_{Z}
can be identically zero because
Z
intersects
the faces of
\square^{2}_{\overline{X}}
properly. Hence,
g
is a well-defined element.

We have a commutative diagram

(6.2)

\textstyle{\overline{Z}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\overline{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\overline{C}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{\overline{C}.}

We let
\widetilde{\nu}=\pi_{\overline{X}}\circ\nu^{\prime}\colon\overline{Z}_{n}\to\overline{X}

and let
\widetilde{\nu}^{*}(D)=m_{1}[x_{1}]+\cdots+m_{r}[x_{r}]
. We set

D_{Z}=(m_{1}+1)[x_{1}]+\cdots+(m_{r}+1)[x_{r}]
. We let
R={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Z}_{n},D_{Z}}
and
let
F
be the quotient field of
R
. We let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
denote the maximal ideal of

R
corresponding to the point
x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
. Then we have

\widetilde{\nu}^{*}(D)\leq D_{Z}\leq\widetilde{\nu}^{*}(D_{+})
. Since
Z\in z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},2)
,
these inequalities and the modulus condition for
Z
together imply that

{\rm ord}_{x_{i}}(g^{1}_{Z}-1)+{\rm ord}_{x_{i}}(g^{2}_{Z}-1)\geq m_{i}+1
for each
1\leq i\leq r
.
By
[
37
, Lem. 3.3]
(with
R
replaced by
R_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}
), this
implies that
g\in(1+{\mathfrak{m}}^{m_{i}+1}_{i})K^{M}_{1}(F)\subset K^{M}_{1}(F)\cdot K^{M}_{1}(F)\subset K^{M}_{2}(F)
for every
1\leq i\leq r
.
If we now apply
[
41
, Lem. 4.3]
(with

A=R_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}
), it follows that

g\in U^{\prime}_{m_{i}}K^{M}_{2}(F)
(see §
3.3
) for every
1\leq i\leq r
.
In other words,
g\in\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\bigcap}}}U^{\prime}_{m_{i}}K^{M}_{2}(F)
.
Using Lemma
4.5
, this implies that
g\in{\rm fil}_{\widetilde{\nu}^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(F)={\rm fil}_{\widetilde{\nu}^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(Z))
.
We now conclude from
[
82
, Lem. 6.19]
and Lemma
4.5

that

\Psi_{2}([Z])=N_{{k(Z)}/{k(C)}}(g)\in{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
. This finishes the
proof of the lemma.
∎

To construct an inverse of
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}
, we let
x\in X_{(0)}
.
We have seen in the proof of Lemma
5.11
that the graph function
defines an isomorphism
K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm CH}^{1}(k(x),1)
.
Since
x\in X_{(0)}
, we have a push-forward homomorphism

{\rm CH}^{1}(k(x),1)\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)
(see
[
65
, § 2]
)
and it is clear that
the composite
\phi_{x}\colon K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)
is simply the
above graph homomorphism. Summing over
(\phi_{x})_{x\in X_{(0)}}
, we get a homomorphism

\phi_{\overline{X}|D}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X)\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

If
\overline{X}
is complete, then
\phi_{\overline{X}|D}
induces a homomorphism

\phi_{\overline{X}|D}\colon SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1).

Proof.

To show that
\phi_{\overline{X}|D}
factors through
SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
, we fix a curve

C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
and
let
\beta\in{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}K^{M}_{2}(k(C))
, where
\nu\colon\overline{C}_{n}\to\overline{X}
is the
canonical map. Using the push-forward map
\nu_{*}\colon{\rm CH}^{2}(\overline{C}_{n}|\nu^{*}(D),1)\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)
, it suffices to show that
\phi_{\overline{C}_{n}|\nu^{*}(D)}
factors through

SK_{1}(\overline{C}_{n}|\nu^{*}(D))
. We can therefore assume that
\overline{X}
is a complete
integral regular curve over
k
. We let

R={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X},D}
and let
I\subset R
be
the ideal defining the divisor
D
. By Lemma
4.5
, we have

{\rm fil}_{D}K^{M}_{2}(k(X))=K^{M}_{2}(R,I)
and
[
38
, Lem. 3.2]
implies
that the latter group is generated by
Steinberg symbols of the form
\{1+a,b\}
, where
a\in I
and
b\in R^{\times}
.
We can therefore assume that
\beta=\{1+a,b\}
with
a\in I
and
b\in R^{\times}
.

Let
\overline{Z}\subset\overline{\square}^{2}_{\overline{X}}
be the closure of the graph of the rational
map
\beta^{\prime}:=(1+a,b)=(f_{1},f_{2})\colon\overline{X}\dashedrightarrow\overline{\square}^{2}_{k}
.
Since
\overline{X}
is a complete integral regular curve over
k
, the rational function

f_{i}
defines a regular morphism
f_{i}\colon\overline{X}\to\overline{\square}^{1}_{k}
for
i=1,2
.
Furthermore,
\overline{Z}
is the graph
\Gamma_{\beta^{\prime}}=\{(x,f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))|x\in\overline{X}\}\subset\overline{\square}^{2}_{\overline{X}}
. It follows that the projection map

\pi_{\overline{X}}\colon\overline{Z}\to\overline{X}
is an isomorphism. In particular,
\overline{Z}
is
regular.

We let
Z=\overline{Z}\cap\square^{2}_{\overline{X}}
.
It is easy to check (e.g., see the proof of
[
67
, Lem. 2.8]
)
that
Z\in z^{2}(\overline{X},2)
. Let

A={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{Z},E}
, where
E=\pi^{*}_{\overline{X}}(D)\cong D=m_{1}[x_{1}]+\cdots+m_{r}[x_{r}]
, say. Since
a\in I
and
b\in R^{\times}
and the composition
\overline{Z}\to\overline{\square}^{2}_{\overline{X}}\to\overline{X}
induces an isomorphism of semilocal rings

R\xrightarrow{\cong}A
,
it follows that
{\rm ord}_{x_{i}}(f_{1}-1)\geq m_{i}
and
{\rm ord}_{x_{i}}(f_{2}-1)\geq 0
, and
therefore,
{\rm ord}_{x_{i}}(f_{1}-1)+{\rm ord}_{x_{i}}(f_{2}-1)\geq m_{i}
for every
1\leq i\leq r
.
In other words, the cycle
Z
satisfies the modulus condition. We conclude
that
Z\in z^{2}(\overline{X}|D,2)
. It remains to show that

\partial([Z])=\phi_{\overline{X}|D}\circ\partial^{1}_{X}(\beta)
.

It is easy to see from the description of
\overline{Z}
as the graph of the morphism

(f_{1},f_{2})\colon\overline{X}\to\overline{\square}^{2}_{k}
that

(6.3)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial([Z])&:=&(\partial^{0}_{1}(Z)-\partial^{\infty}_{1}([Z]))+(\partial^{\infty}_{2}([Z])-\partial^{0}_{2}(Z))\\
&=&([\Gamma_{f^{-1}_{1}(0)}]-[\Gamma_{f^{-1}_{1}(\infty)}])+([\Gamma_{f^{-1}_{2}(\infty)}]-[\Gamma_{f^{-1}_{2}(0)}])\in z^{2}(\overline{X},1),\end{array}

where
\Gamma_{f^{-1}_{j}(t)}\subset\square^{1}_{X}\subset\overline{\square}^{1}_{\overline{X}}

is the graph of the induced morphism
f_{i}\colon f^{-1}_{j}(t)\to\overline{\square}^{1}_{k}
from the scheme theoretic inverse image of
\{t\}

under the
j
-th projection with
i\neq j
and
t\in\{0,\infty\}
.
We let
S^{i}_{t}
be the support of the 0-cycle

[f^{-1}_{i}(t)]\in z^{1}({X},0)
for

i\in\{1,2\}
and
t\in\{0,\infty\}
. Since
Z
intersects the faces
of
\square^{2}_{X}
properly, we can see that
S^{i}_{t}\cap S^{j}_{t^{\prime}}=\emptyset

whenever
i\neq j
or
t\neq t^{\prime}
.

On the other hand, recall that for any
i
,
t
and any
x\in\overline{X}_{(0)}
,
one has that

\partial_{x}(\beta)=\gamma_{x}((-1)^{v_{x}(f_{1})v_{x}(f_{2})}{(f_{1})^{v_{x}(f_{2})}}/{(f_{2})^{v_{x}(f_{1})}})
, where

\partial_{x}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(X))\to K^{M}_{1}(k(x))
is the boundary map of the
Gersten complex for
K^{M}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X},x})
and

\gamma_{x}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X},x}\twoheadrightarrow k(x)
is the quotient map.
Note also that
\partial_{x}(\beta)=0
if
x
does not lie in any
S^{i}_{t}
.
Putting all of these together, we conclude that the image of
\beta
under
the composite map
K^{M}_{2}(k(X))\xrightarrow{\partial_{x}}K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{\phi_{x}}{\rm CH}^{2}(\overline{X}|D,1)

is same as the
x
-component of the cycle class
[\Gamma_{f^{-1}_{i}(t)}]

when
x\in S^{i}_{t}
.
Taking the sum over

i\in\{1,2\}
and
t\in\{0,\infty\}
, we conclude that

\partial([Z])=\phi_{\overline{X}|D}\circ\partial^{1}_{X}(\beta)
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
:

It is easy to check that the composite map

SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D_{+})\xrightarrow{\phi_{\overline{X}|D_{+}}}{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},1)\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}}SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
is the canonical surjection

SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D_{+})\twoheadrightarrow SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
. This already shows that
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\circ\phi_{\overline{X}|D}
is identity at the level of pro-abelian groups. We now show that

\phi_{\overline{X}|D}
is surjective, which will complete the proof of part (1) of
Theorem
6.1
.

We let
[x]\in z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)
be a 0-cycle represented by a closed point
x

and let
y=f(x)\in X
, where
f\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\to\overline{X}
is the restriction
of the projection
\overline{\square}^{1}_{\overline{X}}\to\overline{X}
.
From the construction of
\phi_{\overline{X}|D}
, we have a commutative diagram

(6.4)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}^{1}(k(x),1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\overline{X}|D}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1),}

where the left vertical arrow is the composition

K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{N_{{k(x)}/{k(y)}}}K^{M}_{1}(k(y))\hookrightarrow F^{0}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
. Since the sum of the right vertical arrows

{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}{\rm CH}^{1}(k(x),1)\to{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)
is
surjective as
x
runs through the
closed points of
{\square}^{1}_{X}
not meeting latter’s faces and each
\phi_{x}
is
an isomorphism (e.g., see
[
102
]
), we conclude that
\phi_{\overline{X}|D}
is
surjective.

We now prove part (2) of Theorem
6.1
. After what we have shown above
in the proof of part (1), it suffices to show that the canonical surjection

{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},1)\otimes R\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)\otimes R
is an
isomorphism when
R
is as in part (2). But this is already shown in

[
75
, Thm. 1.3(2)]
.
∎

Combining Lemma
4.10
and Theorem
6.1
, we obtain the
following cycle theoretic interpretation of the tame class group of
X
if
it is not necessarily complete or regular.
We let
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}^{\rm t}_{\overline{X}|D}=\psi_{\overline{X}|D}\circ{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}
(see (
4.4
)).

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
R=[\tfrac{1}{p}]
if
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
and
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
with
m\neq 0
if

{\rm char}(k)=0
. Then the cycle class map

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}^{\rm t}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)\otimes R\to C^{\rm t}(X)\otimes R

is an isomorphism. This isomorphism preserves the norm zero subgroups.

6.2.
Bloch’s formula for higher Chow group with modulus on
curves

As another consequence of Theorem
6.1
and the Thomason-Trobaugh
descent spectral sequence for relative
K
-theory, we obtain the following analogue
of Bloch’s formula for higher Chow groups of 0-cycles with modulus for
curves, in pro-setting. Note that no analogue of Bloch’s formula is yet known
for higher Chow groups of 0-cycles with modulus, even in dimension one.

For a Noetherian scheme
Z
and a closed subscheme
W\subset Z
, we let

{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i,(Z,W)}={\rm Ker}({\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i,Z}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i,W})
, where
{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i,Z}
is the
sheaf (in a given topology on
Z
) whose stalks are the corresponding
Milnor
K
-groups of the local rings of
Z
. We let
K^{W}(Z)
be the
homotopy fiber of the pull-back morphism between the (non-connective) spectra

K(Z)\to K(Z\setminus W)
and let
K^{W}_{i}(Z)
be the
i
-th homotopy group of
this homotopy fiber.

Assume that
X
is regular of dimension
d
and
x\in X_{(0)}
. We then have a
canonical isomorphism

K^{M}_{n-d}(k(x))\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{d}_{x}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{n,(\overline{X},D)})

for every
n\geq 0
(e.g., see
[
39
, Lem. 3.7]
),
where the group on the right
is Zariski cohomology with support in
\{x\}
. By composing with the
‘forget support’
map, we get
K^{M}_{n-d}(k(x))\to H^{d}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{n,(\overline{X},D)})
.
We thus get a homomorphism

z^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D,1)\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}}F^{0}_{1}(X)\to H^{d}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{d+1,(\overline{X},D)})
. We denote this also by
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Assume that
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. Then the cycle class map

z^{2}(\overline{X}|D,1)\to H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})
induces an
isomorphism of pro-abelian groups

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon\{{\rm CH}^{2}(\overline{X}|nD,1)\}_{n\geq 1}\xrightarrow{\cong}\{H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},nD)})\}_{n\geq 1}.

Proof.

By virtue of Theorem
6.1
, we only need to show that there is a
canonical isomorphism

(6.5)

\gamma_{\overline{X}|D}\colon SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})

whose composition with the cycle class map
z^{2}(\overline{X}|D,1)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)

is the same as the one given in the statement of the proposition.
To this end, we first use the Thomason-Trobaugh localization sequence for
relative
K
-theory (see
[
101
, Thm. 7.4]
) to get
an exact sequence of relative
K
-groups

K_{2}(\overline{X}\setminus S,D)\to K^{S}_{1}(\overline{X})\to K_{1}(\overline{X},D)\to K_{1}(\overline{X}\setminus S,D)

for every closed subset
S\subset\overline{X}
contained in
X
.

Taking the filtered colimit over
S
and using the continuity property of
K
-theory,
we get an exact sequence

(6.6)

K_{2}(A,I)\to F^{0}_{1}(X)\to K_{1}(\overline{X},D)\to K_{1}(A,I),

where we let
A={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X},D}
and
I={\rm Ker}(A\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(D))
.
One knows by the construction of the localization sequence that
the boundary map
K_{2}(A,I)\to F^{0}_{1}(X)
coincides with the
composition
K_{2}(A,I)\twoheadrightarrow K^{M}_{2}(A,I)\xrightarrow{\partial^{1}_{X}}F^{0}_{1}(X)
,
where the first arrow is surjective by Lemma
4.1
. We thus get
an exact sequence

(6.7)

0\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\to K_{1}(\overline{X},D)\to K_{1}(A,I).

On the other hand, the Thomason-Trobaugh descent spectral sequence
(see
[
101
, Thm. 10.3]
) gives rise to a short exact sequence

(6.8)

0\to H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})\to K_{1}(\overline{X},D)\to H^{0}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(\overline{X},D)})\to 0.

Note that we used the isomorphisms
{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(\overline{X},D)}\cong{\mathcal{K}}_{1,(\overline{X},D)}
and

H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})
that follow from
Lemma
4.1
.
Moreover, the functoriality of this spectral sequence gives rise to a commutative
diagram

\textstyle{K_{1}(\overline{X},D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(A,I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(\overline{X},D)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{\rm zar}(Y,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(Y,D)}),}

where we let
Y={\rm Spec\,}(A)
.

It is an elementary verification that the right
vertical arrow in the above diagram
is an isomorphism. Since the bottom horizontal arrow is injective,
we see that (
6.7
) is the same as the exact sequence

0\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\to K_{1}(\overline{X},D)\to H^{0}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(\overline{X},D)})\to 0.

Comparing this with (
6.8
), we deduce (
6.5
).
It is also clear from the proof that the composition

z^{2}(\overline{X}|D,1)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\to H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})
is the same
as the one stated in the proposition.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

If
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

C^{\rm t}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D_{0})}).

Proof.

This follows from (
6.5
) once we observe that
the map
SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D_{0})\to C^{\rm t}(X)
(see (
4.4
))
is an isomorphism if
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Assume that
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
.
Let
R=[\tfrac{1}{p}]
if
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
and
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
with
m\neq 0
if

{\rm char}(k)=0
. Then the cycle class map

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{2}(\overline{X}|D,1)\otimes R\to H^{1}_{\rm zar}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,(\overline{X},D)})\otimes R

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
6.1
and (
6.5
).
∎

7.
The reciprocity maps

In this section, we shall define the reciprocity homomorphisms from various class groups
of integral normal schemes over a local field to the corresponding étale fundamental
groups. We shall also establish some formal properties of these homomorphisms.
We shall use the following result from the local class field theory as a key ingredient.
For topological abelian groups
G
and
H
, we let

{\rm Hom}_{{\rm cf}}(G,H)={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G,H)_{\rm tor}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
K
be a complete discrete valuation field whose residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a local
field. Then we have the following.

(1)

There is a commutative diagram of continuous homomorphisms (called the reciprocity
homomorphisms)

(7.1)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{G_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{1}({\mathfrak{f}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{{\mathfrak{f}}}}
\textstyle{G_{{\mathfrak{f}}},}

where the left vertical arrow is the residue map and the right vertical arrow is the
canonical map.

(2)

The cup product pairing

(7.2)

\langle,\rangle\colon K^{M}_{2}(K)\otimes H^{1}(K)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

induces isomorphisms

(7.3)

\rho^{\vee}_{K}\colon H^{1}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\rm Hom}_{\rm cf}(K^{M}_{2}(K),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})&\mbox{if ${\rm char}(K)=0$}\\
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(K^{M}_{2}(K),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})&\mbox{if ${\rm char}(K)>0$}.\end{array}\right.

(3)

These isomorphisms induce the following.

\rho^{\vee}_{K}\colon{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{\rm cf}\left(\frac{K^{M}_{2}(K)}{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K)},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\right)\ \mbox{if}\ {\rm char}(K)=0.

\rho^{\vee}_{K}\colon{\rm fil}_{m}H^{1}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}\left(\frac{K^{M}_{2}(K)}{U^{\prime}_{m}K^{M}_{2}(K)},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\right)\ \forall\ m\geq 0\ \mbox{if}\ {\rm char}(K)>0.

Proof.

The pairing (
7.2
) can be found in
[
52
, § 3.2, p. 661]
.
The reciprocity map for
{\mathfrak{f}}
is classical. The existence of
\rho_{K}
follows from
(
7.2
), and its
continuity is a direct consequence of
[
51
, Thm. 2, p. 304]
(see
also
[
52
, § 3.5]
). The commutativity of (
7.1
)
is shown in
[
52
, § 3.2, Lem. 6(2)]
.
The isomorphisms of (2) follow from Kato’s class field theory for 2-dimensional local
fields (see
[
52
, § 3.5 (ii), Rem. 3.5.4]
).
The first isomorphism and the
m=0
case of the second isomorphism in (3) follow by
combining the isomorphisms of (2) and diagram (
7.1
)
with the classically known fact that
\rho_{\mathfrak{f}}
induces an isomorphism

H^{1}({\mathfrak{f}})\xrightarrow{\rho^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{f}}}(K^{M}_{1}({\mathfrak{f}}))^{\vee}
(e.g., see

[
85
, Thm. 3.1]
). The
m\geq 1
case of the second isomorphism in (3)
follows from
[
70
, Prop. 3.2.7]
and
[
39
, Thm. 6.3]
.
Recall that
{\rm fil}_{m}H^{1}(K)=H^{1}(K)
for
m\geq 1
if
{\rm char}(K)=0
.
∎

7.1.
Reciprocity for idele class group

We now let
k
be a local field and

X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
an integral normal scheme with function field
K
.
For
x\in X
, let
\iota^{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\to X
be the inclusion.
If
\text{\rm dim}(X)=0
, Proposition
7.1
already provides the desired reciprocity
homomorphism. We therefore assume that
\text{\rm dim}(X)\geq 1
.

By taking sum of the composite maps
K^{M}_{1}(k(x))\xrightarrow{\rho_{k(x)}}G_{k(x)}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
over
x\in X_{(0)}
, we get a homomorphism

\rho^{1}_{X}\colon F^{0}_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
.
For
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, Proposition
7.1
yields
a continuous reciprocity homomorphism

\rho^{\eta}_{\infty}\colon K^{M}_{2}(k(\eta)_{\infty})\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}(k(\eta)_{\infty}))
,
where
\eta\in C
is the generic point. Composing this with

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}(k(\eta)_{\infty}))\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(k(\eta))\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
and summing over
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, we get a
homomorphism

\rho^{2}_{X}\colon{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(C_{n})_{\infty})\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
.
It follows from Lemma
3.1
that
\rho^{1}_{X}
and
\rho^{2}_{X}
are
continuous.
We let
\rho_{X}=(\rho^{2}_{X}+\rho^{1}_{X})\colon I(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
. This is clearly
continuous. Our goal is to show that
\rho_{X}
factors through
C(X)
.

Lemma 7.2
.

\rho_{X}
factors through a continuous homomorphism

\rho_{X}\colon C(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X).

Proof.

The factorization will automatically imply continuity because
C(X)
is a topological
quotient of
I(X)
. Now, it is clear from the definition of the reciprocity
homomorphism that the diagram

(7.4)

\textstyle{I(C_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{C_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(k(C_{n}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{*}}
\textstyle{I(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X).}

is commutative for every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
if
\nu\colon C_{n}\to X
denotes the
canonical map. Using this, we are reduced to proving the lemma when
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
.

Let
\overline{X}
be the unique normal compactification of
X
. We let

I^{\prime}(\overline{X})={\underset{x\in\overline{X}_{(0)}}{\prod^{\prime}}}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})

be the restricted product with
respect to the closed subgroups
U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
. We endow

{\underset{x\in\overline{X}_{(0)}}{\prod}}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
with the product topology and

I^{\prime}(\overline{X})
with the induced subspace topology. We let
I^{\prime}(X)={\underset{x\in{X}_{(0)}}{\prod^{\prime}}}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
. We consider the diagram

(7.5)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota_{X},\partial_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{I^{\prime}(\overline{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{G_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X),}

where the right vertical arrow is the canonical surjection and
\alpha

is induced by taking product of completions.
It is easy to check that this arrow is well-defined. Our task is to show that

\rho_{X}\circ(\iota_{X},\partial_{X})=0
.

To show the above, we first define
\beta
and
\gamma
such that
(
7.5
) commutes. To define
\beta
,
note that we can write
I^{\prime}(\overline{X})=K^{M}_{2}(K_{\infty})\bigoplus I^{\prime}(X)
.
We set
\beta
to be the identity map on the first summand and

\beta(a_{x})=\partial_{X,x}(a_{x})
if
a_{x}\in K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
with
x\in X_{(0)}
.
This is well-defined because
{\rm Ker}(\partial_{X,x})=U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
.

To define
\gamma
, let
\chi\in H^{1}(K)
and
(a_{x})\in I^{\prime}(\overline{X})
.
We can find a dense open subscheme
U\subset X

such that
\chi
is unramified along
U
. That is,
\chi\in H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\subset H^{1}(K)
. Equivalently,
\chi
factors through
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(U)
.
It follows therefore from Proposition
7.1
(3) that

\sum_{x\in X_{(0)}}\langle\chi_{x},a_{x}\rangle
is a finite sum. In other words, the cup product
pairing of (
7.2
) induces a pairing

(7.6)

\langle,\rangle\colon H^{1}(K)\otimes I^{\prime}(\overline{X})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Equivalently, we get a homomorphism
\gamma\colon I^{\prime}(\overline{X})\to G_{K}
which is
compatible with the reciprocity homomorphisms of the completions of
K
.
It is clear that
\gamma
is continuous. Furthermore, the commutative
diagram (
7.1
) implies that the diagram (
7.5
) is commutative.
Since the composite horizontal arrow on the top in (
7.5
) is zero by

[
85
, Thm. 1.1, 2.1]
, it follows that
\rho_{X}\circ(\iota_{X},\partial_{X})

is zero. This implies that
\rho_{X}
factors through
C(X)
, as desired.
∎

7.2.
Reciprocity for tame class group

Let
k
be a local field and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
an integral normal scheme with function
field
K
. We shall now construct the reciprocity homomorphisms for the tame class
groups of
X
. Since there is a canonical continuous homomorphism

\delta_{X}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
, it suffices to construct the reciprocity
homomorphism for the latter group.

Proposition 7.3
.

\rho_{X}
induces a continuous homomorphism

\rho^{\rm t}_{X}\colon C^{{\rm t},W}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
such that we have a
commutative diagram

(7.7)

\textstyle{C(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{X}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{X}}
\textstyle{C^{{\rm t},W}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{{\rm t}}_{X}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X).}

Proof.

Using the definition of
C^{{\rm t},W}(X)
and Corollary
2.4
, it suffices to prove
the proposition when
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. We assume this to be the case and let
\overline{X}

be the unique normal compactification of
X
. Let
\eta
denote the generic point of

X
. Using Proposition
3.4
, Definition
5.2
and
Lemma
7.2
, it suffices to show that if
x\in\Delta(X)
, then the
image of
U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
under the reciprocity map
\rho_{K_{x}}\colon K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})\to G_{K_{x}}
lies in the (closed) subgroup
G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}
. If
{\rm char}(k)=0
, then

U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
is a divisible group by Lemma
5.9
. Hence,
its image under
\rho_{K_{x}}
must be zero as
G_{K_{x}}
is a profinite group. If

{\rm char}(k)>0
, then using the Pontryagin duality and isomorphism (
2.3
), the
assertion is equivalent to showing that
\chi\circ\rho_{K_{x}}(a)=0
for every

\chi\in{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(K_{x})
and
a\in U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
.
But this follows from Proposition
7.1
(3).
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

For an integral normal
k
-scheme
X
, there is a commutative diagram

(7.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0}}
\textstyle{C^{\rm t}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\rm t}_{X}}
\textstyle{k^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{G_{k}.}

Proof.

The only thing that may perhaps need an explanation is the commutativity of the right
square.
But this follows from the construction of various maps in the square
and
[
52
, § 3.2, Cor. 1]
.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a
morphism of regular
k
-schemes.
Then we have a commutative diagram

(7.9)

\textstyle{C^{\rm t}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\rm t}_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{C^{\rm t}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\rm t}_{X}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X).}

If
f
is proper, the same holds for the reciprocity maps for idele class groups.

Proof.

The vertical and horizontal arrows exist by Corollary
2.4
,
Proposition
4.13

(use Proposition
5.3
for idele class groups)
and Proposition
7.3
. The commutativity of the diagram follows from the
explicit construction of these maps using
[
52
, § 3.2, Cor. 1]
.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

Corollary
7.5
remains valid without the regularity assumption if we
replace
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(-)
by
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm ctm}}_{1}(-)
.

The following result shows that over a local field of
characteristic
p>0
,
there is no difference between the
p
-adic tame class field theory and the

p
-adic unramified class field theory of smooth varieties.

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
j\colon X\to\overline{X}
be an open immersion between
k
-schemes
such that
\overline{X}
is smooth and projective over
k
. Assume that
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
.
Then we have a commutative diagram of continuous homomorphisms

(7.10)

\textstyle{{C^{\rm t}(X)}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\rm t}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{{C(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}/{p^{n}}}

for every integer
n\geq 1
such that the vertical arrows are isomorphisms.

Proof.

Combine Corollary
3.11
, Proposition
4.13
and Theorem
5.13
.
∎

7.3.
Reciprocity for Idele class and Chow groups with modulus

Let
k
be a local field and
\overline{X}
a complete integral normal
k
-scheme
with an effective Cartier divisor
D\subset\overline{X}
whose complement is
X
.
We assume that
X
is regular. Let
K
denote the function field of
X
.
We let
{\rm fil}^{c}_{D}H^{1}(K)
be the subgroup of characters
\chi
of
G_{K}

satisfying the property that
\chi\in H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
and for every

C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)

with canonical map
\nu\colon\overline{C}_{n}\to\overline{X}
, the character
\nu^{*}(\chi)
lies in

{\rm fil}_{\nu^{*}(D)}H^{1}(k(C_{n}))
(see §
2.4
) under the map

\nu^{*}\colon H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(C_{n},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
. We let

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X},D)=({\rm fil}^{c}_{D}(H^{1}(K)))^{\vee}
with respect to the discrete topology
of
{\rm fil}^{c}_{D}H^{1}(K)
. This notion of étale fundamental group with modulus was
introduced by Deligne and Laumon
[
68
]

(see
[
40
, Definition 5.1]
). It is clear that there is a
canonical surjection
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X},D)
.

Proposition 7.8
.

\rho_{X}
induces a continuous homomorphism

\rho_{\overline{X}|D}\colon SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X},D).

In particular, there is a homomorphism

\widetilde{\rho}_{\overline{X}|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},1)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X},D)

if
\text{\rm dim}(X)=d
.

Proof.

Using (
5.5
), we can replace
SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
by
C(\overline{X},D)
.
The proof of the proposition is now identical to that of
Proposition
7.3
, where we apply Proposition
7.1
(3) for arbitrary values
of

m\geq 1
instead of
m=1
. For the existence of
\widetilde{\rho}_{\overline{X}|D}
, use the
map
{\rm CH}^{d+1}(\overline{X}|D_{+},1)\to SK_{1}(\overline{X}|D)
in the proof of
Theorem
6.1
(where recall that
D_{+}=D+D_{\rm red}
).
∎

8.
Tame class group and motivic cohomology

The theory of motivic cohomology with compact support for schemes (due to Voevodsky)
will be a key player in our proofs of the main results of this paper.
Since we shall be working with schemes over local fields which may not necessarily be
perfect, we need to properly recall the results in the theory of
motives over imperfect fields that we shall use in this paper. This is the purpose
of this section. We shall conclude the section by establishing an isomorphism
between the tame class group with finite coefficients (prime to the characteristic of
the ground field) and certain motivic cohomology group with compact support.

Throughout this section, we fix a field
k
of characteristic exponent
p\geq 1
.
We let
\Lambda
be a commutative ring which we assume to be
{\mathbb{Z}}
if
p=1
or
any
{\mathbb{Z}}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
-algebra if
p\geq 2
. We shall be more specific about
this algebra as and when required.
We let
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
denote the category of regular
k
-schemes.

8.1.
Review of Voevodsky’s motives

It was shown by Cisinski-Déglise
[
17
]
that
given a Noetherian
k
-scheme
X
, there is a monoidal triangulated category of
mixed motives
{\mathbf{DM}}(X,\Lambda)
which coincides with the original construction of
Voevodsky
[
105
]
when
X
is the spectrum of a perfect field and of Suslin

[
95
]
when
X
is the spectrum of an arbitrary field.

One defines
\underline{{\mathbf{DM}}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)

in the same
manner by replacing the Nisnevich topology on
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
by the cdh topology on

{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{X}
. We let
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X)
be the full localizing triangulated subcategory of

\underline{{\mathbf{DM}}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)
generated by motives of the form
M_{X}(Y)(n)
for

Y\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By the main result of
[
15
]
, the assignment
X\mapsto{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)

satisfies Grothendieck’s six functor formalism (see
[
17
, A.5]
)
in full generality in the category of Noetherian
k
-schemes of finite Krull
dimensions. In particular, it satisfies localization, absolute
purity and continuity.
The constant Nisnevich (resp. cdh) sheaf with transfer associated to the ring
\Lambda
is the identity
object for the monoidal structure of
{\mathbf{DM}}(X,\Lambda)
(resp.
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)
). We shall denote both of these by

\Lambda_{X}
.
We recall the following result from
[
15
, Cor. 5.9]
.

Theorem 8.1
.

The change of topology functor
a^{*}_{\rm cdh}\colon{\mathbf{DM}}(X,\Lambda)\to{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)

is an equivalence of monoidal triangulated categories
if
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
.

Using the continuity property and

[
15
, Lem. 3.17, Prop. 7.1]
(see also
[
17
, Prop. 9.1.14]
),
we have the following result in which we follow the notations of
§
4.5
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Assume that
k
is an imperfect field
and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. Let
i\geq 0
be an integer. Let

f_{i}\colon X^{\heartsuit}\to X_{i},\ f\colon X^{\heartsuit}\to X
and
g_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X
be the
projection maps. Then we have the following.

(1)

Each of the compositions

{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{g^{*}_{i}}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X_{i},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{(g_{i})_{*}}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)\ \mbox{and}

{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X_{i},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{(g_{i})_{*}}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{g^{*}_{i}}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X_{i},\Lambda)

is multiplication by
p^{i}
.

(2)

There is an equivalence of monoidal triangulated categories

2-{{\underset{i\geq 0}{\varinjlim}}}\ {\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X_{i},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda),

where the left hand side is a colimit of 2-categories.
In particular, the pull-back functors

{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{g^{*}_{i}}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X_{i},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{f^{*}_{i}}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda)

are equivalences of monoidal triangulated categories.

For a closed immersion
\iota\colon W\hookrightarrow X
with structure maps

f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
, and
h=f\circ\iota
,
recall the following definitions of motives in

{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)\simeq{\mathbf{DM}}(k,\Lambda)
(e.g., see
[
15
, § 8.5]
).

(8.1)

M_{k}(X)=f_{\sharp}\Lambda_{X}\cong f_{!}f^{!}\Lambda_{k},\ M^{c}_{k}(X)=f_{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}\ \mbox{and}\ M^{W}_{k}(X)=h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k},

where all functors are considered on the premotivic category

X\mapsto{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)
. Recall that
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}(1)={{\mathbb{Z}}_{\rm tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k})}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\rm tr}({\rm Spec\,}(k))}
as a presheaf with transfer
via the identification
{\rm Spec\,}(k)\cong k(\infty)
. In particular,

\Lambda_{k}(1)\cong M_{k}({{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}}/{{\rm Spec\,}(k)})\in{\mathbf{DM}}(k,\Lambda)
.
We shall drop highlighting the base field
k
in the notations of motives once it
is fixed in a context.

Definition 8.3
.

We let

(8.2)

H_{i}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k}(j)[i],M_{k}(X));

H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(M_{k}(X),\Lambda_{k}(j)[i]);

H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(M^{c}_{k}(X),\Lambda_{k}(j)[i]);

H^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(M^{W}_{k}(X),\Lambda_{k}(j)[i]).

The above groups are respectively called the (motivic) homology, cohomology, cohomology
with compact support and cohomology with support in
W
of
X
.
If
X={\rm Spec\,}(A)
is affine, we shall often use
the notation
A
instead of
X
while using these groups.

Lemma 8.4
.

Given a closed immersion
\iota\colon W\hookrightarrow X
with the inclusion

u\colon U\hookrightarrow X
of the complement, there are long exact sequences

(1)\hskip 28.45274pt\cdots\to H^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{u^{*}}H^{i}(U,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{i+1}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))\to\cdots.

(2)\hskip 28.45274pt\cdots\to H^{i}_{c}(U,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{u_{*}}H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}H^{i}_{c}(W,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{i+1}_{c}(U,\Lambda(j))\to\cdots.

Proof.

We let
g=f\circ u
and
h=f\circ\iota
, where
f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)

is the structure map.
We then have a distinguished triangle

\iota_{*}\iota^{!}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}\to f^{!}\Lambda_{k}\to u_{*}u^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}

in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)
by
[
15
, Thm. 5.11]
.
Using the isomorphism
u^{*}\cong u^{!}
and
subsequently applying
f_{*}
, we get a distinguished triangle

M^{c}_{k}(W)\to M^{c}_{k}(X)\to M^{c}_{k}(U)

in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
. This yields the second exact sequence in a standard way.

For the first exact sequence, we use the distinguished triangle

u_{!}u^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}\to f^{!}\Lambda_{k}\to\iota_{*}\iota^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}

in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)
by
[
15
, Thm. 5.11]
.
We again proceed as in the previous case to get a distinguished triangle

M_{k}(U)\to M_{k}(X)\to M^{W}_{k}(X)

in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
. This yields the desired exact sequence of
cohomology groups.
∎

The following description of motivic cohomology groups
will be used in §
9
to compare them with étale cohomology.

Lemma 8.5
.

In the notations of Lemma
8.4
,
there are canonical isomorphisms

H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},f_{*}\Lambda_{X}(j)[i]);

H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},f_{!}\Lambda_{X}(j)[i]);

H^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)}(\iota_{*}\Lambda_{W},\Lambda_{X}(j)[i]).

Proof.

This is well known to experts
and we give a brief sketch. We shall use the notations and
distinguished triangles in
the proof of Lemma
8.4
. Part (1) is a direct consequence
of the adjointness of
(f_{\sharp},f^{*})
and
(f^{*},f_{*})
using the canonical
isomorphism
f^{*}\Lambda_{k}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda_{X}
.
Part (2) follows by using the distinguished
triangles

M_{k}(Z)\to M_{k}(\overline{X})\to M^{c}_{k}(X);\ \ f_{!}\Lambda_{X}\to g_{*}\Lambda_{\overline{X}}\to h_{*}\Lambda_{Z}

in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
and applying (1) to
\overline{X}
and
Z
,
where
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
is a compactification of
X
and

\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
is the complementary reduced closed subscheme.
Part (3) follows by using the distinguished triangles

M_{k}(U)\to M_{k}(X)\to M^{W}_{k}(X);\ \ h_{*}\iota^{!}\Lambda_{X}\to f_{*}\Lambda_{X}\to g_{*}\Lambda_{U}

in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
, the adjoint pair
(\iota_{*},\iota^{!})

and the isomorphism
h^{*}\Lambda_{k}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda_{Z}
.
∎

8.2.
Motivic cohomology via stable homotopy category

We let
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
be the localization of the monoidal stable homotopy category of smooth
schemes over
X
at
p
(see
[
24
, § 2.1]
for its definition).
By its definition, every morphism in
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
-module. One defines

\underline{{\mathbf{SH}}}_{\rm cdh}(X)

in the same
manner by replacing the Nisnevich topology on
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
by the cdh topology on

{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{X}
. We let
{\mathbf{SH}}_{{\rm cdh}}(X)
be the full triangulated
subcategory of
\underline{{\mathbf{SH}}}_{\rm cdh}(X)
generated by the objects

\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}Y_{+}
for
Y\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
.

For
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, let
\Sigma^{i,j}=\Sigma^{i-j}_{s}\circ\Sigma^{j}_{t}
,
where
\Sigma_{s}
and
\Sigma_{t}
are the simplicial and the Tate circles
in
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
, respectively (e.g., see
[
66
, § 2.1]
).
We let
{\mathbb{S}}_{X}
be the sphere spectrum (the unit object) of
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
.
Recall from
[
21
, § 2.2]
that
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
is a spectral category,
i.e., it is a category enriched in spectra. In particular, there is an
S^{1}
-spectrum

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(A,B)
for any
A,B\in{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
such that
one has
\pi_{n}({\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(A,B))\cong{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\Sigma^{n}_{s}A,B)
.
The same is true for
{{\mathbf{SH}}}_{\rm cdh}(X)
.
Recall that the canonical morphism of sites
\epsilon_{X}\colon({\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{X})_{\rm cdh}\to({\mathbf{Sm}}_{k})_{\rm nis}
induces a push-forward triangulated functor

a^{\rm cdh}_{*}\colon{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(X)\to{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
which is a right adjoint of the pull-back
functor
a^{*}_{\rm cdh}
.
Furthermore,
a^{\rm cdh}_{*}
is the localization of
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
with respect to cdh-covers.
In particular,
a^{\rm cdh}_{*}\colon{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(X)\to{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
is a full embedding of
triangulated categories (e.g., see
[
23
, Prop. 2.13]
).

By
[
15
, § 2]
,
forgetting the transfer structure and then taking the

T
-suspension defines a functor
\phi_{X}\colon{{\mathbf{DM}}}(X,\Lambda)\to{{\mathbf{SH}}}(X)

which has a strictly monoidal left adjoint
\psi_{X}
. The latter is uniquely determined
via the left Kan extension by the property that

\psi_{X}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}Y_{+})=f_{\sharp}(\Lambda_{Y})

for any
f\colon Y\to X
in
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
. We let
{\rm H}\Lambda_{X}:=\phi_{X}(\Lambda_{X})
.
If
f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)

is the structure map, then the exchange transformation

f^{*}\phi_{k}\to\phi_{X}f^{*}
induces a canonical map

{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}:=f^{*}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k}\to{\rm H}\Lambda_{X}
.
We similarly
define
{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{X}
and
{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{X/k}\in{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
using the
adjoint pair

(\psi_{X},\phi_{X})\colon\underline{{\mathbf{DM}}}_{{\rm cdh}}(X,\Lambda)\to\underline{{\mathbf{SH}}}_{{\rm cdh}}(X)
which has the property that

\psi_{X}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}Y_{+})=f_{\sharp}(\Lambda_{Y})

for any
f\colon Y\to X
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{X}
.
Note that
{\rm H}\Lambda_{{(-)}/k}
and
{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{{(-)}/k}
are absolute ring spectra
functors on
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}

(see
[
77
, Defn.1.1]
for the definition of absolute ring spectra).
It is clear from the above description of
\phi_{X}
that

a^{*}_{\rm cdh}\circ\phi_{X}=\phi_{X}\circ a^{*}_{\rm cdh}
on the subcategory of compact
objects in
{\mathbf{DM}}(X,\Lambda)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

The map
{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}\to{\rm H}\Lambda_{X}
is an isomorphism
if
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
.
In particular,
{\rm H}\Lambda_{(-)}
is an absolute ring spectrum functor on
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
. Moreover, there is a canonical isomorphism
a^{*}_{\rm cdh}({\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\cong{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{X/k}
for
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from
[
15
, Prop. 3.10]
. To prove the second claim,
it suffices to show the isomorphism

a^{*}_{\rm cdh}({\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\cong{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{k}
.
But this is well known, e.g., follows directly from the isomorphism

a^{*}_{\rm cdh}\circ\phi_{k}(\Lambda_{k})\cong\phi_{k}\circ a^{*}_{\rm cdh}(\Lambda_{k}):={\rm H}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k}
, observed above.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

The canonical map
{\rm H}\Lambda_{k}\to a^{\rm cdh}_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By
[
23
, Prop. 2.7]
, it suffices to show that the map

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\Sigma^{i,j}a^{\rm cdh}_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k})

is an isomorphism for every
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
.
To show this, we note that

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})&{\cong}^{1}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}(k,\Lambda)}(f_{\sharp}\Lambda_{X},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&{\cong}^{2}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(f_{\sharp}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{X},\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k}(j)[i])\\
&{\cong}^{3}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(a^{*}_{\rm cdh}\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k})\\
&{\cong}^{4}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\Sigma^{i,j}a^{\rm cdh}_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k}),\end{array}

where
{\cong}^{1}
and
{\cong}^{3}
follow from the adjointness of
(\psi_{k},\phi_{k})
,

{\cong}^{2}
follows from
[
15
, Cor. 5.9]
and
{\cong}^{4}
follows from the
adjointness of
(a^{*}_{\rm cdh},a^{\rm cdh}_{*})
. This concludes the proof.
∎

For a closed immersion
\iota\colon W\hookrightarrow X
with structure maps

f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
, and
h=f\circ\iota
, we let

(8.3)

E^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k});

E^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}({\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}f_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k});

E^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{*}{\mathbb{S}}_{W},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}).

Lemma 8.8
.

We have the following canonical isomorphisms.

(1)

H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\cong}E^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))
.

(2)

H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\cong}E^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))
if
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
.

(3)

H^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))=E^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j))
if
X,W\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
.

Proof.

To prove (1), we use
[
66
, Thm. 2.14]

(see also the proof of Prop. 2.13 of op. cit.), which says that there are canonical
weak equivalences

(8.4)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}a^{*}_{\rm cdh}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{X},\phi_{k}(\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])),\\
\end{array}

where the second weak equivalence follows from Lemma
8.6
.
The induced maps between the homotopy groups yield a canonical
isomorphism

(8.5)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{X},\phi_{k}(\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])).

On the other hand, via the equivalences
{\mathbf{DM}}(k,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\underline{{\mathbf{DM}}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
(see
[
15
, Prop. 8.1]
), there are isomorphisms

(8.6)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{X},\phi_{k}(\Lambda_{k}(j)[i]))&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{\underline{{\mathbf{SH}}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\phi_{k}(\Lambda_{k}(j)[i]))\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{\underline{{\mathbf{DM}}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(\psi_{k}\Sigma^{\infty}_{T}X_{+},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathbf{DM}}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(f_{\sharp}\Lambda_{X},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(M_{k}(X),\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j)).\end{array}

Combining (
8.5
) and (
8.6
), we get (1).

To prove (2), we let
u\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be an open immersion such that

\overline{X}
is proper over
k
with the structure map
g\colon\overline{X}\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
.
We then get

(8.7)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}({\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}f_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}({\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}g_{!}u_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}({\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}g_{*}u_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({\overline{X}})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}u_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}).\end{array}

Since
a^{\rm cdh}_{*}
is a full embedding, (
8.4
) implies that

(8.8)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({\overline{X}})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{{\overline{X}}/k})&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}a^{*}_{\rm cdh}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}a^{\rm cdh}_{*}a^{*}_{\rm cdh}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\simeq}&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k}),\\
\end{array}

where the third isomorphism follows from Lemmas
8.6
and

8.7
.

To proceed further,
we let
Z=\overline{X}\setminus X
, endowed with the reduced closed subscheme
structure. Let
v\colon Z\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion and
h=g\circ v
.
Using the canonical isomorphism
t_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{Y}\cong t_{*}t^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

(for
Y\in\{\overline{X},Z\}
and
t\in\{g,h\}
) and
the distinguished triangle
[
6
, § 4.5.3]

v_{*}v^{!}g^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\to g^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\to u_{*}u^{!}g^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

in
{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(\overline{X})
, we obtain a distinguished triangle

(8.9)

h_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z}\to g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}}\to f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

in
{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)
.
In particular, a distinguished triangle

(8.10)

a^{\rm cdh}_{*}h_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z}\to a^{\rm cdh}_{*}g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}}\to a^{\rm cdh}_{*}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

in
{\mathbf{SH}}(k)
.

We also have the distinguished triangle in
{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})
by
[
6
, § 4.5.3]

u_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}\to{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k}\to v_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda_{Z/k}.

We now consider the induced commutative diagram of
S^{1}
-spectra

(8.11)

\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({\overline{X}})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({\overline{X}})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}v_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda_{Z/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}h_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k}),}

whose vertical arrows are given by (
8.8
). In particular,
they are weak equivalences.
Taking the homotopy fibers of the two rows, we get a canonical weak equivalence of

S^{1}
-spectra

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({\overline{X}})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}u_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k}).

The induced homomorphisms between the homotopy groups
yield a canonical isomorphism

(8.12)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}({\overline{X}})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}u_{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k}).

We now note that since
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
, the premotivic categories

X\mapsto{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(X)
and
X\mapsto{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X)
satisfy the isomorphisms

\psi_{k}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}{\cong}f_{*}\psi_{X}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\cong f_{*}f^{!}\psi_{k}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\cong f_{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k},

where the first three isomorphisms follow from (the cdh analogue of)

[
15
, Cor. 3.2]
.
We thus get

(8.13)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(k)}(a^{\rm cdh}_{*}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}a^{\rm cdh}_{*}a^{*}_{\rm cdh}{\rm H}\Lambda_{k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda^{\rm cdh}_{k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(\psi_{k}f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(f_{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\cong&H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j)),\\
\end{array}

where the first isomorphism follows Lemma
8.7
and the
second isomorphism follows from the fully faithfulness of
a^{\rm cdh}_{*}
.
Combining (
8.13
) with (
8.7
) and
(
8.12
), we get (2).

To prove (3), we let
w\colon U\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of the complement.
We let
g\colon U\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
and
h\colon W\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
be the
structure maps. We then have a canonical weak equivalence

(8.14)

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(U)}({\mathbb{S}}_{U},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{U/k})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}w_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda_{U/k}).

We also have isomorphisms

(8.15)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{*}{\mathbb{S}}_{W},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})&\xrightarrow{\cong}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{W},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(W)}({\mathbb{S}}_{W},\Sigma^{i,j}\iota^{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}).\end{array}

We next recall that there is a distinguished triangle

(8.16)

\iota_{*}\iota^{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}\to{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}\to w_{*}{\rm H}\Lambda_{U/k}

in
{\mathbf{SH}}(X)
by
[
6
]
.
Using the canonical isomorphism
t_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{Y}\cong t_{*}t^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

(for
Y\in\{X,U\}
and
t\in\{f,g\}
) and
the distinguished triangle

w_{!}w^{!}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\to f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\to\iota_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

in
{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(X)
,
we also obtain a distinguished triangle

(8.17)

g_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{U}\to f_{\sharp}{\mathbb{S}}_{{X}}\to h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}

in
{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)
.

Using (
8.14
), (
8.16
),
(
8.17
) and
comparing (
8.4
) for
X
and
U
, we get a weak equivalence

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{k}).

The induced maps between the homotopy groups yields a canonical isomorphism

(8.18)

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}({\mathbb{S}}_{X},\Sigma^{i,j}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{k}).

On the other hand, using the regularity of
X
and
Z
, (the cdh analogue of)

[
15
, Cor. 3.2]
implies that

\psi_{k}h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\cong h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}\psi_{k}{\mathbb{S}}_{k}\cong h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}.

In particular,

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda^{{\rm cdh}}_{k})&\xrightarrow{\cong}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}_{\rm cdh}(k)}(h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{k},\phi_{k}(\Lambda_{k}(j)[i]))\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(\psi_{k}h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)}(h_{!}\iota^{*}f^{!}\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{k}(j)[i])\\
&\xrightarrow{\cong}&H^{i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(j)).\end{array}

We now use (
8.15
) and (
8.18
) to
conclude the proof of (3), and of the lemma.
∎

8.3.
Gysin homomorphisms

In this subsection, we shall construct very general Gysin homomorphisms for the
cohomology theory
E^{*}(-,\Lambda(\bullet))
.

Let
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be a regular closed
immersion of pure codimension
n
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, i.e.,
Z
is locally defined
by a regular sequence of length
n
on
X
. Let
W\subset Z
be a closed subset.
Since
{\rm H}\Lambda_{{(-)}/{k}}
is an oriented absolute spectrum,
it follows from
[
77
, Defn. 2.8]
(see also
[
21
, Thm. 3.2.21]
)
that there is a fundamental class
\eta^{X}_{Z}\in E^{2n}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n))

and a cup product pairing

\bigcup\colon E^{i}_{W}(Z,\Lambda(j))\times E^{2n}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n))\to E^{2n+i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(n+j))
(see
[
77
, Defn. 1.6]
) which together give
rise to the refined Gysin homomorphism

(8.19)

\iota_{*}\colon E^{i}_{W}(Z,\Lambda(j))\to E^{2n+i}_{W}(X,\Lambda(n+j));\ \ \alpha\mapsto\alpha\cup\eta^{X}_{Z}.

This map is linear with respect to the action of the (relative) motivic cohomology
ring of
X
. Furthermore, for a flat morphism
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
of
k
-schemes
with
Z^{\prime}=Z\times_{X}X^{\prime}
,
one has
\eta^{X^{\prime}}_{Z^{\prime}}=f^{*}(\eta^{X}_{Z})
(see
[
77
, Prop. 2.10]
).
In particular, the refined Gysin map commutes with the flat
pull-back map
f^{*}
on the cohomology of
Z
and
X
with support in
W
.

Recall that when
k
is a perfect field and
X
is a regular
k
-scheme, then
there is a natural duality isomorphism

\Phi_{X}\colon M^{c}(X)^{*}(d)[2d]\xrightarrow{\cong}M(X)

(see
[
56
, Thm. 5.3.18(iii)]
).
Furthermore, if
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
is a closed immersion of regular
k
-schemes of
pure codimension
n
, then one has a purity isomorphism
M_{Z}(X)\cong M(Z)(n)[2n]

(e.g., see
[
17
, (2.4.39.1)]
).

When
Z
is moreover complete (e.g., a closed point of
X
),
the duality and purity isomorphisms yield the Gysin homomorphism

\iota_{*}\colon H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\to H^{2n+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(n+j))
which is dual to the
push-forward homomorphism
\iota_{*}\colon H_{d-n-i}(Z,\Lambda(d-n-j))\to H_{d-n-i}(X,\Lambda(d-n-j))
. In other words, there is a commutative diagram

(8.20)

\textstyle{H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2d-2n-i}(Z,\Lambda(d-n-j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(n+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{2d-2n-i}(X,\Lambda(d-n-j)).}

If
k
is not perfect, we shall need Gysin homomorphism even when

Z
is not regular. The purity theorem does not hold in this case. However, the
notions of fundamental class and refined Gysin map of (
8.19
) allow us
to show the existence of the Gysin homomorphism for
E
-theory without assuming purity.
This is the context of the following result.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be a regular closed immersion of pure codimension
n
in

{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
. Assume that
Z
is complete. Then there is a natural Gysin homomorphism

\iota_{*}\colon E^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\to E^{2n+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(n+j)).

Proof.

By the construction of the refined Gysin homomorphism, it suffices to show that the
forget support map
E^{i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(j))\to E^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))
factors through

E^{i}_{c}(Z,\Lambda(j))
.
To show this, we choose a compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
and let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion. Let
\iota^{\prime}\colon W=\overline{X}\setminus X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be
the inclusion of the complementary closed subset, endowed with the reduced subscheme
structure. We let
\tau=j\circ\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
.

Now, we note that
