\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{*}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},j^{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(X)}(\iota_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},j^{!}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(j_{!}\iota_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{*}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k}),\end{array}

where the last isomorphism uses
\tau_{!}\cong\tau_{*}
, thanks to the completeness of

Z
. It follows that there is a canonical (excision) isomorphism

(8.21)

E^{i}_{Z}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\cong}E^{i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(j)).

Note also that the map

E^{i}_{Z}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))\to E^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))
factors through

E^{i}_{Z}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))\to E^{i}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))
.
Hence, we are reduced to showing that the map

E^{i}_{Z}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))\to E^{i}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))
factors through

E^{i}_{c}({X},\Lambda(j))
.

Next, we claim that the composite

(8.22)

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\to{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\hskip 85.35826pt

\hskip 199.16928pt\to{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k})

is null-homotopic, where the first arrow is induced by the unit of adjunction

{\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}}\to\tau_{*}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z}\cong\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z}
.

To prove the claim, we note that there is a commutative diagram

(8.23)

\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k}).}

It suffices therefore to show that

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k})

is contractible.

Finally, we observe that there are canonical weak equivalences of
spectra

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k})&\cong&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(Z)}({\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\tau^{!}\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k})\\
&\cong&{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(Z)}({\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\beta_{*}\alpha^{!}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k}),\end{array}

where
\alpha\colon W\times_{\overline{X}}Z\to W
and
\beta\colon W\times_{\overline{X}}Z\to Z
are the canonical maps. The claim now follows because

W\times_{\overline{X}}Z=\emptyset
.

Using the claim and the distinguished triangle of spectra (e.g., see

[
21
, Prop. 2.2.10]
)

(8.24)

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},j_{!}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\to{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\hskip 85.35826pt

\hskip 227.62204pt\to{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\iota^{\prime}_{*}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{W/k}),

we see that the map

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\to{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})

factors through

(8.25)

{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{{\overline{X}}/k})\to{\rm Maps}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},j_{!}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}).

Considering the induced maps on
\pi_{0}
, we get the factorization

(8.26)

E^{i}_{Z}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j))\to E^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))\to E^{i}(\overline{X},\Lambda(j)),

as desired. This concludes the proof.
∎

8.4.
A functorial property of Gysin map

One knows that the Gysin homomorphism commutes with the pull-back homomorphism on
motivic cohomology in a transverse square. But we need to know the relation between
these two homomorphisms when the square is not Cartesian.
We consider a special case of this situation in this subsection.

We assume that
k
is a perfect field. We first assume that

X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
is an integral scheme of dimension one. We let
Z\subset X
be a
closed subscheme such that
Z_{\rm red}
is a closed point
x
of
X
.
Let
S={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
and let
S\xrightarrow{h}Z\xrightarrow{g}X
be the
inclusions. Write
f=gh
and
e=\ell({\mathcal{O}}(Z))
. Assume that
g
is a regular
closed immersion. Since
X
is regular,
f
is also a regular closed immersion.
We thus have a diagram

(8.27)

\textstyle{E^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{*}}
\scriptstyle{h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{E^{2+i}_{S}(X,\Lambda(1+j))}
\textstyle{E^{i}(S,\Lambda(j)).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
m
be an integer prime to
p
and
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
.
Then we have the identity
g_{*}=e(f_{*}\circ h^{*})
.

Proof.

Suppose that we know the equality
\eta^{X}_{Z}=e\eta^{X}_{S}
.
Then, for any
i,j
and

\alpha\in E^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))
, we get

g_{*}(\alpha)=\alpha\cup\eta^{X}_{Z}=h^{*}(\alpha)\cup\eta^{X}_{Z}=h^{*}(\alpha)\cup e\eta^{X}_{S}=ef_{*}(h^{*}(\alpha)),

where the second identity follows from the construction of the
cup product in
[
77
, Defn. 1.6, p. 509]
and the isomorphism (of the unit of
adjunction)
{\mathbb{S}}_{Z}\xrightarrow{\cong}h_{*}{\mathbb{S}}_{S}
in
{\mathbf{SH}}(Z)
.
It remains to show that
\eta^{X}_{Z}=e\eta^{X}_{S}
. By definition of the
fundamental classes (see
[
77
, Defn. 2.1]
), this is equivalent to showing that

c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z})=e(c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{S}))
, where
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{D})
denotes the ‘Chern class with
support’ of the divisor
D\subset X
in
E^{2}_{D}(X,\Lambda(1))
. We are now done by

[
77
, Prop. 1.43]
because
{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k}\cong{\rm H}\Lambda_{X}
is an
oriented absolute ring spectrum
with additive group law
(e.g., see
[
15
, Rem. 3.7]
).
∎

Suppose now that
k
is an imperfect field and
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
is an integral scheme
of dimension
d\geq 1
. We let
x\in X
be a closed point and let
Z={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\times_{X}X^{\heartsuit}
. We let
y
be the unique point in
X^{\heartsuit}
lying over
x

so that
{\rm Spec\,}(k(y))=Z_{\rm red}
. Let
e=\ell({\mathcal{O}}(Z))
. Let
u\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X
and
v\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(y))\hookrightarrow X^{\heartsuit}
be the inclusions.
Let
f\colon X^{\heartsuit}\to X
and
g\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(y))\to{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
be the
projections. We have the refined Gysin maps

u_{*}\colon E^{i}(k(x),\Lambda(j))\to E^{2d+i}_{x}(X,\Lambda(d+j))
and

v_{*}\colon E^{i}(k(y),\Lambda(j))\to E^{2d+i}_{y}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+j))
.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
m
be an integer prime to
p
and
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
. Then
e\in\Lambda^{\times}

and one has a commutative
diagram

(8.28)

\textstyle{E^{i}(k(x),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{*}}
\scriptstyle{eg^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{x}(X,\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{i}(k(y),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{y}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+j)).}

Proof.

By the Bertini theorem of Altman-Kleiman
[
3
, Thm. 1]
, we can find an integral
curve
C\subset X
which passes through
x
and is smooth over
k
.
We now consider the diagram

(8.29)

\textstyle{E^{i}(k(x),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{eg^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2+i}_{x}(C,\Lambda(1+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{x}(X,\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{i}(k(y),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{2+i}_{y}(C^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(1+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{y}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+j)),}

where the middle and the right vertical arrows are the pull-back maps,
and the horizontal arrows are the refined Gysin maps of (
8.19
).
We note that
C^{\heartsuit}=C\times_{X}X^{\heartsuit}
. It follows from

[
77
, Prop. 1.8, (1) and (3)]
that
u_{*}
(resp.
v_{*}
) is the composition of
the two upper (resp. lower) horizontal arrows, and the right square is commutative.
It suffices therefore to show that the left square is commutative. This allows us to
assume that
d=1
.

We now let
{\rm Spec\,}(k(y))\xrightarrow{\iota}Z\xrightarrow{\tau}X^{\heartsuit}
be the
inclusions and let
h\colon Z\to{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
be the projection.
Since
f
is flat, it follows that
\tau
is a regular closed immersion.
We consider the diagram

(8.30)

\textstyle{E^{i}(k(x),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{*}}
\scriptstyle{eg^{*}}
\scriptstyle{h^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2+i}_{x}(X,\Lambda(1+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{i}(k(y),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2+i}_{y}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(1+j)).}

Since
Z={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\times_{X}X^{\heartsuit}
, the argument for the commutativity of the right
square in (
8.29
) also shows that
f^{*}\circ u_{*}=\tau_{*}\circ h^{*}
.
Since
g^{*}=\iota^{*}\circ h^{*}
, it suffices to show that

\tau_{*}=e(v_{*}\circ\iota^{*})
. But this follows from Lemma
8.10
.
It remains to show that
e\in\Lambda^{\times}
.

Now, we have shown in the proof of Lemma
4.21
that there exists a
finite purely inseparable extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
and a unique closed point
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}:=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
such that
{\rm Spec\,}(k(y))={\rm Spec\,}(k(y^{\prime}))\times_{X^{\prime}}X^{\heartsuit}
. It follows then from Lemma
4.16
that

e=\ell({\mathcal{O}}(Z^{\prime}))
, where
Z^{\prime}={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\times_{X}X^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, we know that

[k^{\prime}:k]=\text{\rm dim}_{k(x)}({\mathcal{O}}(Z^{\prime}))=[k(y^{\prime}):k(x)]\ell({\mathcal{O}}(Z^{\prime})).

where the second equality follows from
[
29
, Lem. A.1.3]
. Since
[k^{\prime}:k]
is a power of
p
, it follows that so is

\ell({\mathcal{O}}(Z^{\prime}))
and we are done.
∎

Corollary 8.12
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of Lemma
8.11
.
Then one has a commutative diagram

(8.31)

\textstyle{H^{i}(k(x),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{*}}
\scriptstyle{eg^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}(k(y),\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+i}_{c}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+j)).}

Proof.

We choose an integral compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
. Let

\iota^{\heartsuit}\colon Z\hookrightarrow X^{\heartsuit},\ j^{\prime}\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
and

j^{\heartsuit}\colon X^{\heartsuit}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}
be the
inclusions, where
Z={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\times_{X}X^{\heartsuit}
. We set

\tau^{\heartsuit}=j^{\heartsuit}\circ\iota^{\heartsuit}
.
In view of Lemmas
8.8
,
8.9
and
8.11
,
we only need to show that the diagram

(8.32)

\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{x}(X,\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{Z}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{2d+i}_{c}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+j))}

is commutative, where the horizontal arrows are constructed in the proof of
Lemma
8.9
. But this is equivalent to the commutativity of
the diagram

(8.33)

\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}(\tau_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{x},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{\overline{X}/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},j^{\prime}_{!}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X}^{\heartsuit})}(\tau^{\heartsuit}_{!}{\mathbb{S}}_{Z},\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{{\overline{X}^{\heartsuit}}/k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{SH}}(\overline{X}^{\heartsuit})}({\mathbb{S}}_{\overline{X}},j^{\heartsuit}_{!}\Sigma^{i,j}{\rm H}\Lambda_{X^{\heartsuit}/k}),}

which is clear by the compatibility of the lower shriek functors with the
pull-back (e.g., see
[
17
, § A.5.1(5)]
).
∎

8.5.
Milnor
K
-theory and motivic cohomology

Let
\alpha=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}\in K^{M}_{n}(k)
be a Steinberg symbol.
Then each
f_{i}
defines a morphism
f_{i}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k)\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m,k}
.
Taking the induced map between the motives and using

[
17
, Prop. 11.2.11]
, we get a
morphism
f_{i}\colon{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}(1)[1]
in
{\mathbf{DM}}(k,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Taking the tensor product over
1\leq i\leq n
, we get a morphism

\alpha\colon{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}(n)[n]
in
{\mathbf{DM}}(k,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Equivalently,
\alpha
defines a unique element
\Psi_{k}(\alpha)\in H^{n}(k,{\mathbb{Z}}(n))
. Hence, we get a map (a priori, only of sets)

(8.34)

\Psi_{k}\colon K^{M}_{1}(k)\times\cdots\times K^{M}_{1}(k)\to H^{n}(k,{\mathbb{Z}}(n)).

This is clearly natural with respect to the pull-back maps via inclusion of fields.
For
n=1
, the following result follows by combining

[
15
, Prop. 8.1, Cor. 8.2]
and
[
17
, Thm. 11.2.14]
.
It provides the base case of the more general isomorphism (with prime-to-
p

coefficients) between the
tame class group and a motivic cohomology group with compact support for smooth
schemes over a local field that we shall prove in the next subsection.

Lemma 8.13
.

For any integer
m
prime to
p
and
n\geq 0
, the map

\Psi_{k}
induces a canonical group isomorphism

(8.35)

\Psi_{k}\colon{K^{M}_{n}(k)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{n}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))

which is natural with respect to the pull-back maps on both sides via inclusion of
fields.

Proof.

If
k
is perfect, it is well known that
\Psi_{k}
, as defined in
(
8.34
),
induces an isomorphism
\Psi_{k}\colon{K^{M}_{n}(k)}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{n}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(n))
.
Since
H^{n+1}(k,{\mathbb{Z}}(n))\cong{\rm CH}^{n}(k,n-1)=0
(e.g., see
[
106
]
),
the universal coefficient theorem
implies that the change of coefficients map

{H^{n}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(n))}/m\to H^{n}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))

is an isomorphism. This implies (
8.35
) when
k
is perfect.

Suppose now that
k
is not perfect and look at the diagram

(8.36)

\textstyle{{K^{M}_{n}(k)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{n}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}}
\textstyle{{K^{M}_{n}(k^{\heartsuit})}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{k^{\heartsuit}}}
\textstyle{H^{n}(k^{\heartsuit},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n)),}

where
\pi\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\heartsuit})\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
is the projection.
We have seen that
\Psi_{k}
is defined on the Steinberg symbols in
K^{M}_{n}(k)
and
the diagram commutes on these symbols.
The right vertical arrow is an isomorphism by Proposition
8.2
and the left vertical
arrow is an isomorphism by Lemma
4.24
. Since
\Psi_{k^{\heartsuit}}

is an isomorphism, we conclude that
\Psi_{k}
, as defined in
(
8.34
),
induces a map
\Psi_{k}\colon{K^{M}_{n}(k)}/m\to H^{n}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))
, which is
an isomorphism of groups.
∎

8.6.
Motivic cohomology and idele class group

Assume that
k
is an infinite field of characteristic exponent
p>0
and
let
\Lambda^{\prime}:={\mathbb{Z}}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
.
Let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
be integral of dimension
d
. We fix an integer
m
prime to
p

and let
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
. The goal of this subsection is to
prove an isomorphism between the mod-
m
idele class group of
X
and

H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))
. This will play a key role in the proofs of the main
results of the paper.

Lemma 8.14
.

Assume that
k
is perfect. Then the change of coefficients homomorphism

{H_{-1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1))}/m\to H_{-1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(-1))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We can replace
{\mathbb{Z}}
by
\Lambda^{\prime}
.
By the universal coefficient theorem, it
suffices to show that
H_{-2}(X,{\Lambda}^{\prime}(-1))=0
.
By
[
56
, Thm. 5.3.18(iii)]
, this is equivalent to showing that

H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{\Lambda}^{\prime}(d+1))=0
.
To this end, we choose an integral compactification

\overline{X}
of
X
and let
Z=\overline{X}\setminus X
with the reduced closed subscheme
structure. We then have the localization exact sequence

H^{2d+1}(Z,\Lambda^{\prime}(d+1))\to H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,\Lambda^{\prime}(d+1))\to H^{2d+2}(\overline{X},\Lambda^{\prime}(d+1)).

The terms on the two ends of this exact sequence are zero by

[
66
, Thm. 5.1]
.
The lemma therefore follows.
∎

Proposition 8.15
.

There is an isomorphism

\Psi_{X}\colon{C(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))

such that the diagram

(8.37)

\textstyle{{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{k(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{x}_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{x}_{*}}
\textstyle{{C(X)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))}

is commutative for every inclusion
\iota^{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X
of
a closed point.

Proof.

By Lemmas
5.6
and
5.8
,
we can replace
{C(X)}/m
by
{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m
. We shall work with the latter
group throughout the proof.

We first assume that
k
is perfect. For any
x\in X_{(0)}
, we let

\Theta_{k(x)}
denote the composition of the isomorphisms

(8.38)

K^{M}_{1}(k(x))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{\Psi_{k(x)}}H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda^{\prime}(1))\xrightarrow{\Phi_{k(x)}}H_{-1}(k(x),\Lambda^{\prime}(-1)),

where
\Phi_{k(x)}
is the duality isomorphism (see (
8.20
)).
We then get a diagram

(8.39)

\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}H_{-1}(k(x),\Lambda(-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{-1}(X,\Lambda(-1)),}

where the top horizontal arrows are from (
8.38
), the left vertical
arrow is the canonical surjection, the middle one is the sum of Gysin maps
(see Lemmas
8.8
and
8.9
) and
the right vertical arrow is the sum of push-forward maps. The right square is
commutative by (
8.20
).
It follows from
[
111
, Thm. 1.3]
and Lemma
8.14
that the composition of
the right vertical arrow with the two top horizontal arrows induces
\Theta_{X}
which
is an isomorphism. Since
\Phi_{X}
is an isomorphism, it follows that there is a unique
isomorphism
\Psi_{X}
such that the left square commutes.

Suppose now that
k
is not perfect and let
f\colon X^{\heartsuit}\to X
be the projection
map. For
x\in X_{(0)}
and the unique point
y\in X^{\heartsuit}
lying over
x
, we let

f^{*}_{x}\colon H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\to H^{1}(k(y),\Lambda(1))
be given by

f^{*}_{x}(a)=\ell(S_{x})\tau^{*}_{x}(a)
, where
S_{x}={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\times_{X}X^{\heartsuit}
and

\tau_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(y))\to{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
is the projection map.
The map
f^{*}_{x}\colon{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\to{K^{M}_{1}(k(y))}/m
has been earlier defined in
the same manner in (
4.12
).
We let
\widetilde{f}^{*}={\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}f^{*}_{x}
.
We let
\alpha={\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}\Psi_{k(x)}

(see Lemma
8.13
). The map
\alpha^{\heartsuit}
is defined in the similar
manner.

We now consider the diagram

(8.40)

\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}^{*}}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}^{*}}
\textstyle{{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X^{\heartsuit}_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\heartsuit}}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in X^{\heartsuit}_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{C^{\rm t}(X^{\heartsuit})}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X^{\heartsuit}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X^{\heartsuit},\Lambda(d+1)).}

We have shown above that
\Psi_{X^{\heartsuit}}
is an isomorphism. The right vertical arrow of
the front face is an isomorphism by Proposition
8.2
. Its left vertical arrow is an
isomorphism by Corollary
4.23
. It follows that there is a unique
isomorphism
\Psi_{X}\colon{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))

such that the front face of the diagram commutes. To complete the proof of the
proposition, it remains to show that the top face of (
8.40
) commutes.
Since the right vertical arrow of the front face is an isomorphism, it suffices to show
that all other faces of (
8.40
) commute.

Now, the left face of (
8.40
) commutes by the construction of the
pull-back map
f^{*}
between the tame class groups. The right face commutes by
Corollary
8.12
.
The back face commutes by definitions of all its arrows. The bottom face commutes by
definition of
\Psi_{X^{\heartsuit}}
(see the diagram (
8.39
)). The front face
has been shown to commute. This concludes the proof.
∎

9.
Reciprocity and étale realization

The goal of this section is to describe the reciprocity homomorphism of the tame
class group as the realization map from the motivic cohomology with compact
support to the étale cohomology with compact support.
This will allow us to paraphrase the statements of some of the main results of this
paper as statements about the étale realization map.
We begin with a rapid recollection of the étale realization functor.

9.1.
Étale realization of motivic cohomology

Let
k
be a field of characteristic exponent
p\geq 1
.
We fix an integer
m\geq 1
prime to
p
and let
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
.
We recall the following from
[
15
, § 9]
and
[
16
, § 1,2]
.
For any
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, let
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
be the monoidal triangulated
category of étale sheaves of
\Lambda
-modules with transfer on
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
.
Let
{\mathbf{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
be
the unbounded derived category of étale sheaves of
\Lambda
-modules on
X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
.
There is a commutative diagram of triangulated categories

(9.1)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DM}}(X,\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{\prime*}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm h}(X,\Lambda),}

where
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm h}(X,\Lambda)
is the triangulated category of étale motives with
respect to Voevodsky’s h-topology
[
104
, § 3]
.
The functor
\tau_{X}
is obtained by forgetting the transfer
structure and the remaining functors are induced by sheafification with respect
to the underlying topologies on their targets.

One knows that
\omega^{*}_{X}
are
\tau_{X}
are equivalences of monoidal triangulated
categories (e.g., see
[
16
, Thm. 4.5.2, Cor. 5.5.4]
),
essentially because every étale sheaf on
X
with
\Lambda
-coefficients is
canonically equipped with a transfer structure and the Tate object in

{\mathbf{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
is invertible. Furthermore, étale sheaves of

\Lambda
-module satisfy h-descent.
The equivalence of
\omega^{*}_{X}
implies that

\epsilon^{\prime*}_{X}
factors through

(9.2)

\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X,\Lambda)\to{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)

such that two triangles in (
9.1
) commute.
Both categories in (
9.2
) are equipped with Grothendieck’s six operations
(e.g., see
[
17
, Thm. 2.4.50]
) and the functor
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
is
compatible with these operations when restricted to constructible objects (see
[
15
, Rem. 9.6]
).
Henceforth, we shall make no distinction between

{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
,
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm h}(X,\Lambda)
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
in order to
simplify notations.

For any
f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
and a closed immersion

\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
, we let

(9.3)

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},f_{*}\Lambda_{X}(j)[i])

H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},f_{!}\Lambda_{X}(j)[i])

H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Z}(X,\Lambda(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)}(\iota_{*}\Lambda_{Z},\Lambda_{X}(j)[i]).

Since
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
is monoidal, Lemma
8.5
provides
natural étale realization maps

(9.4)

H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda(j));\ \ H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(j));

H^{i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(j))\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Z}(X,\Lambda(j)).

Remark 9.1
.

If we let
{\mathbf{SH}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X)
denote the stable homotopy category of simplicial sheaves on

{\mathbf{Sm}}_{X}
with respect to the étale topology (e.g., see
[
23
, § 2]
),
then we have an adjoint
pair of functors
(\psi_{X},\phi_{X})\colon{\mathbf{SH}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X)\to{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
as
in the Nisnevich and cdh cases. Furthermore, one can check that the analogue of
Lemma
8.8
(with the same proof) holds in the étale setting
too.

Before we proceed further, we recall the following.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
k_{0}
be any field and
X
any Noetherian
k_{0}
-scheme with structure map

f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k_{0})
. Then the canonical map
f^{*}\mu_{m,k_{0}}\to\mu_{m,X}
is an
isomorphism for all integers
m\in k_{0}^{*}
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that
f^{*}\mu_{m,k_{0}}\to\mu_{m,X}
is injective. To prove its
surjectivity,
it suffices to show that for any
k_{0}
-algebra
A
and
\alpha\in\mu_{m}(A)
, there is
a factorization
k_{0}\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phi}A
and
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mu_{m}(A^{\prime})
such
that
A^{\prime}
is étale over
k_{0}
and
\phi(\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha
.
To show this, we let
\phi^{\prime}\colon k_{0}[t]\to A
be the
k_{0}
-algebra homomorphism
defined by setting
\phi^{\prime}(t)=\alpha
. Then
\phi^{\prime}
has a factorization

\phi\colon\frac{k_{0}[t]}{(t^{m}-1)}\to A
. Since
m\in k^{\times}_{0}
, it follows that

A^{\prime}:={k_{0}[t]}/{(t^{m}-1)}
is étale over
k_{0}
. Letting
\alpha^{\prime}
be residue
class of
t
in
A^{\prime}
, we conclude the proof.
∎

For any
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we let
\Lambda^{\prime}(j)
be the sheaf on
X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
as defined in

[
74
, § V.1, p. 163]
.

Lemma 9.3
.

For any
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, the functor
\tau_{X}
induces an isomorphism

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda(j))\to H^{i}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\Lambda^{\prime}(j)).

The same holds for cohomology with compact support and cohomology with support
in a closed subscheme.

Proof.

The second part of the lemma follows from its first part using the long exact
sequences of cohomology and naturality of
\tau_{X}
.
So we only need to prove the first part. By (
9.3
), it suffices to show that
the natural map
\Lambda^{\prime}_{X}(j)\to\tau_{X}(\Lambda_{X}(j))
is an isomorphism.
Let
k_{0}
denote the prime field of
k
and let
f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k_{0})
be the
projection map. Since the pull-back map
f^{*}\Lambda_{k_{0}}(j)\to\Lambda_{X}(j)

is clearly an isomorphism, and the same holds for
\Lambda^{\prime}(j)
by
Lemma
9.2
, it suffices to prove the above isomorphism when
X={\rm Spec\,}(k_{0})
.
However, as
k_{0}
is a perfect field, this isomorphism is well known
(e.g., see
[
72
, Prop. 10.6]
).
∎

9.2.
Gysin homomorphisms in motivic and étale
cohomology

Let
k
and
\Lambda
be as above.
Suppose now that
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
is a closed immersion of
pure codimension
n
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Reg}}}_{k}
.
Recall from §
8.3
(see Lemma
8.8
) that
there is a fundamental class
\eta^{X}_{Z}\in H^{2n}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n))

such that the cup product

\bigcup\colon H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\times H^{2n}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n))\to H^{2n+i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n+j))
gives rise to the refined Gysin homomorphism

(9.5)

\iota_{*}\colon H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\to H^{2n+i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n+j));\ \ \alpha\mapsto\alpha\cup\eta^{X}_{Z}.

This construction of the refined Gysin homomorphism is in fact obtained by mimicking
the construction of already existing
fundamental classes and refined Gysin homomorphism in étale
cohomology by Gabber-Riou (see
[
28
]
and
[
83
]
).

Lemma 9.4
.

There is a commutative diagram

(9.6)

\textstyle{H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{Z,{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,\Lambda(n+j)).}

Proof.

Since the étale realization functor is monoidal,
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
preserves
cup products. Hence, we only need to show that it also preserves the fundamental
class
\eta^{X}_{Z}
. But this is part of the construction of the refined
Gysin homomorphism in the motivic cohomology. We briefly recall this construction for
the sake of completeness of our arguments.

Let
X^{\prime}
be the blow-up of
X
along
Z
with exceptional divisor
E\cong{\mathbb{P}}_{Z}({\mathcal{F}})
.
Let
\pi\colon X^{\prime}\to X,\ \pi^{\prime}\colon E\to Z
be the projections and

\iota^{\prime}\colon E\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
the
inclusion. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the conormal sheaf of the embedding
Z\hookrightarrow X
.
Then the pull-back map
\pi^{*}\colon H^{2n}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n))\to H^{2n}_{E}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(n))
is an inclusion
[
77
, Cor. 2.6]
(see

[
83
]
for the étale case). Furthermore, there is a class
\omega^{X}_{Z}\in H^{2n-2}(E,\Lambda(n-1))
which is a linear combination of powers of

c_{1}(\iota^{\prime*}{\mathcal{L}}_{E})
and
the Chern classes of
\pi^{\prime*}({\mathcal{F}})
such that the image of
\omega^{X}_{Z}
under
the refined Gysin homomorphism
\iota_{*}\colon H^{2n-2}(E,\Lambda(n-1))\to H^{2n}_{E}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(n))
lies in
H^{2n}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n))
and this image is the
class
\eta^{X}_{Z}
. Hence, all one needs to check is that
\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E}))

and
\epsilon^{*}_{Z}(c_{i}({\mathcal{F}}))
coincide with
c^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E})
and

c^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{i}({\mathcal{F}})
.

Since the ordinary Chern classes of
{\mathcal{F}}
are preserved under
\epsilon^{*}_{Z}

(as is clear from
[
77
, Defn. 1.32]
), we have to check the same for
the ‘Chern class with support’
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E})
.
But this is also well known. Indeed, recall that

(9.7)

H^{2}_{E}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(1))\cong{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(X^{\prime},\Lambda)}(\iota^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{E},\Lambda_{X^{\prime}}(1)[2])\ {\cong}^{\dagger}\ {\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}(X^{\prime},\Lambda)}(\iota^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{E},\Lambda_{X^{\prime}}(1)[2])

\hskip 142.26378pt\cong{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}(X^{\prime},\Lambda)}(\iota^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{E},{\mathbb{G}}_{m,X^{\prime}}\otimes\Lambda(1))\cong H^{1}_{E}(X^{\prime}_{\rm nis},{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{\prime}}}/m),

where
{\cong}^{\dagger}
is
[
15
, Cor. 5.9]
and
the isomorphism
\Lambda_{X^{\prime}}(1)[1]\cong{\mathbb{G}}_{m,X^{\prime}}\otimes\Lambda
is shown in the
proof of
[
17
, Prop. 11.2.11]
.
The last isomorphism in (
9.7
) is a classical fact in

{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy theory of schemes (e.g., see
[
17
, Ex. 10.4.4]
).

Now, it follows from the Cartier exact sequence that
H^{1}_{E}(X^{\prime}_{\rm nis},{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{\prime}}})

is the group of Cartier divisors on
X^{\prime}
which are supported on
E
.
Since
E\subset X^{\prime}
is a Cartier divisor, it has a uniquely defined class
(say,
[E]
) in
H^{1}_{E}(X^{\prime}_{\rm nis},{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{\prime}}}/m)
. Furthermore,

c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E})\in H^{2}_{E}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(1))
corresponds to

[E]
under the composite isomorphism of (
9.7
)
(see
[
77
, Defn. 1.41]
).
As the class
c^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E})\in H^{2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},E}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(1))\cong H^{2}_{E}(X^{\prime}_{\text{\'{e}t}},\Lambda^{\prime}(1))
is defined in the identical way, one gets

\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E}))=c^{\text{\'{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{E})
.
∎

Corollary 9.5
.

Assume in Lemma
9.4
that
Z
is complete. Then there is a
commutative
diagram

(9.8)

\textstyle{H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(n+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(n+j)).}

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
9.4
because
(
9.8
) is the composition of (
9.6
)
with the diagram

(9.9)

\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{Z}(X,\Lambda(n+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Z}(X,\Lambda(n+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(n+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2n+i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(n+j)),}

where the vertical arrows are the forget support maps (see Lemma
8.9
).
But this diagram commutes by the naturality of
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
because the
right vertical arrow is obtained from the left vertical arrow by applying

\epsilon^{*}_{X}
to (
8.23
).
∎

9.3.
Saito-Tate duality in étale cohomology

We shall now assume that
k
is a local field of characteristic exponent
p>0
.
Let
m
be an integer prime to
p
and
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
. Recall from
[
83
]
that
in the étale setting, there is a theory of Gysin morphism for
any projective morphism between regular
k
-schemes (see
[
21
]
for its
extension to motivic cohomology). We shall need this construction in the following
special case in order to state and work with the Saito-Tate duality.

We consider the commutative diagram

(9.10)

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\rm Spec\,}(k).}

Here,
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
is an integral scheme of
dimension
d
, the left vertical arrow is a closed immersion of codimension
n
,
the top horizontal arrow is smooth and projective with
Z
integral, and the right
vertical arrow is induced by
a finite field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
. In particular,
Z
is a projective and regular

k
-scheme. We let
e=d-n
.

In the above setting, there are canonical maps

(9.11)

f_{!}\Lambda_{X}\cong f_{!}f^{*}\Lambda_{k}\cong f_{!}f^{!}\Lambda_{k}(-d)[-2d]\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X/k}}\Lambda_{k}(-d)[-2d],

where the middle isomorphism is a consequence of the well known relation between

f^{!}
and
f^{*}
for smooth morphisms and
\alpha_{X/k}
is the counit of adjunction.
The composite
f_{*}\colon f_{!}\Lambda_{X}\to\Lambda_{k}(-d)[-2d]

is the Gabber-Riou Gysin map.
We similarly have
g^{\prime}_{*}\colon g^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{Z}\to\Lambda_{k^{\prime}}(-e)[-2e]
.

We have maps
\iota_{!}\Lambda_{Z}\xrightarrow{\cong}\iota_{!}\iota^{!}\Lambda_{X}(n)[2n]\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X/Z}}\Lambda_{X}(n)[2n]
, where the first map is Gabber’s purity isomorphism

[
28
]
and
\alpha_{X/Z}
is the counit of adjunction.
The composite map
\iota_{*}\colon g_{*}\Lambda_{Z}\to f_{!}\Lambda_{X}

induces the Gysin homomorphism
\iota_{*}\colon H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(Z,\Lambda(j))\to H^{2n+i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(n+j))

(see Corollary
9.5
).
The finite map
\pi\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
induces the
counit of adjunction
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\Lambda_{k}\to\Lambda_{k}
which
yields the Gysin map
\pi_{*}\colon\pi_{!}\Lambda_{k^{\prime}}\to\Lambda_{k}
.
Let
g_{*}\colon g_{!}\Lambda_{Z}\to\Lambda_{k}(-e)[-2e]
denote the
Gabber-Riou Gysin homomorphism associated to the projective morphism

g:Z\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
between regular
k
-schemes.

Lemma 9.6
.

The diagram

(9.12)

\textstyle{g_{!}\Lambda_{Z}(e+1)[2e+2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{!}\circ\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{!}\circ g^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{g_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{!}g^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{Z}(e+1)[2e+2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{!}\Lambda_{k^{\prime}}(1)[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{f_{!}\Lambda_{X}(d+1)[2d+2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{k}(1)[2]}

is commutative.

Proof.

The proof of the lemma is part of the construction of the Gysin homomorphism

g_{*}
. More precisely,
[
83
, Thm. 2.5.12]

(see also
[
20
, § 3]
) shows that

\pi_{*}\circ g^{\prime}_{*}=g_{*}
and
f_{*}\circ\iota_{*}=g_{*}
.
∎

To translate (
9.12
) in the language of cohomology groups, we note that

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},\pi_{!}g^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{Z}(e+1)[2e+2])&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},\pi_{*}g^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{Z}(e+1)[2e+2])\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda)}(\pi^{*}\Lambda_{k},g^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{Z}(e+1)[2e+2])\\
&\cong&{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k^{\prime}},g^{\prime}_{!}\Lambda_{Z}(e+1)[2e+2])\\
\end{array}

Similarly, we have

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k},\pi_{!}\Lambda_{k^{\prime}}(1)[2])\cong{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda)}(\Lambda_{k^{\prime}},\Lambda_{k^{\prime}}(1)[2])\cong H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda(1)).

In particular, the counit of adjunction
\pi_{*}\Lambda_{k^{\prime}}\to\Lambda_{k}

induces
\pi_{*}\colon H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\to H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(j))
.
Hence, by considering the morphisms in
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)
,
induced by (
9.12
), we get:

Lemma 9.7
.

There is a commutative diagram

(9.13)

\textstyle{H^{2e+i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(Z,\Lambda(e+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(j)).}

Let
{\mathfrak{f}}
denote the residue field of
k
. Then there is a Witt-residue map

{\rm Inv}_{k}\colon{\rm Br}(k)\to H^{1}({\mathfrak{f}}_{\text{\'{e}t}},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
, which is an isomorphism
(see
[
12
, Defn. 1.4.11]
).
In particular, the Kummer sequence shows that the composite map

H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\xrightarrow{\cong}{}_{m}{\rm Br}(k)\xrightarrow{{\rm Inv}_{k}}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda)

is an isomorphism. Composing this with the canonical composite isomorphism

(9.14)

{\operatorname{tr}}_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda)\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(G_{\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda,

we get an isomorphism
{\operatorname{tr}}_{k}\colon H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda
.
This map was used by Tate
[
98
]
as the trace homomorphism for his
duality theorem for étale cohomology over local fields.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
{k^{\prime}}/k
be a finite extension of local fields and let
\pi\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
be the projection map. Then the diagram

(9.15)

\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}_{k^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}_{k}}
\textstyle{\Lambda}

is commutative.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}
denote the residue field of
k^{\prime}
and let
\pi^{\prime}\colon{\rm Spec\,}({\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime})\to{\rm Spec\,}({\mathfrak{f}})
be the projection. By
[
91
, Exc. XII.3.2]
,
there is a commutative diagram

(9.16)

\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Inv}_{k^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime},\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Inv}_{k}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda).}

By this, the lemma is reduced to showing that the diagram

(9.17)

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime},\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}_{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}_{{\mathfrak{f}}}}
\textstyle{\Lambda}

is commutative.

To prove the above, we recall that
{\operatorname{tr}}_{{\mathfrak{f}}}
is the same as the composite
isomorphism
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda)\cong H^{1}(G_{\mathfrak{f}},H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{f}}},\Lambda))\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(G_{\mathfrak{f}},\Lambda)\cong\Lambda
, where the second term is the
continuous Galois cohomology and the first isomorphism is the edge map in the
Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence. The same holds for
{\operatorname{tr}}_{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}}
too.
Since
\overline{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}}=\overline{{\mathfrak{f}}}
, it suffices to show that the diagram

(9.18)

\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(G_{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\prime}},\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Lambda\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(G_{{\mathfrak{f}}},\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Lambda,}

commutes. But this follows from
[
52
, Cor. 3.2.1]
.
∎

We now recall the following duality theorem
due to Tate
[
98
]
and Saito
[
86
]
.
For
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
integral of dimension
d
, let

{\operatorname{tr}}_{X/k}
denote the composite

H^{2d+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\xrightarrow{f_{*}}H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{tr}}_{k}}\Lambda
. We shall call this the trace map for
X
.

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
be an integral scheme of dimension
d
with structure map

f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
. Let
K\in{\mathbf{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)
be a bounded complex with
constructible cohomology sheaves. Then the following hold.

(1)

H^{i}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},K)
are finite for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(2)

The trace map
{\operatorname{tr}}_{X/k}\colon H^{2d+2}_{c}(X_{\text{\'{e}t}},\Lambda(d+1))\to\Lambda

is an isomorphism.

(3)

The pairing

H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,K)\times H^{2d+2-i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{\mathcal{H}{om}}_{X}(K,\Lambda(d+1-i)))\to H^{2d+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{tr}}_{X/k}}\Lambda

is a perfect paring of finite abelian groups.

We shall let
\omega_{X}\colon H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda(j))^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d+2-i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1-j)
denote the Saito-Tate duality homomorphism.

Suppose now that
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
is as in (
9.10
).
We then get a diagram

(9.19)

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,\Lambda)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota^{*})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Z}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2e+1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,\Lambda(e+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1)).}

Here,
(\iota^{*})^{\vee}
is the dual of the pull-back

\iota^{*}\colon H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,\Lambda)
, the vertical arrows
are the unique maps induced by the Saito-Tate duality for
Z
and
X

(see Theorem
9.9
) and
\iota_{*}
is the Gysin homomorphism.

Lemma 9.10
.

The diagram (
9.19
) is commutative.

Proof.

Since the Gysin map
\iota_{*}
is linear with respect to the action of the
étale cohomology ring of
X
on the étale cohomology of
Z
via
\iota^{*}
,
it follows that the diagram

(9.20)

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,\Lambda)\times H^{2e+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(Z,\Lambda(e+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2e+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(Z,\Lambda(e+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)\times H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1)).}

is commutative.

We now note that the map
{\rm Hom}(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda),H^{2d+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1)))\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)^{\vee}
, obtained by composing with
{\operatorname{tr}}_{X/k}
, is an isomorphism.
The same is also true for
Z
. Hence, (
9.20
) reduces the
proof of the lemma to showing that the diagram

(9.21)

\textstyle{H^{2e+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(Z,\Lambda(e+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k^{\prime},\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}_{k^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}_{k}}
\textstyle{\Lambda}

is commutative. But this follows by Lemmas
9.7
and

9.8
.
∎

9.4.
Comparison of reciprocity and étale realization maps

We shall now prove the main result of this section.
Let us recall that
k
is a local field of characteristic exponent
p>0
and

\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
, where
m
is an integer prime to
p
.
We need the following base case.

Lemma 9.11
.

The diagram

(9.22)

\textstyle{{K^{M}_{1}(k)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{k}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{k}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(k,\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{k}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))}

is commutative.

Proof.

Recall that
\rho_{k}
is defined via the pairing

{K^{M}_{1}(k)}/m\times H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{{\rm NR}_{k}\times{\rm id}}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\times H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)\to H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(1))\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{tr}}_{k}}\Lambda,

where
{\rm NR}_{k}\colon{K^{M}_{n}(k)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda(n))

is the Norm-residue isomorphism.
In particular,
{\rm NR}_{k}=\omega_{k}\circ\rho_{k}
.
We thus have to show that
\epsilon^{*}_{k}\circ\Psi_{k}={\rm NR}_{k}
.
But this is well known and elementary using the description of

\Psi_{k}
given in §
8.5
.
∎

The following is the main result of §
9
.

Proposition 9.12
.

Let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
be of pure dimension
d
and let
m\in k^{\times}
be an integer.
Then the diagram

(9.23)

\textstyle{{C(X)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X}}
\textstyle{{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))}

is commutative and the vertical arrows are isomorphisms.

Proof.

We can assume that
X
is integral. Using the natural isomorphism

(9.24)

{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda),\Lambda)=H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)^{\vee},

we can replace
{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m
by
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)^{\vee}
.
The right vertical arrow is now the Saito-Tate duality isomorphism.
The left vertical arrow is an isomorphism by Proposition
8.15
.
It remains to show that (
9.23
) is commutative.

Since the map

{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\to{C(X)}/m
is surjective by
Lemma
5.8
, it suffices to show that (
9.23
)
commutes after we compose it with the canonical map

{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\to{C(X)}/m
for every
x\in X_{(0)}
.
We therefore fix an arbitrary closed point
x\in X
and let

\iota\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion.

We now look at the diagram

(9.25)

\textstyle{{K^{M}_{1}(k(x))}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k(x),\Lambda)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota^{*})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{x}}
\textstyle{{C(X)}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{x}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k(x),\Lambda(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,\Lambda(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(d+1)).}

We need to show that
\omega_{X}\circ\rho_{X}\circ\iota_{*}=\epsilon^{*}_{X}\circ\Psi_{X}\circ\iota_{*}
. For this, it suffices to show that all faces of the above cube,
except possibly the front face, commute.

The top face commutes by the construction of the reciprocity map
\rho_{X}
.
Note here that the under the isomorphism (
9.24
), the map

(\iota^{*})^{\vee}
is identified with the push-forward map

\iota_{*}\colon{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}(k(x)))}/m\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m
.
The bottom face commutes by Corollary
9.5
.
The left face commutes by Proposition
8.15
and the right face commutes by
Lemma
9.10
. Finally, the back face commutes by Lemma
9.11
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

10.
Finiteness of motivic and étale cohomology

Our goal in this section is to prove some finiteness results for motivic and
étale cohomology over local fields.
Let
k
be a local field with the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
and residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
Let
p={\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})
be the residue characteristic of
k
.
For any Noetherian
k
-scheme
X
and integers
i\geq 0
and
j
, we let

(10.1)

H^{i}(X,j)={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}H^{i}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}(j))={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}\ {\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}}\ H^{i}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}}(j));

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,j)={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}(j))={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}\ {\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}}\ H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}}(j)),

where the sums are over all primes different from
p
. We define
H^{i}_{c}(X,j)
and

H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,j)
in similar fashion.

10.1.
A property of the étale realization map

In this subsection, we allow
k
to be any field.
We fix an integer
m
such that
m\in k^{\times}
and set
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
.
Recall that
cd(k)
denotes the étale cohomological dimension of
k
for all
sheaves which are torsion of finite exponents prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.

Recall from (
9.4
) that
for any
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, there is a natural étale realization homomorphism

\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))\to H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda(j))
. The naturality
of this map implies that we have a commutative diagram

(10.2)

\textstyle{H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(j)),}

where
\pi\colon X^{\prime}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})\to X
is the projection map
for any field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
be of pure dimension
d
. Then
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
is an isomorphism
when
j\geq{\min}\{i,d+cd(k)\}
. This map is injective when
i=j+1
.

Proof.

We can assume that
\Lambda={{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}}
, where
\ell\neq{\rm char}(k)
is any prime
and
n\geq 1
is any integer.
By Proposition
8.2
, the motivic cohomology is invariant under perfection.
The similar result for étale cohomology is classical
(see
[
4
, Exp. VIII, Thm. 1.1]
).
In particular,
cd(k)=cd(k^{\heartsuit})
.
Using (
10.2
), we can therefore assume that
k
is perfect.

Now, the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture (which is a consequence of the Bloch-Kato
conjecture by
[
30
]
, and the latter is now a theorem of Voevodsky

[
107
]
) says that the étale realization map

(10.3)

\epsilon^{*}\colon\Lambda(j)\to\tau_{\leq j}{\bf R}\epsilon_{*}(\Lambda(j)_{\text{\'{e}t}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\tau_{\leq j}{\bf R}\epsilon_{*}(\Lambda(j))

is an isomorphism for
j\geq 0
in
{\mathbf{DM}}(k,\Lambda)
, where
\epsilon\colon{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k,{\text{\'{e}t}}}\to{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k,{\rm nis}}
is the canonical morphism
from the étale to the Nisnevich site of essentially of finite type smooth schemes
over
k
.
The assertion of the lemma is an immediate consequence of this isomorphism
once we observe that
{\bf R}\epsilon_{*}(\Lambda(j))\to\tau_{\leq j}{\bf R}\epsilon_{*}(\Lambda(j))

is an isomorphism when
j\geq d+cd(k)
because the étale cohomological
dimension of a
d
-dimensional finite type affine
k
-scheme is bounded by

d+cd(k)
.
∎

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be of dimension
d
. Then
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
is an
isomorphism
when
j\geq{\min}\{i,d+cd(k)\}
. This map is injective when
i=j+1
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
10.1
, we can assume that
k
is perfect.
If
d=0
, then
X
must be smooth over
k
and we are done by
Lemma
10.1
.
We shall now prove the general case by induction on
d
. Since the motivic
and étale cohomology are both nil-invariant, we can assume that
X
is
reduced.
Since
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
clearly satisfies the Mayer-Vietoris property for
closed covers, and so does the étale cohomology
(e.g., see
[
74
, Exc. VI.3.4]
), we can apply this property to
immediately reduce the proof to the case when
X
is integral.

If
{\rm char}(k)=0
, then we can find a resolution of singularities

\pi\colon W\to X
. If we let
Z=X_{\rm sing}
and
E=\pi^{-1}(Z)
, then

\{W\amalg Z\rightarrow X\}
is a cdh cover of
X
such that

\text{\rm dim}(Z)<\text{\rm dim}(X)
and
\text{\rm dim}(E)<\text{\rm dim}(X)
.
A combination of proper base change theorem and
localization theorem in étale cohomology
(see
[
74
, Rmk. III.3.13, Cor. VI.2.3]
) implies that

{\mathbf{D}}^{c}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)
satisfies
cdh-descent, where
{\mathbf{D}}^{c}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)
is the full subcategory of

{\mathbf{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\Lambda)
consisting of complexes with constructible cohomology sheaves.
The category
{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,\Lambda)
clearly satisfies cdh-descent.
We therefore get an exact sequence

(10.4)

\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}H^{i-1}(W,\Lambda(j))\\
\hskip 34.14322pt\oplus\\
H^{i-1}(Z,\Lambda(j))\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}(E,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}(X,\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}H^{i}(W,\Lambda(j))\\
\hskip 34.14322pt\oplus\\
H^{i}(Z,\Lambda(j))\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}(E,\Lambda(j)),}

which maps to the similar exact sequence of étale cohomology.
Comparing the two exact sequences and using
induction on
d
, it suffices to prove that
\epsilon^{*}_{W}
is an isomorphism
for
i\leq j
and injective for
i=j+1
.
But this follows from Lemma
10.1
.

We now assume that
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
.
Using Temkin’s strengthening of the alteration theorems of de Jong and Gabber
(see
[
100
, Thm. 1.2.5]
) and the flatification theorem of Raynaud-Gruson

[
35
, Thm. 5.2.2]
, it is shown in the proof of

[
8
, Prop. 6.2]
that there exists a commutative diagram

(10.5)

\textstyle{W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X,}

where
f
is the blow-up along a nowhere dense closed subscheme
Z\hookrightarrow X
and
f^{\prime}
is
the strict transform of
W
along
f
. The map
\pi
is a
p
-alteration of
X

such that
W
is integral and smooth over
k
and
\pi^{\prime}
is a finite and flat
morphism between integral schemes of degree
p^{r}
for some
r\geq 0
.

Since
\{X^{\prime}\amalg Z\rightarrow X\}
is a cdh cover of
X
, we can apply the above
characteristic zero
argument to reduce the proof to showing that the lemma holds for

{X^{\prime}}
.
By applying this argument to
f^{\prime}
and using induction,
we see that the lemma holds for
W^{\prime}
because it
holds for
W
by Lemma
10.1
.

The assertion for
\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}
now follows from
[
15
, Prop. 6.3]

and
[
16
, Prop. 6.1.8(4)]
which together say that there is a commutative
diagram

(10.6)

\textstyle{H^{i}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{i}(W^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{i}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(W^{\prime},\Lambda(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime},\Lambda(j))}

such that the composition of the horizontal arrows on each of the two rows is
multiplication by
p^{r}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 10.3
.

Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be of dimension
d
. Then the étale realization
map
\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon H^{i}_{c}(X,\Lambda(j))\to H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,\Lambda(j))
is an
isomorphism when
j\geq{\min}\{i,d+cd(k)\}
. This map is injective when

i=j+1
.

Proof.

Choose a compactification
u\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
and let
Z\subset X
be the
complementary
closed subset with reduced subscheme structure. An application of
Lemma
8.4
and
its analogue in étale cohomology allow us to assume that
X
is
complete. The latter case follows from Lemma
10.2
.
∎

10.2.
Finiteness of étale cohomology over local fields

The key ingredient to prove our finiteness theorem for étale cohomology
over local fields (see Theorem
10.10
) is the following analogous result
over finite fields due to Colliot-Thélène-Sansuc-Soule

[
18
, Thm. 2]
and Kahn
[
49
, Thm. 1, 2]
3
3

3

The reader may check
that in
[
49
, Thm. 1, 2]
, the word ‘variety’ means any finite type
separated
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-scheme.
.

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
be a smooth scheme of dimension
d
, where
q=p^{r}
for some

r\geq 1
and let
n
be any integer.
Then
H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,n)
is finite if
n\notin[0,d]
or
i\notin[n,2n+1]
.
If
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
is any scheme of dimension
d
, then
H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,n)
is finite
if
n\notin[0,d]
or
i\notin[n,n+d+1]
.

We shall now prove the finiteness of étale cohomology over local fields
in a few steps,
beginning with the following base case. We fix a local field
k
having the
ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
, maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
and residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
We let
p={\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})
be the
residue characteristic of
k
. We let
S={\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})

with generic point
\eta
and closed point
s
. Recall that

cd(k)=2
and
cd({\mathfrak{f}})=1
. We fix an integer
i\geq 0
.
For any
S
-scheme
{\mathcal{X}}
, we let

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathcal{X}},j)={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}H^{i}({\mathcal{X}}_{\text{\'{e}t}},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}(j))={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}\ {\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}}\ H^{i}({\mathcal{X}}_{\text{\'{e}t}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}}(j)),

where the sum is over all primes different from
p
. We shall use a similar notation
for cohomology with support.

We begin with the following extension of Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Given a commutative diagram of Noetherian schemes

(10.7)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{\rm Spec\,}({\mathfrak{f}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{S}

with
\pi
a dominant morphism,
the canonical map
\iota^{\prime*}\mu_{m,{\mathcal{X}}}\to\mu_{m,X}
is an isomorphism on

X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
if
m\in{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
9.2
applies verbatim to show that

\pi^{*}\mu_{m,S}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mu_{m,{\mathcal{X}}}
. Since

\pi^{\prime*}\mu_{m,{\mathfrak{f}}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mu_{m,X}
by Lemma
9.2
, it
suffices to show that
\iota^{*}\mu_{m,S}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mu_{m,{\mathfrak{f}}}
. But this
is an elementary exercise using our hypothesis, which implies that

m\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k}
and hence
(1+{\mathfrak{m}})
is
m
-divisible,
see the last part of the proof of Lemma
5.9
.
∎

Lemma 10.6
.

The group
H_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}^{i}(k,n)
is finite when
n\neq 0,1
.

Proof.

The Gersten sequence, localization, absolute purity and rigidity theorems in
étale cohomology together with Lemma
10.5

yield a short exact sequence

(10.8)

0\to H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},n)\to H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,n)\to H^{i-1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},n-1)\to 0.

The lemma follows by combining this exact sequence with Theorem
10.4
.
∎

Recall that a reduced Noetherian scheme
X
of pure dimension
d
with
irreducible components
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\}
is called a normal crossing scheme
if for every nonempty subset
J\subset[1,r]
, the scheme theoretic
intersection
X_{J}:={\underset{i\in J}{\bigcap}}X_{i}
is a regular scheme
which is either empty or of pure dimension
d+1-|J|
. It is clear from this
definition that if
X
is a normal crossing scheme, then

X_{i}\bigcap\ (\cup_{j\neq i}X_{j})
is also a normal crossing scheme (of smaller
dimension than that of
X
).

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to S
be a projective and flat morphism of relative dimension

d
from a regular integral scheme. Let
Y\subset{\mathcal{X}}
be a normal crossing
closed subscheme of pure codimension
c
such that
Y\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
. Then

H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Y}({\mathcal{X}},n)
is finite if
n\notin[c,d+1]
.

Proof.

We shall prove the lemma by a double induction on the dimension and the number of
irreducible components of
Y
. We can assume that
Y
is reduced.
If
Y
is regular (e.g., if
\text{\rm dim}(Y)=0
), then Gabber’s
purity theorem implies that
H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Y}({\mathcal{X}},n)\cong H^{i-2c}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(Y,n-c)
and the
latter group is finite in the given range by Theorem
10.4
because

n-c\notin[0,\text{\rm dim}(Y)]
.

In the general case, we write

Y=Y^{\prime}\bigcup Z
, where
Y^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
Y
and
Z
is the
union of its remaining irreducible components. We have
the Mayer-Vietoris exact sequence

H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Y^{\prime}}({\mathcal{X}},n)\oplus H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Z}({\mathcal{X}},n)\to H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Y}({\mathcal{X}},n)\to H^{i+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Y^{\prime}\cap Z}({\mathcal{X}},n).

The term on the left is finite by induction on the number of irreducible components
of
Y
and the term on the right is finite by induction on
\text{\rm dim}(Y)
because

Y^{\prime}\cap Z
is a normal crossing scheme of strictly smaller dimension as we
observe before. It follows that the middle term is finite.
∎

Recall that an integral and smooth projective
k
-scheme
X
is said to admit a
semistable reduction if there exists a regular integral scheme
{\mathcal{X}}
together with
a faithfully flat projective morphism
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to S
such that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\eta}=X
and

({\mathcal{X}}_{s})_{\rm red}
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
{\mathcal{X}}
. It is a consequence of

[
22
]
and
[
13
, Thm. 0.1, Cor. 0.4]

(see also
[
14
]
) that
X
admits a semistable reduction if
its dimension is at most one.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
be an integral projective scheme of dimension
d
which admits a
semistable reduction. Then
H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,n)
is finite if
n\notin[0,d+1]
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to S
be a semistable reduction of
X
with the reduced closed fiber

Y
. The localization and rigidity theorems yield an exact sequence

(10.9)

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Y,n)\to H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,n)\to H^{i+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},Y}({\mathcal{X}},n).

In the given range, the left term is finite by Theorem
10.4
and the
right term is finite by Lemma
10.7
. Hence, the middle term is finite.
∎

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be a projective scheme of dimension
d
. Then

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,n)
is finite if
n\notin[0,d+1]
.

Proof.

We shall prove the lemma by induction on
d
. If
d=0
, then
we are done by Lemma
10.6
. We shall now assume that
d>0
.

By
[
100
, Thm. 4.3.1]
, we can find a finite field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k

and a smooth projective integral
k^{\prime}
-scheme
W
together with a projective
morphism
\pi\colon W\to X
such that the following hold.

(1)

k^{\prime}=k
and
\pi
is birational if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.

(2)

The degrees
[k^{\prime}:k]
is some power of
p

if
{\rm char}(k)={\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})=p
.

(3)

The morphism

\pi
is a
p
-alteration (an alteration à la de Jong whose degree is a

p
-power) if
{\rm char}(k)=p
.

(4)

W\to{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
admits a semistable reduction.

It follows from Lemma
10.8
that
H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(W,n)
is finite.
If
{\rm char}(k)=0
, then a Mayer-Vietoris argument (see (
10.4
) in the
proof of Lemma
10.2
) and induction on
d
reduce the proof to
the finiteness of
H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(W,n)
.
If
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
, we can again repeat
the second part of the proof of Lemma
10.2

(see (
10.5
) and (
10.6
)) to
conclude that
H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,n)
is finite.
∎

We can now prove the main finiteness result for motivic and étale cohomology over
local fields.

Theorem 10.10
.

Let
k
be a local field and
X
a
k
-scheme of dimension
d\geq 0
. Let
n
be any
integer. Then
H^{i}_{c}(X,n)
and
H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,n)
are finite if
n\geq d+2
.

Proof.

We shall prove the theorem by induction on
d
.
If
d=0
, then we are through by Lemmas
10.2
and

10.6
.
In general, we can find a nowhere dense closed subscheme
Z\subset X

whose complement is affine. By using induction and Lemma
8.4
, we can thus
assume that
X
is quasi-projective. In the latter case, we can use a similar
argument to assume that
X
is projective. In this case, the theorem follows by
Lemmas
10.2
and
10.9
because
cd(k)=2
.
∎

Corollary 10.11
.

Let the notations be as in Theorem
10.10
and let
n\geq d+2
be an integer.
Then there exists an integer

M(n)\gg 0
such that
|H^{i}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))|\leq M(n)
and

|H^{i}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))|\leq M(n)

for all integers
m
not divisible by
p
.

Proof.

Use Theorem
10.10
and the exact triangle

{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n)\to({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{\prime}(n)\xrightarrow{m}({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{\prime}(n),

where
({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{\prime}={\underset{\ell\neq p}{\bigoplus}}{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}
.
∎

10.3.
Finiteness in characteristic zero

Let us now assume that
k
is a local field of characteristic zero. In this case,
Corollary
10.11
is not quite sufficient for us because it excludes
the
p
-primary part of the motivic and étale cohomology. We shall prove the
following weaker version of Corollary
10.11
which will be enough for our
purpose.

Proposition 10.12
.

Let
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
be of dimension
d\geq 0
. Then there exists an integer

M\gg 0
such that
|H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+2))|\leq M
and

|H^{2d+2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+2))|\leq M

for all integers
m\neq 0
.

Proof.

We can assume that
X
is a reduced scheme. By Corollary
10.3
,
we need to prove the proposition only for the étale cohomology.
Using the argument of Theorem
10.10
and the fact that

cd_{k}(Z)\leq 2\text{\rm dim}(Z)+2
for any
Z\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}

(see
[
92
, Chap. II, Prop. 12]
and
[
5
, Exp. X, Cor. 4.3]
),
we reduce to the case when
X
is projective over
k
.

Let us first assume that
d=0
. In this case, it suffices to prove the proposition
for
{\rm Spec\,}(k)
.
The norm-residue isomorphism
{\rm NR}_{k}\colon{K^{M}_{2}(k)}/{m}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))
now reduces the problem to showing the
uniform boundedness of
{K^{M}_{2}(k)}/{m}
.
To this end, note that we can write
K^{M}_{2}(k)=F\oplus D
, where
F
is a finite
cyclic group and
D
is a divisible group by
[
99
]
(see
[
73
]

for a stronger statement).
Hence, we have a surjection
F\twoheadrightarrow H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))
for all integers

m>0
. Therefore, we get
|H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))|\leq|F|
for all
m>0
.

We now assume that
X
is a smooth projective scheme over
k
and prove the
proposition by induction on
d=\text{\rm dim}(X)
. We can assume
X
to be integral.
If
d=1
, we choose a dense affine open

U\subset X
and let
S
be the complementary closed subset with the reduced
subscheme structure. We then get an exact sequence

(10.10)

H^{4}_{{}_{\text{\'{e}t}},S}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))\to H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))\to H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3)).

Since
U
is an affine curve over
k
, its étale cohomological
dimension is at most three (e.g., see
[
92
, Chap. II, Prop. 12]
and

[
93
, Tag 0F0P]
). In particular, the last term in the above
exact sequence is zero. On the other hand,
we have
H^{4}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},S}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))\cong H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(S,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))

by excision and purity for étale cohomology. Since
S
is the spectrum of a
finite product of local fields, we are done by the
d=0
case solved above.

If
d\geq 2
, we can use the Altman-Kleiman Bertini theorem
[
3
, Thm. 1]

to find a smooth hypersurface section (in a chosen embedding of
X
in some
projective space over
k
)
Y\subset X
. Note that
Y
is necessarily integral
by Grothendieck’s Lefschetz hyperplane theorem for
\pi_{0}(-)
. Furthermore,

U:=X\setminus Y
is affine. We thus have an exact sequence

(10.11)

H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d+2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+2))\to H^{2d+2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+2))

by the localization and purity theorems. The last term is zero because

2d+2>d+2=cd_{k}(U)
and the first term is uniformly bounded by induction on
d
.
This proves the proposition when
X
is smooth and projective over
k
.

If
X
is projective over
k
but not necessarily smooth, we can choose
a resolution of singularities
\pi\colon W\to X
. Then the
resulting exact sequence (
10.4
) reduces the problem
to showing the boundedness for
W
because
cd_{k}(E)<2d+1
and
cd_{k}(Z)<2d+2
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 10.13
.

We do not expect Proposition
10.12
to be valid
under the weaker conditions of Corollary
10.11
.
For instance,
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}(2))
is not a finite group if
k
is a

p
-adic field (e.g., see
[
109
, Cor. 7.4.1]
).
This is perhaps the main source of problem in
generalizing Proposition
10.12
.

11.
Some properties of
{\mathbb{L}}
-completions

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.1
and
part (1) of Theorem
1.4
. We shall also prove some properties of
the completions of the class groups and fundamental groups with respect to some
sets of primes. These results will be key steps in the proofs of the
remaining main results.
We begin by recalling some known elementary results which will be used frequently
in the rest of the paper.

11.1.
Some results on completions of abelian groups

Let
{\mathbb{M}}
be a set of prime numbers
and
I_{{\mathbb{M}}}
the set of all integers whose prime divisors belong to
{\mathbb{M}}
.
Recall from §
3.2
that for an abelian group
A
,
we write
A_{{\mathbb{M}}}={\varprojlim}_{m\in I_{\mathbb{M}}}A/{m}
.
We shall call this the
{\mathbb{M}}
-completion of

A
. We shall say that
A
is
{\mathbb{M}}
