-divisible if it is divisible by every prime
in
{\mathbb{M}}
(equivalently, divisible by every integer in
I_{\mathbb{M}}
). We shall say that

A
is
{\mathbb{M}}
-torsion if every element of
A
is annihilated by some element of
I_{\mathbb{M}}
.
We let
A_{{\mathbb{M}}-{\rm tor}}
denote the
{\mathbb{M}}
-torsion subgroup of
A
.
The following two results are
[
47
, Lem. 7.7, 7.8]
.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
A
be an abelian group,
\{B_{m}\}_{m\in I_{\mathbb{M}}}
a pro-abelian group and

\phi\colon\{A/m\}\to\{B_{m}\}
a morphism of pro-abelian groups. Let

\widehat{\phi}\colon{A}_{\mathbb{M}}\to\widehat{B}
be the induced homomorphism between the limits.
Assume that there exists
N\in I_{\mathbb{M}}
such that
N\widehat{B}_{\rm tor}=N{\rm Ker}(\widehat{\phi})=0
. Then
\bigcap_{m\in I_{\mathbb{M}}}mA
is the maximal
{\mathbb{M}}
-divisible
subgroup of
A
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
0\to D\to A\xrightarrow{\pi}T\to 0
be a short exact sequence of abelian
groups such that
D
is
{\mathbb{M}}
-divisible and
T
is torsion. Let
T=T^{\prime}\oplus T^{\prime\prime}
, where
T^{\prime\prime}
is
{\mathbb{M}}
-torsion and
T^{\prime}
has no
{\mathbb{M}}
-torsion element. Let
D^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(T^{\prime})
. Then
D^{\prime}
is
{\mathbb{M}}
-divisible and
A=D^{\prime}\oplus T^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
\{G_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be an inverse system of finite abelian groups.
Assume that there exists an integer
M>0
such that
|G_{i}|\leq M

for all
i\geq 1
. Let
G={\underset{i}{\varprojlim}}\ G_{i}
be the inverse
limit. Then the projection map
G\to G_{i}
is injective for all
i\gg 1
.

Proof.

One can easily check that there is an inverse system
\{G^{\prime}_{i}\}
with surjective
transition maps such that

G^{\prime}_{i}\subset G_{i}
for every
i\geq 1
and
G={\underset{i}{\varprojlim}}\ G^{\prime}_{i}
.
The lemma easily follows from this observation.
∎

The following consequence of Lemma
11.1
may be known to experts.

Corollary 11.4
.

Let
A
be an abelian group and
{\mathbb{M}}
a set of prime numbers. Suppose that
there exists an integer
M\gg 0
such that
|{A}/{m}|\leq M
for all

m\in I_{\mathbb{M}}
. Then
A\cong F\bigoplus D
, where
F
is finite group of
order lying in
I_{\mathbb{M}}
and
D
is
{\mathbb{M}}
-divisible.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
11.1
with
B_{m}=A/m
and
\phi\colon\{A/m\}\to\{B_{m}\}
,
the identity map. Lemma
11.3
says that the conditions of
Lemma
11.1
are satisfied. It follows that
A_{\mathbb{M}}
is finite group of order lying in
I_{{\mathbb{M}}}
and the
kernel of the completion map
A\to A_{\mathbb{M}}
is
{\mathbb{M}}
-divisible. We now done by Lemma
11.2
.
∎

We shall also need the following result.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a finite étale morphism between
Noetherian schemes such that
X
is integral.
Then there exists a continuous homomorphism
f^{*}\colon\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{\prime})

such that
f_{*}\circ f^{*}
is multiplication by
\deg(f)
on
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
.
Furthermore, given a morphism of integral Noetherian schemes
\pi\colon Y\to X

and
X^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\pi^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}=Y\times_{X}X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g}Y
the canonical
projections, the diagram

(11.1)

\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{\prime})}

is commutative.

Proof.

We first assume that
X^{\prime}
is integral.
Let
{\mathcal{U}}(X,x)
denote the Galois category of finite étale covers of
X
(with
respect to a chosen base point
x
). We let
{\mathcal{U}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})
be defined similarly.
Then the operation of composition with
f
defines a canonical inclusion functor

f_{*}\colon{\mathcal{U}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{U}}(X,f(x^{\prime}))
. This in turn gives rise to a
homomorphism
f^{*}\colon\pi_{1}(X,f(x^{\prime}))\to\pi_{1}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})
. The desired
homomorphism is obtained by passing to the abelianization.
For a non-connected finite étale
cover
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
, we define the desired pull-back homomorphism by considering the irreducible components of
X^{\prime}
.
The commutativity of (
11.1
) follows because one easily checks that
the corresponding diagram of the Galois categories of finite
étale covers commutes.

To show the remaining part of the lemma, we let
K
(resp.
K^{\prime}
) denote the
function field of
X
(resp. the ring of total quotients of
X^{\prime}
).
We then obtain a diagram

(11.2)

\textstyle{G_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{G_{K^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{G_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X),}

where the vertical arrows are induced by the inclusions of generic points of

X
and
X^{\prime}
. Note that
X^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of integral normal schemes and

K^{\prime}
is the product of the function fields of these integral schemes.
The right square clearly commutes and the left square commutes by
(
11.1
) because
X^{\prime}\times_{X}{\rm Spec\,}(K)\cong{\rm Spec\,}(K^{\prime})
.

Since the vertical arrows in (
11.2
)
are surjective because
X
(and hence
X^{\prime}
) is normal,
it is enough to show that the composition of the top horizontal arrows is
multiplication by
\deg(f)
. Since
\deg(f)
is the sum of degrees of the
irreducible components of
X^{\prime}
over
X
, it suffices to show that if

X^{\prime}_{i}
is an irreducible component of
X^{\prime}
with function field
K^{\prime}_{i}
, then
the composite map
G_{K}\xrightarrow{f^{*}}G_{K^{\prime}_{i}}\xrightarrow{f_{*}}G_{K}
is
multiplication by
\deg({X^{\prime}_{i}}/X)
. We can therefore assume that
X^{\prime}
is integral.

Now, it is classical that under the Pontryagin duality between Galois groups and
their characters, the maps
G_{K}\xrightarrow{f^{*}}G_{K^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{f_{*}}G_{K}

are dual to the maps between Galois cohomology

(11.3)

H^{1}(G_{K},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\xrightarrow{{\rm Res}}H^{1}(G_{K^{\prime}},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\xrightarrow{{\rm Cor}}H^{1}(G_{K},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}),

where
{\rm Res}
and
{\rm Cor}
are, respectively, the
restriction and corestriction homomorphisms between the Galois cohomology groups.
Hence, it suffices to show that
{\rm Cor}\circ{\rm Res}
is multiplication by

\deg(f)
.
To show this, note that we can replace the abelian Galois groups in
(
11.3
) by the corresponding absolute (nonabelian) Galois groups
without changing the Galois cohomology.
The claim then follows by
[
33
, Prop. 4.2.10]
.
∎

11.2.
The finiteness theorem for
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}

For the rest of §
11
, we fix a local field
k
, a smooth and connected
projective
k
-scheme
\overline{X}
of dimension

d
and a nonempty open immersion
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
. We let

\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion of the complementary closed subset
endowed with the reduced subscheme structure.
We let
{\mathfrak{f}}
be the residue field of
k
.
Recall from §
3.2
that
{\mathbb{P}}
is the set of all primes different from

{\rm char}(k)
. We let
{\mathbb{L}}
denote the set of all primes
different from
{\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})
. In particular,
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{L}}
if
{\rm char}(k)>0
and

{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{L}}\cup\{{\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})\}
if
{\rm char}(k)=0
.

We shall now prove Theorem
1.1
and part of Theorem
1.4

and give some applications.
The precise result is the following.

Theorem 11.6
.

The group
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)
satisfies the following properties.

(1)

In the short exact sequence

0\to J^{\rm t}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\to 0,

the left group
J^{\rm t}(X)
is finite of order prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.

(2)

If
X
is geometrically connected, then

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}\cong F\oplus\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{r},

where
F
is a finite group and
r
is the
{\mathfrak{f}}
-rank of the special fiber of
the Néron model of
{\rm Alb}(\overline{X})
.

Proof.

We shall follow the notations of §
3.2
while proving (1).
By Corollary
3.11
, we have a short exact sequence

(11.4)

0\to J^{\rm t}(X)\to{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\to 0.

A combination of
Proposition
9.12
, (the étale analogue of) Lemma
8.4
,
Corollary
10.11
and Proposition
10.12
implies that we
have a compatible system of short exact sequences

(11.5)

0\to A_{m}\to{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/m\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}/m\to 0

as
m
varies through integers invertible in
k^{\times}
such that

|A_{m}|\leq M
, where
M
is an integer not depending on
m
.
Taking the inverse limit and using its left exactness, we get an exact
sequence

(11.6)

0\to{\varprojlim}_{m}A_{m}\to{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\to 0.

The uniform boundedness property of
\{|A_{m}|\}
implies that

{\varprojlim}_{m}A_{m}
is finite (e.g., see Lemma
11.3
).
Comparing this sequence with
(
11.4
), we conclude that
J^{\rm t}(X)
is a finite group of exponent
prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.

To prove part (2), note that our hypothesis implies that
\overline{X}
is geometrically
connected. We also note that

(11.7)

0\to J^{\rm t}(X)\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{0}\to 0

is exact. We now apply part (1) of the proposition and

[
112
, Thm. 1.1]
to conclude the desired assertion about

\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}
, and hence conclude the proof of the theorem.
∎

The following corollary proves part of Theorem
1.4
whose complete proof will
follow from Theorem
12.8
.

Corollary 11.7
.

Assume that
X
is geometrically connected. Then the image of the degree zero
reciprocity map

\rho^{t}_{X,0}\colon C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}
is finite.
Furthermore,
{\rm Coker}_{\rm top}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
is the direct sum of
\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{r}
and a
finite group.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
11.6
and
[
27
, Cor. 2.3(1,4)]
.
Note that the proof of the cited result actually shows that

{\rm Coker}_{\rm top}(\rho_{\overline{X}})
is the direct sum of
\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{r}
and a finite group.
∎

The following application of Theorem
11.6
is a significantly improved version of
Proposition
3.5
.

Theorem 11.8
.

There is an exact sequence of topological
abelian groups

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{G^{(0)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}}{G^{(1)}_{\Delta(C_{n})}}\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\overline{X}}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\to 0.

Proof.

Combine Proposition
3.5
and part (1) of Theorem
11.6
.
∎

11.3.
Torsion in the image of degree zero tame reciprocity

We continue with setup of §
11.2
.
A consequence of Proposition
7.3
and Corollary
7.4
is that there is a commutative diagram
of continuous homomorphisms of topological groups

(11.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(X)_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X,0}}
\textstyle{C(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{k^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{G_{k}.}

We shall refer to this diagram and the analogous commutative diagrams
corresponding to the reciprocity homomorphisms for
C^{\rm t}(X)

(see (
7.8
)) and
C(\overline{X})
very
often in the rest of the paper.

Suppose that
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a finite extension and let
X^{\prime}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
.
Let
\pi\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
and
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be the projections.
Then we get a diagram

(11.9)

\textstyle{C(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{N_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{C(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{N_{X}}
\textstyle{k^{\prime\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{{k^{\prime}}/k}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{k^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{k}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{G_{k^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{G_{k}.}

Lemma 11.9
.

The above diagram is commutative.

Proof.

The front, back, left and right faces commute by Corollary
7.5
and (
11.8
).
The bottom face clearly commutes. To show the commutativity of the top face,
we can assume that
X
is the spectrum of a field, in which case, it is a standard
property of norms.
∎

Lemma 11.10
.

The image of
\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0}
is a torsion group of finite exponent.
In particular,
({\rm Image}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X}))_{\rm tor}
has finite exponent.

Proof.

The second assertion follows from the first since
(G_{k})_{\rm tor}
is finite.
We shall therefore prove the first assertion.
By Theorem
11.6
, Corollary
7.5
and the surjection

C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}\twoheadrightarrow C(\overline{X})_{0}
, it suffices to show that
the image of
\rho_{\overline{X},0}
is a torsion group of finite exponent.
If
\overline{X}
is geometrically connected, then the image of
\rho_{\overline{X},0}

is in fact a finite group by
[
27
, Cor. 2.3(1)]
.
If
\overline{X}
is not geometrically connected, we can find a finite Galois
extension
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
such that
\overline{X}^{\prime}:=\overline{X}_{k^{\prime}}
is the disjoint union of
its irreducible components
\overline{X}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\overline{X}^{\prime}_{r}
each of which is
geometrically connected. In particular, the lemma holds for
\overline{X}^{\prime}
.

We let
f\colon\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
be the projection. By Lemma
11.9
,
we have a commutative diagram

(11.10)

\textstyle{C(\overline{X}^{\prime})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\overline{X}^{\prime},0}}
\textstyle{C(\overline{X})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\overline{X},0}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X}^{\prime})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{0}.}

It follows from Corollary
4.19
that the cokernel of the top horizontal
arrow is of exponent
[k^{\prime}:k]
. Since the image of the left vertical arrow
is finite, the image of the right vertical arrow must have finite exponent.
∎

11.4.
Torsion in the fundamental groups

We shall now prove that the torsion subgroup of
the abelian étale fundamental group of a smooth scheme over a local field has
finite exponent. This will be a key step in proving Theorem
1.2
.
We first prove this in the projective case.

Lemma 11.11
.

The groups
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}
and
(\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{\mathbb{P}})_{\rm tor}
have finite
exponents.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7
, it suffices to prove the assertion for

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}
.
We first assume that
\overline{X}
is geometrically connected. In this case, the sequence

(11.11)

0\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{0}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\to G_{k}\to 0

is exact.
We also have an exact sequence

(11.12)

0\to k^{\times}\xrightarrow{\rho_{k}}G_{k}\to{\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\to 0

which shows that
(k^{\times})_{\rm tor}\cong(G_{k})_{\rm tor}
.
We can now apply
[
112
, Thm. 1.1]
and
[
79
, Prop. II.5.7]

to conclude the proof.

If
\overline{X}
is not geometrically connected, we have seen in the proof of
Lemma
11.10
that there exists a finite separable (in fact, Galois)
extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
such that each irreducible component of

\overline{X}\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
is geometrically connected. We choose one such
irreducible component
\overline{X}^{\prime}
and let
f\colon\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
be the
projection map. Then
f
is a finite étale morphism of
k
-schemes.
By Lemma
11.5
, the composite map

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{f_{*}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})
is multiplication by
\deg(f)
. This implies that the
composite map

(11.13)

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X}^{\prime})_{\rm tor}\xrightarrow{f_{*}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}

is also multiplication by
\deg(f)
. Since the middle term has finite exponent, it
follows that the same holds for
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}
.
∎

Lemma 11.12
.

The groups
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}({X})_{\rm tor}
and
(\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({X})_{\mathbb{P}})_{\rm tor}
have finite
exponents.

Proof.

Using Proposition
3.10
, it suffices to prove the
assertion for
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}({X})_{\rm tor}
. But this follows by combining
Theorem
11.6
and Lemma
11.11
plus the observation that
the torsion functor is left exact.
∎

Corollary 11.13
.

The groups
{\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})_{\rm tor}
and
{\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})_{\rm tor}

have finite exponents.

Proof.

We first argue for the degree zero part.
We let
F^{\prime}
be the image of
\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0}
and
F={\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})
so that
there is an exact sequence

(11.14)

0\to F^{\prime}\to\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}\to F\to 0.

Using Lemma
11.10
, we see that
F^{\prime}\otimes{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}=0
.
This implies that

0\to{F^{\prime}}_{\rm tor}\to(\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0})_{\rm tor}\to F_{\rm tor}\to 0

is exact. Hence, it suffices to show that
(\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0})_{\rm tor}
has finite
exponent. But this follows from Lemma
11.12
.

To prove the finite exponent property for
{\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})_{\rm tor}
, we let

F={\rm Image}(N_{X})
and
F^{\prime}={\rm Image}(\pi_{X})
. Since
\rho_{k}
is injective,
its cokernel is uniquely divisible and
{\rm Coker}(N_{X})
is finite, it follows that

(11.15)

0\to{\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})\to{\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})\to{F^{\prime}}/F\to 0

is exact and
({F^{\prime}}/F)_{\rm tor}
is finite. The desired assertion now follows from the
left exactness of the torsion functor and the
finite exponent property of
{\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})_{\rm tor}
we showed above.
∎

Remark 11.14
.

We shall show in §
12.5
that the finite exponent claims in
the results of §
11.3
and §
11.4
can be replaced
by a finiteness assertion, at least away from
{\rm char}(k)
.

11.5.
The kernel of the reciprocity

We continue with the setup of §
11.2
. We let
\rho^{m}_{X}\colon{C(X)}/m\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m
be the reciprocity homomorphism mod-
m
, where
m>0
is an
integer. We define
\rho^{{\rm t},m}_{X}
and
\rho^{m}_{\overline{X}}
similarly.

Lemma 11.15
.

There exists an integer
M\gg 0
such that
|{\rm Ker}(\rho^{m}_{X})|\leq M
for all

m\in I_{\mathbb{L}}
.

Proof.

For
\overline{X}
, the lemma follows from
[
27
, Thm. 5.4]
. Indeed,
note that none of the groups
C(\overline{X})\cong SK_{1}(\overline{X})
and
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})

depends on
k
. Hence, we can replace
k
by
k^{\prime}:=H^{0}(\overline{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{X}})
.
Observe that
k^{\prime}
is a finite separable field extension of
k
because

X
is integral and smooth over
k
, in particular, geometrically reduced.
Moreover, the structure map
X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
canonically factors through
{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})

and
\overline{X}
is geometrically connected over
k^{\prime}
(e.g., see
[
93
, Tag 0366]
). We
can therefore assume that
\overline{X}
is geometrically connected.
In this case, there is an exact sequence

{{\rm Ker}(\rho_{\overline{X}})}/m\to{\rm Ker}(\rho^{m}_{\overline{X}})\to\overline{{}_{m}{\rm Coker}(\rho_{\overline{X}})}\to 0

for every integer
m>0
, where the term on the right is a quotient of

{}_{m}{\rm Coker}(\rho_{\overline{X}})
.
The term on the left is uniformly finite as
m
runs through
I_{\mathbb{L}}

by
[
27
, Thm. 5.4]
and the
term on the right is uniformly finite over
I_{\mathbb{P}}
by
[
112
, Thm. 1.1]
.

We shall now prove the lemma for
X
.
By Propositions
8.15
and
9.12
, the lemma is equivalent
to the statement in motivic cohomology that the kernel of the étale realization map

\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))

is uniformly finite as
m
runs through
I_{\mathbb{L}}
. To prove this new assertion, we
consider the commutative diagram

(11.16)

\textstyle{H^{2d}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{Z}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The top row is exact by Lemma
8.4
and the bottom row is the well known
localization sequence in étale cohomology. The term on the right end of the
top row is zero by Proposition
8.2
and
[
66
, Thm. 5.1]
.
The term on the right end of the bottom row is zero because
cd_{k}(Z)\leq 2d
.
We have shown above (this also uses Propositions
8.15
and

9.12
) that
{\rm Ker}(\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}})
is uniformly finite
over
I_{\mathbb{L}}
. We now apply Corollary
10.11
to conclude the same for
X
.
∎

For any set of primes
{\mathbb{M}}
, we let
{{\rm Ker}}(\rho_{X})^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{M}}={\varprojlim}_{m\in I_{\mathbb{M}}}{\rm Ker}(\rho^{m}_{X})
.
It follows from Lemmas
11.3
and
11.15
that

(11.17)

|{{\rm Ker}}(\rho_{X})^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{L}}|\leq M\ \mbox{for \ some \ integer}\ M\gg 1.

Furthermore, the left
exactness of the inverse limit functor implies that there exists an exact sequence

(11.18)

0\to{{\rm Ker}}(\rho_{X})^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{L}}\to C(X)_{\mathbb{L}}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)_{\mathbb{L}}.

Lemma 11.16
.

The kernels of the
{\mathbb{L}}
-completion maps
C(X)\to C(X)_{\mathbb{L}}
and

C^{\rm t}(X)\to C^{\rm t}(X)_{\mathbb{L}}
are
{\mathbb{L}}
-divisible.

Proof.

We only need to show that the kernel of
C(X)\to C(X)_{\mathbb{L}}
is
{\mathbb{L}}
-divisible.
This is proven by using
\rho_{X}
and Lemma
11.1
as follows. We set
A=C(X)
,

B_{m}={\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m
and
\phi=\{\rho^{m}_{X}\}
with
m\in I_{\mathbb{L}}
.
Then we get
\widehat{B}={\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}_{\mathbb{L}}
in the notations of
Lemma
11.1
. It follows from Lemma
11.12
that

M\widehat{B}_{\rm tor}=0
for some integer
M\gg 0
. Using the left exactness of the
inverse limit functor and (
11.17
), we can choose
M
large enough
so that we also have
M({\rm Ker}(\widehat{\phi}))=0
.
Note that it follows from (
11.18
) that

M
necessarily lies in
I_{\mathbb{L}}
in this case. We now apply Lemma
11.1
to
conclude the proof.
∎

12.
End of proofs under bad reduction

In this section, we shall complete the proofs of all our main results except the case of good reduction.
We shall also prove refinements of
the results of §
11.3
and §
11.4
.
We continue with setup of §
11.2
.

12.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We shall begin by proving Theorem
1.2
.
The following result proves part of Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 12.1
.

{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})
is a direct sum of a finite group and an
{\mathbb{L}}
-divisible group.
The same holds also for
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
.

Proof.

We let
{\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})=p>0
. We first show the assertion for
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})
.
We look at the commutative diagram

(12.1)

\textstyle{0\to{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\to{{\rm Ker}}(\rho_{X})^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(X)_{\mathbb{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\rho_{X})_{\mathbb{L}}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)_{\mathbb{L}},}

where the vertical arrows are induced by the
{\mathbb{L}}
-completion. Note that
the left vertical arrow is the composition
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})\to{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})_{\mathbb{L}}\to{{\rm Ker}}(\rho_{X})^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{L}}
. It follows from Lemma
3.7
that
the right vertical arrow is surjective and its kernel is
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)_{p}
.
In particular, this kernel is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-module, and hence uniquely
{\mathbb{L}}
-divisible.

We let
D
and
T
denote the kernel and the image of the left vertical arrow,
respectively,
so that we have a short exact sequence

(12.2)

0\to D\to{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})\to T\to 0.

It follows from (
11.17
) and (
11.18
)
that
T
is a finite group of exponent lying in
I_{\mathbb{L}}
. By Lemma
11.2
,
it remains to show that
D
is
{\mathbb{L}}
-divisible.
Using Lemma
11.16
, it suffices to show that
the kernel of the right vertical arrow in (
12.1
) has no

{\mathbb{L}}
-torsion. But we have seen this in the previous paragraph.
The claim that
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
has the same property has an identical proof.
∎

12.2.
Theorem
1.2
for surfaces

Assume in Theorem
1.2
that
\text{\rm dim}(X)\leq 2
. We wish to prove the
following.

Lemma 12.2
.

One has
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})=F^{\prime}\oplus D
, where
F^{\prime}
is a finite group and
D
is
divisible by every integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
. The same holds also for

{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
.

Proof.

We shall give a proof for
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})
which also works word by word for

{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
. We can assume that
{\rm char}(k)=0
, else the lemma already
follows from Theorem
12.1
. We let
{\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})=p>0
.
Suppose that we can prove Lemma
11.15

by replacing
I_{\mathbb{L}}
with
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}=I_{{\mathbb{P}}}
.

It is a straightforward checking that
Lemma
11.16
now also holds if we replace
{\mathbb{L}}
by

{\mathbb{P}}
.
We now look at the diagram (
12.1
). The right vertical arrow is
now an isomorphism and the kernel of the middle vertical arrow is
{\mathbb{P}}
-divisible. It
follows that the kernel of the left vertical arrow is
{\mathbb{P}}
-divisible.
Now, we apply Lemma
11.2
and argue exactly as in the proof of
Theorem
12.1
to conclude that
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})
(which is the same as

{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X,0})
) has the desired form.
It remains therefore to prove the improved version of Lemma
11.15
, namely,
there exists an integer
M\gg 0
such that
|{\rm Ker}(\rho^{m}_{X})|\leq M
for all

m>0
.

To prove the above assertion, we shall argue as we did in the proof of
Lemma
11.15
and make necessary changes.
For
\overline{X}
, only change we need to make is to use
[
47
, Thm. 1.8]

instead of
[
27
, Thm. 5.4]
. No other change is required.
For
X
, we only need to make a change in the last line of the proof,
where we need to replace Corollary
10.11
by its improved version.
Hence, we are finally left with showing that

|H^{4}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))|\leq M
for all
m>0
. But this follows from
Proposition
10.12
.
∎

Remark 12.3
.

In view of Proposition
10.12
, the proof of Lemma
12.2
shows that
its assertion can extended in any higher dimension if
[
47
, Thm. 1.8]

is valid in that dimension.

12.3.
Theorem
1.2
for curves

Assume now in Theorem
1.2
that
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
. We wish to prove the following.

Lemma 12.4
.

{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
is divisible.

Proof.

Using the argument of Lemma
11.15
, we can assume that
X
is
geometrically connected.
Suppose first that
m
is an integer prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.
Then Corollary
10.3
says that the map

\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon H^{3}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))\to H^{3}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))
is
injective. Combining this with Proposition
9.12
,
we get that the map
\rho^{m}_{X}\colon{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m\to{\pi^{\rm ab,t}_{1}(X)}/m

is injective.
If
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
, then it follows from Corollary
7.7
and

[
54
, Prop. 3]
that the reciprocity map

{C^{\rm t}(X)}/{p^{n}}\to{\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/{p^{n}}

is injective for all
n\geq 1
.
Taking the limits, it follows that the map

(12.3)

\rho^{m}_{X}\colon{\varprojlim}_{m>0}{C^{\rm t}(X)}/m\to{\varprojlim}_{m>0}{\pi^{\rm ab,t}_{1}(X)}/m\cong\pi^{\rm ab,t}_{1}(X)

is injective in any characteristic.
In particular,
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})=\bigcap_{m>0}\ mC^{t}(X)
.

To finish the proof, we can now apply Lemma
11.1
with

{\mathbb{M}}=\{\mbox{all \ primes}\}
,

A=C^{\rm t}(X)
and
B_{m}={\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)}/m
(with
m>0
).
Lemma
11.12
and (
12.3
) ensure that the
conditions of Lemma
11.1
are met.
∎

We finally have:

Proof of Theorem
1.2
:

Combine Proposition
7.3
, Theorem
12.1
, Lemma
12.2
and
Lemma
12.4
.

\hfill\qed

12.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

We shall now prove Theorem
1.3
. We restate it here
for reader’s convenience. Recall that

E^{\rm t}(X)
and
J^{\rm t}(X)
are the kernels of the canonical surjections

C^{\rm t}(X)\twoheadrightarrow C(\overline{X})
and
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})
,
respectively.

Theorem 12.5
.

The map
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}\colon E^{\rm t}(X)_{\rm tor}\to J^{\rm t}(X)
is surjective.

Proof.

The proof of the theorem is easily reduced to the case when
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
by
Corollaries
5.12
,
7.5

and Theorem
11.8
.
We thus assume that
\text{\rm dim}(X)=1
.
We let
K=k(X)
and
K_{x}
the completion of
K
at a closed point
x
.

We recall at this point that the local reciprocity map

\rho_{K_{x}}\colon K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})\to G_{K_{x}}
maps
U^{\prime}_{i}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})
to the ramification
subgroup
G^{(i)}_{K_{x}}
for every
i\geq 0
by Proposition
7.1
.
Using Definition
5.1
and Lemma
5.6
,
we have a commutative diagram

(12.4)

\textstyle{{\underset{x\in Z}{\bigoplus}}\frac{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\rm t}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\rm t}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in Z}{\bigoplus}}\frac{G^{(0)}_{K_{x}}}{G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J^{\rm t}(X),}

where
Z=\overline{X}\setminus X
and
the left vertical arrow is the sum of local reciprocity maps for
K_{x}

as
x
runs through
Z
.
The horizontal arrows are surjective by Corollary
5.12
and
Theorem
11.8
. Since
\frac{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}\cong K^{M}_{2}(k(x))
, it suffices to
show that
\rho_{K_{x}}
induces a surjection

K^{M}_{2}(k(x))_{\rm tor}\twoheadrightarrow{G^{(0)}_{K_{x}}}/{G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}}
for every
x\in\overline{X}_{(0)}
.

To prove the above, we first note that the induced map

\rho^{\vee}_{K_{x}}\colon({G^{(0)}_{K_{x}}}/{G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}})^{\vee}\cong\frac{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{1}(K_{x})}{{\rm fil}_{0}H^{1}(K_{x})}\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(\frac{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})

is injective by (
2.3
) and Proposition
7.1
. Equivalently, the map

\frac{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}\to{G^{(0)}_{K_{x}}}/{G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}}
has dense image by
[
39
, Lemma 7.11]
.

On the other hand,
{G^{(0)}_{K_{x}}}/{G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}}
is finite by Lemma
3.12

and
\frac{U^{\prime}_{0}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}{U^{\prime}_{1}K^{M}_{2}(K_{x})}\cong K^{M}_{2}(k(x))
is a
direct sum of divisible and finite groups by
[
99
]
(see also

[
73
]
). This forces

K^{M}_{2}(k(x))_{\rm tor}\to{G^{(0)}_{K_{x}}}/{G^{(1)}_{K_{x}}}
to be surjective.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 12.6
.

The maps
C^{\rm t}(X)_{\rm tor}\to C(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}
and
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})_{\rm tor}\to{\rm Ker}(\rho_{\overline{X}})_{\rm tor}
are surjective.

Proof.

We have seen in the proof of Theorem
12.5
that
E^{\rm t}(X)
is a quotient of
a direct sum of divisible and finite groups. In particular,

E^{\rm t}(X)\otimes{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}=0
. This implies that
C^{\rm t}(X)_{\rm tor}\to C(\overline{X})_{\rm tor}
is surjective.
It follows from Theorem
12.5
that

(12.5)

0\to{\rm Ker}(E^{\rm t}(X)\to J^{\rm t}(X))\to{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})\to{\rm Ker}(\rho_{\overline{X}})\to 0

is exact. Since
E^{\rm t}(X)_{\rm tor}\to J^{\rm t}(X)
is surjective and

E^{\rm t}(X)\otimes{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}=0
, it follows that

({\rm Ker}(E^{\rm t}(X)\to J^{\rm t}(X)))\otimes{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}=0
.
We conclude from this that
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})_{\rm tor}\to{\rm Ker}(\rho_{\overline{X}})_{\rm tor}
is surjective.
∎

12.5.
Finiteness of the torsion subgroups of fundamental groups

In this subsection, we shall prove a refinement (see Theorem
12.8
)
of the results of §
11.3
and §
11.4
. We remark
though that this refinement already follows from
Theorem
11.6
when
X
is geometrically connected.
At the same time, we also remark that Theorem
12.8
was not earlier known
even for projective schemes over
k
in any dimension without the assumption of
geometrically connectedness.

The key lemma for proving Theorem
12.8
is the following.

Lemma 12.7
.

The groups
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)\{\ell\}
and
\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)\{\ell\}

are finite for every prime
\ell\neq{\rm char}(k)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.10
and Theorem
11.6
, it suffices to prove the
assertion of the lemma for
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(\overline{X})\{\ell\}
. We can therefore assume that

X
is projective over
k
. By Lemma
11.11
, it suffices
to show that
\ell^{n}
-torsion subgroup of
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
is finite for every

n\geq 1
. By Lemma
3.7
, we can replace
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)
by

\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({X})_{\ell}
.

We now compute

\begin{array}[]{lll}{}_{\ell^{n}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({X})_{\ell}&=&{\rm Hom}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}},\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({X})_{\ell})\\
&=&{\rm Hom}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}},{\rm Hom}(H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}),{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}))\\
&=&{\rm Hom}({H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})}/{\ell^{n}},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}),\end{array}

where all homomorphisms are in the category of abelian groups.
It suffices therefore to show that
{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})}/{\ell^{n}}
is finite.

The exact sequence

0\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}\xrightarrow{\ell^{n}}{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}\to 0

implies that
{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})}/{\ell^{n}}\hookrightarrow H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}})
.
We thus need to show that
H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}})
is finite.
But this is a special case of the general fact that

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(j))
is finite for any integers
i,j\geq 0
and
m\geq 1

such that
m\in k^{\times}
.
The latter result is well known. For instance, it is
an easy consequence of the Hochschild-Serre spectral
sequence using that
cd(k)=2
(e.g., see
[
46
, Rem. 3.5(c)]
).
∎

We can now prove the following. This also completes the proof of
Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 12.8
.

Let
p>0
denote the characteristic exponent of
k
.
Let
F
be any of the following groups:

(1)\ \pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}({X})_{\rm tor},\ (2)\ {\rm Image}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0}),\ (3)\ ({\rm Image}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X}))_{\rm tor},

\ (4)\ {\rm Coker}(\rho^{{\rm t}}_{X,0})_{\rm tor},\ \mbox{and}\ (5)\ {\rm Coker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})_{\rm tor}.

Then
F
has finite exponent and
F\{p^{\prime}\}
is finite.

Proof.

All cases except possibly (4) and (5) follow by combining
Lemma
12.7
with Lemmas
11.10

and
11.12
. We next note that (5) follows from (4)
by (
11.15
) and the latter follows from the case (1) because
we showed in the proof of Corollary
11.13
that

(\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}({X})_{0})_{\rm tor}\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Coker}(\rho^{{\rm t}}_{X,0})_{\rm tor}
.
∎

12.6.
Proof of Theorem
1.8

In this subsection, we shall prove Theorem
1.8
which we restate below for
reader’s convenience. We keep the notations of §
11.2
.
In particular, we let
X
be as in Theorem
1.8
with smooth compactification
\overline{X}
.
For
f\colon Y\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
,
we let
H^{i}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}}(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}(k,{\mathbb{Z}})}({\mathbb{Z}}_{\rm tr}(Y),{\mathbb{Z}}(j)[i])
and

H^{i}_{c}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}}(j))={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{DM}}(k,{\mathbb{Z}})}(f_{*}f^{!}{\mathbb{Z}}_{k},{\mathbb{Z}}(j)[i])
, where
{\mathbb{Z}}(1)={{\mathbb{Z}}_{\rm tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k})}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\rm tr}({\rm Spec\,}(k))}
. Note that the premotivic category

Y\mapsto{\mathbf{DM}}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})

is equipped with the six functors by
[
17
]
.

Theorem 12.9
.

We have the following.

(1)

There is a decomposition
H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d+2))\cong F\bigoplus D
, where

F
is a finite group of order lying in
I_{\mathbb{P}}
and
D
is
{\mathbb{P}}
-divisible.

(2)

For
n\geq 3
, the group
H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d+n))
is
{\mathbb{P}}
-divisible.

(3)

{\rm CH}^{d+n}(X,n)
is divisible if
n\geq 3
and
X
is projective over
k
.

Proof.

Using the inclusion

{H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d+2))}/m\hookrightarrow H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+2))
and Corollary
11.4
,
we see that (1) will follow if show that there exists an integer

M\gg 0
such that
|{H^{2d+2}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+2))}|\leq M
for all
m\in I_{\mathbb{P}}
.
But this follows from Corollary
10.11
and Proposition
10.12
.

To prove (2), note that
{H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}(d+n))}/m\hookrightarrow H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))
.
It is therefore enough to show that
H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))=0
if

({\rm char}(k),m)=1
. To check the latter statement,
we let
Z=\overline{X}\setminus X
. Noting that
{\mathbf{DM}}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{DM}}_{\rm cdh}(k,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)
, Lemma
8.4
says that there is an
exact sequence

{H^{2d+n-1}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))}\to{H^{2d+n}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))}\to{H^{2d+n}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))},

By Lemma
10.2
, the realization map

H^{2d+n-1}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))\to H^{2d+n-1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))
is an
isomorphism. But the latter group is zero because
cd_{k}(Z)\leq 2d
.
This implies that the term on the left in the above exact sequence is zero.
It suffices therefore to show that

{H^{2d+n}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+n))}=0
. But this is a direct consequence of
Lemma
10.2
and the fact that
cd_{k}(\overline{X})\leq 2d+2
.

We now prove (3). By
[
2
, Thm. 1.1]
, we have a canonical isomorphism

{\rm CH}^{d+n}(X,n)\cong H^{d}(X_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{d+n,X})
. On the other hand,
the Gersten resolution for Milnor
K
-theory due to Kerz
[
57
]

implies that there is a surjection
{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{n}(k(x))\twoheadrightarrow H^{d}(X_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{d+n,X})
. We are now done because
each
K^{M}_{n}(k(x))
is a divisible group for
n\geq 3
by

[
26
, Thm. IX.4.11]
. This concludes the proof.
∎

13.
The case of good reduction

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.5
. We shall work
under the following setup throughout this section.
We let
k
be a local field with the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
and the residue field

{\mathfrak{f}}
. We let
\overline{X}
be a smooth projective scheme over
k
of pure dimension
d

and let
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be an open immersion with dense image.
We let
K=k(X)
. We fix an integer
m
prime to
{\rm char}(k)
.
Recall that
\overline{X}
is said to admit a good reduction if
there exists a smooth projective morphism
f\colon\overline{{\mathcal{X}}}\to{\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})

whose generic fiber is
\overline{X}
. We let
\overline{X}_{s}
denote the special fiber of
f
.
We begin with some preparations towards the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

13.1.
A vanishing theorem for surfaces

We let
{\mathcal{H}}^{j}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))
be the sheaf on the big Zariski site of
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}

associated to the presheaf
U\mapsto H^{j}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))
.
Similarly, we let
{\mathcal{H}}^{j}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))
be the sheaf on the big Zariski site of

{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
associated to the presheaf
U\mapsto H^{j}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))
.

Lemma 13.1
.

If
d=2
and
\overline{X}
admits a good reduction, then

H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))=0
.

Proof.

Using the exact sequence (e.g., see
[
19
, Cor. 2.2.2]
)

(13.1)

0\to H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\to H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))\xrightarrow{\partial_{\overline{X}}}{\underset{x\in\overline{X}^{(1)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k(x),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2)),

the lemma is equivalent to the assertion that
{\rm Ker}(\partial_{\overline{X}})=0
.

We now recall the Kato complex
K(r,s)
(this exists even if
d\neq 2
):

(13.2)

H^{r+d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(s+d))\xrightarrow{\partial_{\overline{X}}}\cdots\to{\underset{x\in\overline{X}^{(d-1)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{r+1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k(x),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(s+1))\to{\underset{x\in\overline{X}^{(d)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{r}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k(x),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(s)),

where
r\geq 1
and
s\geq 0
(e.g., see
[
47
, § 1]
). The differentials are
the residue maps.
The Kato homology
H^{r,s}_{i}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)
is the
i
-th homology of
K(r,s)
.
Hence, we have reduced the problem to showing that
H^{2,1}_{2}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)=0
.
For this, it suffices to show that
H^{2,1}_{2}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}})=0
for every prime

\ell\neq{\rm char}(k)
and
n\geq 1
.

Since
H^{2,1}_{3}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})=0
as
d\leq 2
, the exact sequence
(e.g., see
[
47
, Lem. 7.3]
)

(13.3)

0\to{H^{2,1}_{3}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})}/{\ell^{n}}\to H^{2,1}_{2}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\ell^{n}})\to{}_{\ell^{n}}H^{2,1}_{2}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})\to 0

reduces us to showing that
H^{2,1}_{2}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})=0
.
To this end, we note that the residue map

H^{2,1}_{2}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})\to H^{1,0}_{2}(\overline{X}_{s},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}})
is an
isomorphism (even if
\ell={\rm char}({\mathfrak{f}})
)
by
[
47
, Thm. 1.6]
. But the latter group is zero by

[
47
, Thm. 1.4]
because
\overline{X}_{s}
is smooth over
{\mathfrak{f}}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

13.2.
A surjectivity of étale realization

We next prove the following general result.

Lemma 13.2
.

Assume that
\overline{X}
admits a good reduction. Then the étale realization map

\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon H^{2d}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))

is surjective.

Proof.

We shall prove the lemma by induction on
d
. The case
d\leq 1
follows directly
from Corollary
10.3
. To prove
d=2
case, we consider the commutative
diagram of the Zariski descent spectral sequences

(13.4)

\textstyle{E^{i,j}=H^{i}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{j}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{H^{i+j}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{E^{i,j}_{\text{\'{e}t}}=H^{i}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{j}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(n))}
\textstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{H^{i+j}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(n)).}

The above diagram of spectral sequences gives rise to the following
two commutative diagrams of exact sequences.

(13.5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{4}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{4}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3)).}

(13.6)

\textstyle{H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{3}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{2}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{3}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{3}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

In the above two diagrams, the vertical arrows are the étale realization maps.

It follows from Lemma
10.1
that all vertical arrows (except possibly the third vertical arrow from the left) in (
13.6
) are isomorphisms. It follows that the left vertical arrow in
(
13.5
) is an isomorphism.
We are therefore left with the task of showing that

H^{0}(\overline{X}_{\rm zar},{\mathcal{H}}^{4}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)(3))=0
. But this follows from
Lemma
13.1
. We have thus proven the lemma when
d=2
.

We now assume that
d>2
and that the assertion of the lemma
holds in dimensions smaller than
d
.
By
[
32
, Lem. 9.1, Thm. 9.6]

(see the proof of Thm. 9.6 of op. cit.)
we can find a very ample smooth
divisor
\overline{Y}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
such that
\overline{Y}
also admits a good reduction.
Let
\iota\colon\overline{Y}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
be the inclusion.
By Corollary
9.5
, we have a commutative diagram

(13.7)

\textstyle{H^{2d-2}(\overline{Y},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{Y}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{Y},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1)).}

Since
\overline{X}
is of pure dimension
d
and
\overline{Y}
is a very ample divisor on

\overline{X}
, it follows that
\overline{Y}
is of pure dimension
d-1
.
In particular, the left vertical arrow in (
13.7
) is
surjective by induction. To conclude the proof, it remains to show that
the bottom horizontal arrow in (
13.7
) is surjective.

To prove the above, we recall that the map
\iota_{*}
is the composition

H^{2d-2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{Y},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d))\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2d}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},\overline{Y}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1)),

where the first arrow is Gabber’s purity isomorphism and the second is the forget
support map. Hence, we are reduced to showing that the second arrow
is surjective. To this end, we let
U=\overline{X}\setminus\overline{Y}
and
look at the exact localization sequence

H^{2d}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},\overline{Y}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1)).

Since
\overline{Y}
is a very ample divisor of the projective
k
-scheme
\overline{X}
, it
follows that
U
is an affine
k
-scheme of dimension
d
.
In particular,
cd(U)\leq d+2
. Since
d\geq 3
, it follows that

H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))=0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

13.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.5

We now prove Theorem
1.5
. We state it here for reader’s convenience.
A special case of this, when
{\rm char}(k)=0
and
\overline{X}
is a geometrically connected
rational surface over
k
, was earlier shown by Yamazaki
[
111
, Thm. 6.5]

Theorem 13.3
.

Let
k
be a local field and
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
an open
immersion in
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
with dense image such that
\overline{X}
is
projective over
k
. Assume that
{X}
is birational to a smooth projective
k
-scheme
which admits a good reduction. Then

\rho_{X}\colon{C(X)}/m\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)}/m

is an isomorphism for every integer
m\in k^{\times}
.
In particular,
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})
is divisible away from
{\rm char}(k)
.
The same also holds for
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.11
, Lemma
5.8
and
Proposition
7.3
, the assertions of the
theorem for
\rho_{X}
and
\rho^{\rm t}_{X}
are equivalent.
By Proposition
8.15
, Theorem
9.9
and Proposition
9.12
,
the first part of the theorem is equivalent to the statement that the
étale realization map

(13.8)

\epsilon^{*}_{X}\colon H^{2d+1}_{c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))\to H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))

is an isomorphism.

By our assumption, there are dense open immersions

\overline{X}^{\prime}\hookleftarrow X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
, where
\overline{X}^{\prime}
is a smooth connected
projective
k
-scheme which admits a good reduction.
We let
Z^{\prime}=\overline{X}\setminus X^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}=\overline{X}^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}
with their
reduced subscheme structures.
In the argument below, we shall write
H^{i}_{c}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(d+1))
simply
as
H^{i}_{c}(Y)
for
Y\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
.
We shall follow the same convention for the étale cohomology
(with and without compact support).

We now consider the commutative diagram with exact rows (see Lemma
8.4
and
(
11.16
))

(13.9)

\textstyle{H^{2d-1}(W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{c}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}(\overline{X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}(W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}(\overline{X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{2d-1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The arrows
\epsilon^{*}_{W^{\prime}}
are isomorphisms by Corollary
10.3
.
Since
\overline{X}^{\prime}
admits a good reduction, it follows from Lemma
13.2
that
the third vertical arrow from the left in (
13.9
) is surjective.
For the same reason, the vertical arrow on the extreme right is an isomorphism
by
[
48
, Thm. 4.1]
in view of Proposition
9.12
.
An easy diagram chase shows that the second vertical
arrow from the left is surjective and the second vertical arrow from the right is
an isomorphism. If we now replace
\overline{X}^{\prime}
(resp.
W^{\prime}
) by
\overline{X}

(resp.
Z^{\prime}
) in the above diagram and repeat the argument, we conclude that the map

(13.10)

\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}\colon H^{i}(\overline{X})\to H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X})

is surjective for
i=2d
and isomorphism for
i=2d+1
.

We let
Z=\overline{X}\setminus X
with the reduced subscheme structure
and look at the commutative diagram of exact sequences

(13.11)

\textstyle{H^{2d}(\overline{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{Z}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{c}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}(\overline{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2d+1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\overline{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We showed above that
\epsilon^{*}_{\overline{X}}
is surjective on the left end and
an isomorphism on the right end. The arrow
\epsilon^{*}_{Z}
is an isomorphism by
Corollary
10.3
. An easy diagram chase shows that
\epsilon^{*}_{X}
is
an isomorphism.

To show that
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X})
is divisible away from
{\rm char}(k)
, we
simply repeat the argument for proving Theorem
12.1
with

{\mathbb{L}}
replaced by
{\mathbb{P}}
and note that
T=0
in the present case by
what we have shown above. The same argument also applies to
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X})
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 13.4
.

Let the assumptions be as in Theorem
13.3
and assume furthermore
that
X
is geometrically connected. Then there is an
exact sequence

0\to{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})\to C^{\rm t}(X)_{0}\xrightarrow{\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0}}\pi^{\rm ab,{\rm t}}_{1}(X)_{0}\to F\to 0,

where
{\rm Ker}(\rho^{\rm t}_{X,0})
is divisible away from
{\rm char}(k)
and
F
is
zero (resp. a finite
p
-group) if
{\rm char}(k)=0
(resp.
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
).

Proof.

Recall that we have an exact sequence
0\to k^{\times}\to G_{k}\to\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to 0
. Using this, the corollary is a direct consequence of
Theorems
1.1
,
1.4
and
13.3
if we know that the norm map
C^{t}(X)\to k^{\times}
is surjective. Using the technique of the proof of Theorem
13.3
and the surjection
C^{t}(X)\twoheadrightarrow C(\overline{X})
, we are reduced to show that the norm map
C(\overline{X})\to k^{\times}
is surjective when
\overline{X}
admits a good reduction. But this follows from
[
54
, Lemma 8]
.
∎

14.
Reciprocity for rational curves

In this section, we shall study the reciprocity map for the class group of
rational curves and provide an evidence for Conjecture
1.7
.
Our setup is the following. We let
k
be a local field of characteristic zero and let

j\colon X\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}
be the inclusion of a rational curve. Since every open
subset of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}
contains a
k
-rational point, we can assume
up to an automorphism of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}
that
X\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}
.
We let
S={\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}\setminus X
and
S_{0}=S\setminus\{\infty\}
.
Unless the underlying topology is specified,
all sheaf cohomology groups below will be considered with respect to the
Zariski topology.

By Corollary
6.6
, we have an exact sequence

(14.1)

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}})\to K_{2}(S)\to C^{\rm t}(X)\to C({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k})\to 0.

The composition

K_{2}(k)\cong H^{0}({\rm Spec\,}(k),{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\rm Spec\,}(k)})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}})\cong K_{2}({\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k})

is an isomorphism by the homotopy invariance of
K
-theory. Furthermore, the map

H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k}})
is injective,
as can be seen using the Gersten resolution for Milnor
K_{2}
. Since
the composite map
K_{2}(k)\to H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}})\to K_{2}(S)\to K_{2}(k(\infty))
is an isomorphism, it follows that the map
K_{2}(k)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{2,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}})
is an isomorphism. In particular, we get an exact sequence

(14.2)

0\to\frac{K_{2}(S)}{K_{2}(k)}\to C^{\rm t}(X)\to C({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k})\to 0,

where the first term is the quotient of the pull-back map
K_{2}(k)\to K_{2}(S)
.
We also have the exact sequence of étale cohomology groups

(14.3)

0\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(S,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1))\to H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}).

We look at the commutative diagram of exact sequences

(14.4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k(\infty),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}),}

where
F(S)
is defined so that the bottom sequence is exact.

Since
X
contains
k
-rational points, it follows from the homotopy invariance of
étale cohomology that the right vertical arrow is injective.
In particular, the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism.
We thus get an exact sequence

(14.5)

0\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(S_{0},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1))\to 0.

Comparing (
14.2
) and (
14.5
), we get a commutative
diagram of exact sequences

(14.6)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{2}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\underset{x\in S}{\bigoplus}}K^{M}_{2}(k(x))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\rm t}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu_{\infty}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\underset{x\in S}{\bigoplus}}\mu_{\infty}(k(x))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J^{\rm t}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

By the main result of
[
73
]
, the left and the middle vertical arrows are the
Hilbert symbol maps which are surjective with uniquely divisible kernels.
From this, we conclude the following result. This provides an evidence of
Conjecture
1.7
. It also shows that the kernel of the surjection

\rho^{\rm t}_{X}\colon E^{\rm t}(X)\to J^{\rm t}(X)
may be arbitrarily large.

Theorem 14.1
.

Let
k
be a local field of characteristic zero and let

j\colon X\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{k}
be the inclusion of a rational curve.
Then there exists a split short exact sequence

0\to D\to E^{\rm t}(X)\to J^{\rm t}(X)\to 0,

where
D
is a uniquely divisible group. It is uncountable iff
X\not\cong{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k}
.
